#147. Israeli tanks cross ‘yellow line’ boundary into Hamas-controlled central Gaza

First incursion across boundary since ceasefire deal comes as Eisenkot seeks to form government without Netanyahu

The Israeli military crossed into Hamas-controlled central Gaza on Wednesday in its first ground operation across the “yellow line” boundary since a fragile ceasefire was agreed last October, forcing dozens of displaced Palestinians to flee their tents.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The operation lasted about 24 hours and on Thursday the military said it had been “completed”.

The IDF ​has not commented extensively on the operation, which came as the Israeli opposition politician, Gadi Eisenkot, has been seeking to form a government without the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, who has built his political reputation on promises to keep Israel safe.

A Gaza security source told AFP there was “intense Israeli artillery shelling” north of Nuseirat refugee camp and reported Israeli military vehicles in central Gaza “amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes since midday”.

Hamas condemned the Israeli raid and the forced displacement of Palestinians as “a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement” and a dangerous escalation. The Israeli military did not immediately respond to Hamas’s statement.

Residents reported hearing tank fire in the area. Palestinian medics said one person was killed in an Israeli airstrike near the site of the raid.

After the statement from the IDF that it had concluded the tank raid to dismantle a suspected Hamas tunnel, the defence ministry’s body in charge of civilian affairs in Palestinian territories, Cogat, said Palestinians would again be free to move in the targeted areas near al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps.

In separate incidents on Wednesday, Palestinian medics said two people were killed by Israeli fire, one in Deir al-Balah and the other in Gaza City. The Israeli military said it was unaware of any incident in Deir al-Balah and had struck a militant in Gaza City.

Israel has stepped up its attacks on Hamas, which it says has been regrouping and rearming. Hamas has accused Israel of trying to derail the US-brokered ceasefire agreed in October 2025.

Israeli strikes have continued since the ceasefire, and Hamas remains armed.

Last week, an Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The body overseeing Donald Trump’s plan to end the war in Gaza has accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities, leaving Palestinians in dire conditions and the ceasefire at risk of collapse.

In its second report to the UN security council since the truce began 13 months ago, the Trump-led Board of Peace said the gap between what was agreed and what was happening on the ground remained the biggest threat to the deal. The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza since Israel launched its military campaign in response to the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed.

In June, an ⁠independent UN inquiry determined that Israel was continuing to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children. A previous report by the commission in September 2025 found that Israel had committed genocide in Gaza, and Israeli officials, including the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, incited those acts. Netanyahu is separately wanted by the international criminal court (ICC) for war crimes.

Reuters and AFP contributed to this report

#145. They were fined $811m for conning detained migrants, but the scams didn't stop

Mildred Juarez was stuck in detention for a month after her friends sent her bail money to Libre Immigration Services

Every morning for a month, Mildred Juarez woke up at five to be the first person in the showers at the immigration centre in Texas where she was being detained. Her friends had paid her bail, and she wanted to look presentable when she was finally released.

But every day, she was told her bond had not been paid.

Juarez was confused. Her friends had wired $2,600 (Â£1,970) to a bond company, Libre Immigration Services (LIS), that had been recommended by her lawyer. LIS said it would begin "the process of paying her immigration bond". But the next day came, and the day after that, with no release.

What she didn't know was that LIS was owned by a convicted fraudster, who had acquired the assets of another bail company co-founded by two men who also had fraud convictions. These two men were fined $811m in 2024 for scamming their clients.

Their firm, Libre By Nexus, which was at one point the largest immigration bail company in America, had appeared to shut down after federal regulators caught up with them.

But public records and BBC interviews with clients and former employees reveal a tangle of interconnected operations continued - along with the flow of cash - while leaving immigrants stuck in detention centres.

Since President Donald Trump has returned to office after promising an immigration crackdown, Immigrations and Customs Enforcement (ICE) has made over 1.2m arrests, and fast-tracked deportations. The administration has tried unsuccessfully to make it illegal to give migrants bail but the sharp increase in detentions has increased demand for bond companies to secure bail and their release.

Conditions in these centres can be poor. Held in custody, away from family, often scared to speak out, many have faced health problems and endured inadequate food or treatment, according to immigrant advocates and reports by the US government.

"He did everything right" - The impact of Trump's immigration crackdown in Ohio

In the detention centre where Juarez was locked up, she and about 100 other detainees slept in bunks laid out in rows in a cacophonous room. The lights were left on all night and guards routinely woke everyone up for roll call. According to Castro and advocacy groups, detainees who caused trouble were put in "the hold" - solitary confinement.

The White House and officials from Homeland Security did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.

For Juarez, the wait to be bailed out of detention seemed interminable.

In an email to the BBC, a spokesperson for LIS said Juarez "misunderstands" their services - that they are not bondsmen themselves, but they connect detained individuals to those who are licensed bondsmen. They said another company ultimately decided not to work with her.

An email sent by LIS, and reviewed by the BBC, said that the company had paid a cash bond to ICE on behalf of Juarez. The message was forwarded to Juarez as proof of payment a few days before her release.

But it was another bail agent, who was not affiliated with LIS, that got her out of detention - after her friends raised even more money to hire another company.

"I was losing it inside there," Juarez recalled, using an expletive.

"I lost everything. When I was released I had nightmares I was back inside."

Libre Immigration Services never paid back her deposit, despite sending messages promising a refund. A spokesperson told the BBC that they would refund Juarez's bail amount once her friend verified their details.

It is notoriously difficult for migrants like Juarez to make bail, which can be upwards of $7,000.

Once charged with illegal entry, a judge may grant bail, which allows the migrant to live freely while their case is heard, a process which can take years. Once a person's court case is completed, the bond amount is returned.

Bail agents, who are licensed to provide a down payment on bail and vouch that if the accused does not show up for court they will pay the rest, can help people get out of detention sooner. But many won't help migrants because they view them as too much of a flight risk. A 2024 government report found that a third of migrants not already in custody did not show up for court.

Libre by Nexus, also known as Nexus, was willing to take that risk.

Founded in 2013, its co-founders Michael Donovan and Richard Moore had served prison time in the 1990s and early 2000s for a string of fraud and forgery convictions.

Through his attorney, Donovan declined to comment. Moore did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.

Although people with felony convictions are barred from holding a bond licence in many states, Nexus operated as a kind of middleman between immigrant clients and licensed bondsmen.

Holed up in detention in Louisiana after crossing the border, Rosa Murillo heard about Libre by Nexus from another inmate, and the 18-year-old Honduran's mother called them to arrange her $7,000 bail.

A Nexus employee picked her up and took her to a hotel room where she was told she would have to pay $420 a month and wear a GPS ankle monitor until her case was completed.

Rosa Murillo says she was ashamed to wear the ankle bracelet that Nexus required

Federal regulators accused Nexus of implying, in their contracts, that the GPS bracelets were court-mandated, when they were actually the private company's requirement.

Murillo got a job as a waitress and tried to hide the ankle bracelet for fear that people might think she was a violent criminal.

Over the course of a decade, Libre by Nexus enabled more than 20,000 migrants like Murillo to be released on bail - and took in more than $230m in revenue, court documents show.

But the ankle bracelets were a constant source of annoyance for Nexus clients released from detention, according to two former employees who spoke to the BBC.

A former Nexus co-owner, Evan Ajin, told the BBC that the company stopped requiring released detainees to wear ankle bracelets in 2018.

When Mark Supetran worked for Libre by Nexus, he says he would tell people about how they had to wear an ankle bracelet

But Mark Supetran, who was based in the Philippines and worked for the company for a few months in 2022, says the bracelets were still in use when he worked for the company.

Supetran said he would field 80 calls a day from people who were either going through the process themselves or were the loved one of someone who was. Telling them they would have to wear a tag on release was part of the conversation.

"Part of the script was to say something like: 'The client will be wearing an ankle monitor.' So that got me thinking, like, why?" he recalled. "It doesn't make sense, we're not the government."

Not getting refunds of bail deposits was another complaint. Another former employee told the BBC he was instructed to tell callers that they would get their money back once their case was completed.

"But that that was never the case," he told the BBC. "I mean, you had people calling me in almost every day like 'Hey, am I gonna get my money back? Am I gonna get my money back?'"

Ajin, the former Nexus co-owner, told the BBC that the contract clearly stated that monthly fees did not go towards their bond, but were programme fees, and so they could issue no refund.

The US Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB), an agency created to stop predatory lending and other abusive financial practices, successfully sued Libre by Nexus and its parent company.

The lawsuit accused the firm of running a "predatory immigrant-services scam" that trapped victims into paying high, long-term fees. Nexus also misrepresented and concealed the terms, the lawsuit alleged, such as the non-refundable, monthly payments.

The judgment sided with the CFPB and a federal judge found the company liable for using predatory tactics against its clients in 2024, fining Nexus, its parent company, Ajin, Donovan and Moore $811m.

In addition to the hefty fine, the judge forbade the company from collecting on past debts.

Days after the 2024 order, Donovan sold a number of the company's assets, including customer lists and records - for $3.50.

As part of the agreement the buyer would also take on its debt.

Ajin says he ceased contact with everyone involved following the sale.

Moore is now in prison, after being convicted of filing fraudulent Nexus taxes. He and Donovan, who is also behind bars, are awaiting sentencing for insurance fraud and conspiracy. Donovan has also been charged with sexually abusing a boy under 12, but has not entered a plea in that case.

The purchaser of Nexus's assets was a Pennsylvania businessman named Vincent Smith, who had been sentenced to 10 months' probation in 2015 for conspiring with employees to write over $2m in fake bail bonds.

Smith called the new business Libre Immigration Services.

This March, the judge who issued the fine also ruled the sale of the company had violated her order by transferring the debts.

Judge Elizabeth Dillon voided the sale, but did not shut Libre Immigration Services down. She is due to review claims that LIS also violated the order by trying to build an app to collect debts from Nexus's old clients.

A former paralegal at Nexus, who went on to work for LIS, told the BBC about the app. She said after the sale, the lines between the two companies were blurred.

Another employee confirmed her account independently to the BBC, noting that the app was never finished because they could not obtain the client data.

A hearing date has not been set, and LIS has denied any wrongdoing, noting the company was never in possession of the client lists.

A screenshot of Libre Immigration Service's website

Libre Immigration Services continues to solicit immigrant clients and advertises its services online and on social media.

Its website features glossy photos of beaming families alongside the promise to "get your loved one released from a detention centre today!".

That wasn't Janie Aviles's experience. She had wired Libre Immigration Services $10,000 to help get her friend out of detention in Texas. But LIS did not pay his bail, Aviles said, until a month later, leaving him, like Juarez, in limbo.

When she called they offered various explanations and then stopped answering the telephone, she said.

Eventually she drove to the office listed on the website, which had shown a picture of LIS's logo hanging above the door. But on arrival, she found there was no storefront with the business's name and logo anywhere.

She said the company finally paid the bail a month after getting the funds, after she had reported them to the district attorney. No charges have been laid.

An LIS spokesperson said that "verification of significant information had to occur to prevent or minimise loss".

"The application was ultimately approved, and the cash bond was pledged to ICE. Presumably any delay related to the substantial bond amount and the heightened requirements of review."

They also said the office at that location was for business operations, and not client services, and that a sign had been ordered and would be installed, pending permits.

But the ordeal was enough to anger Aviles, who feared for her friend while he was stuck in detention. "I'm like 'This ain't right.'"

Haitian migrants changed this US town - Trump's deportation drive is reshaping it again

Immigrants held in small outdoor cages at 'Alligator Alcatraz' in Florida, investigators find

#144. Trump Flaunts Camaraderie With Xi and Putin but Has Little to Show for It

President Trump called Russia’s leader his “friend” and marveled at China’s warm welcome for his first-ever trilateral summit. But there were no breakthroughs on nuclear control, the war in Ukraine or A.I.

#143. COP31: Climate deal 'not adopted,' summit president says

The summit was extended as nations worked to reach a deal, but they couldn't agree on including fossil fuels. European nations, small island states and activists have criticized oil-producing countries for the impasse.

Delegates from nearly 200 countries failed to reach an agreement despite prolonging the talks

The summit's president acknowledges that a deal was 'not adopted'

The final draft presented at the summit in Antalya, Turkey omitted any mention of fossil fuels

The EU says it cannot accept a deal that 'greenwashes fossil fuels'

Oil and gas consumption remains a major flashpoint amid soaring energy prices and tensions over the US-Israeli war with Iran

UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres said he was 'deeply disappointed' by the outcome

These are the major developments from the closing phase of the UN's COP31 Climate Summit in Antalya, Turkey, on Friday, November 20 through Sunday, November 22:

People from all over the world are suffering due to high prices of fossil fuels, Germany's Environment Minister Carsten Schneider said after the COP31 breakdown. He added that clean technologies such as electric vehicles, heat pumps and modernizing energy industries would free the people from this dependence.

"I understand that this poses a threat to the business model of those countries that sell us oil and gas. But electrification will make the world a more peaceful and stable place," he said.

Previously, Schneider accused the oil and gas industry of dragging its feet on climate change, referencing the last-minute draft proposals which steered clear of fossil fuel phaseout.

"Some oil-producing nations realize that the situation is becoming serious for them — that is why they are blocking it so vehemently," Scheider said from Turkey.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Separately, the head of the German Environment Agency Dirk Messner said the world was divided in two camps, with some countries focusing on averting climate change and others defending oil, coal, and natural gas.

"A fossil-fuel-based economy can quickly become obsolete if the majority of countries stand together," Messner said.

He urged the nations protecting the climate to continue boosting cooperation. At the same time, Messner warned that the world would not become climate-neutral by the middle of the century without nations like Russia, Saudi Arabia and the US all supporting this goal.

"This is why the global climate dialogue must continue," he said.

The EU's outright rejection to provide new finance commitments "set the tone" for the failed COP 31 climate summit, says Tasneem Essop, executive director at the Climate Action Network, a group over 2,500 NGOs from various parts of the world.

Talking to DW from Turkey, Essop questioned the EU's narrative of rejecting the proposed agreement for not mentioning fossil fuels.

Essop said that the EU was pushing back and obstructing discussions dealing with the issues of the Global South.

"Rich nations are responsible for providing public finance to developing countries so that they can take climate action, so that they can transition away from fossil fuels," Essop said.

"That requires financial support. And yet they came here without finance. So, it's left a bitter taste in many mouths about the action of rejection by the EU."

Essop also pointed to the UK, Australia, and European countries granting additional licenses to obtain fossil fuels.

"You can't say you're rejecting something because of fossil fuels and yet in your practice and your actions back home, you're doing exactly the opposite," she said.

She described the outcome of the summit, which went into overtime and then ended without an agreement, as "deeply disappointing."

While oil producers like Saudi Arabia and Russia were criticized for blocking any mention fossil fuels in the draft COP31 declaration, one climate campaigner told DW that other wealthy countries also failed more vulnerable nations by not agreeing to a funded pathway for an energy transition.

Harjit Sing, strategic advisor to the Fossil Fuel Non-Proliferation Treaty Initiative said developing nations had big ambitions to turn away from oil and gas, but needed resources to do so.

"If you look at Canada, the UK, Norway, not to mention the US, they continue to expand fossil fuels, yet they point fingers towards developing countries who have a large population," Sing told DW.

"But the reality is that [the developed countries] were the ones who were blocking the conversation and the progress that we needed to create a just transition mechanism."

Sing said this lack of a funded plan for developing countries was more urgent than whether or not the final declaration mentioned fossil fuels.

"So not mentioning fossil fuels is just one part of it. The more important thing is we now need a plan, not repackaged national roadmaps, but a fair, fast and funded transition away from fossil fuels," he said.

"Fair: So rich countries move fast, [but] they are the ones who are historically responsible for the climate crisis. Fast, which is in line with science. And funded, so that developing countries get the right kind of support to make that shift."

A combination of climate change effects and insufficient funding would be "a life or death scenario" for developing countries, Sing added.

"Even at one degree temperature rise, they were facing so many disasters and we are already going to breach that 1.5 degrees Celsius of warming," he said.

After the failure of the COP31 summit over fossil fuels, Russia's delegate Sergey Kononuchenko argued that reducing the supply of oil without reducing the demand will cause "deficit, price shock and a switch to dirtier types of fuel" as well as "energy poverty," which will affect poorer countries first.

"For that reason, we cannot agree to any formulation on fossil fuel," he said.

Meanwhile, Saudi Arabia's representative referred to a last-minute proposal that called for expanding carbon capture capacities.

"We believe that climate change requires all solutions on the table," said Mohammad Ayoub, speaking for the Arab Group of nations at the summit.

"It makes no sense that solutions, concrete solutions... will be taken off the table."

While capturing carbon dioxide from the atmosphere is technically possible, critics say the technology is still in its infancy and lacks real-world infrastructure to move the needle on climate change.

Both Russia and Saudi Arabia had sought to omit any mention of fossil fuels in the final declaration, contributing the the impasse on Sunday.

UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres said he was "deeply disappointed" by the collapse of negotiations for a joint resolution at COP31.

"I share the very profound frustration of all those who expected governments to rise to the moment the climate crisis demands," Guterres said in a statement.

"There may be no consensus in the negotiating room. There is no ambiguity in the science," he added.

"Those who profit from the destruction of our planet must never dictate the terms of the fight to save it," the UN chief said. "The continued addiction to coal, oil and gas is fueling climate chaos, exposing economies to price shocks, and undermining energy security," Guterres added.

"Major emitters must lead. Developed countries must deliver on finance. And every country must do their part."

Marshall Islands climate envoy Kathy Jetnil-Kijiner spoke out against fossil fuel interests after the conclusion of the summit with no deal.

The Marshall Islands are a chain of volcanic atolls and coral islands numbering over 1,100 islands in total in the Pacific Ocean. Like many island nations, they are severly threatened by the effects of climate change on the earth's oceans.

"We have been let down ​on all sides. We were presented with the worst possible response to overshoot, a ⁠response to enhance oil and gas. We will not be forced to accept false solutions," Jetnil-Kijiner said.

"We will not compromise our future to benefit fossil fuel interests. We will not accept an outcome that undermines our security and threatens our future."

Australian environment minister and COP31's president of negotiations, Chris Bowen, expressed disappointment that the sweeping deal he tried to broker ultimately collapsed.

As part of a compromise between the two countries, Turkey hosted the summit while Australia took a leading role over negotiations.

"Unfortunately, we were unable to reach agreement on a cover decision... And that's disappointing. Very disappointing," said Bowen.

"An obvious issue is the transition away from fossil fuels, where some parties insisted that must be mentioned, other parties insisted that if it was mentioned they would veto."

Nevertheless, Bowen highlighted some of the ways in which the summit has still had a positive impact.

"A lot of good things happened this COP. A lot of events which told the clean energy story. A lot of commitments made — like the more than 90 countries that signed the electrification pledge, for example. And all that is good work and a great thing," he said.

The COP31 climate summit has failed to agree on a final joint declaration.

During a plenary session, the summit's Turkish president acknowledged there would be no climate deal.

"I understand that there is no agreement on this text. For that reason, these decisions have not been adopted," Murat Kurum, who is also Turkey's environment minister, told a closing plenary in the Mediterranean city of Antalya.

The rift centered on whether to include the phaseout of fossil fuels in the final declaration. Scientists say the burning of fossil fuels such as coal, oil and gas is the main cause of global warming.

European nations, smaller island states and climate activists have criticized oil-producing countries for the omission.

Several nations continued to raise serious objections to the draft.

Antwi-Boasiako Amoah, chair of the African Group of Negotiators, took a shot at the presidency for how it has handled the negotiations.

"With all due respect Mr. President, you have not shown respect to a group of 54 countries (from Africa) and my country (Ghana) for that matter," Amoah said.

Panama's representative said the Central American nation was amongst those who suffer most from the consequences and issue of climate justice.

Panamanian Environment Minister Juan Carlos Navarro said the talks were being held hostage by a "petroleum mafia" and need to be reformed.

He said science must be the basis for the cover decision, adding that those who contributed to this crisis must shoulder the responsibility to deal with it.

"We are running out of time," Navarro told attendees. "Panama cannot support this decision. The planet cannot continue to wait."

Ireland's Climate Minister Darragh O'Brien has said the European Union is "profoundly disappointed" with the current draft deal on the table at COP31 because it "fails to meet the moment" and that the bloc will not agree to it.

"We will not endorse a cover decision that does not respond to the science, that disregards the implementation gap, and risks deepening the chasm between global commitments and the action required to correct the overshoot and bring 1.5°C within reach," O'Brien said.

"We will not endorse a cover decision that does not even mention fossil fuels, the driver of climate change. And we will not accept negotiating backward in time. And we will not accept a COP that greenwashes fossil fuels," he added.

Former Greenpeace executive Jennifer Morgan said it would be better to end the COP31 summit with no deal than a watered-down declaration.

Her remarks come amid deep divisions over the omission of fossil fuels from the draft declaration.

Morgan rejected the notion that it would be better to accept the current draft than have no resolution at all, as the Turkish COP president has suggested.

"I think this is the day for the first time in my 31 COPs that I thought it was better not to have a decision that sets us back, that goes against what small islands and vulnerable people are saying," Morgan added.

She praised Germany for standing up to pressures to accept the current deal.

"Of course it would have been better to have a strong outcome here with everyone moving forward, but I agree with the German minister who just said if the text stays the way that it is, then it should not be accepted," Morgan said.

The US climate activist blamed oil-producing nations for the impasse.

"Saudi Arabia's fingerprints are all over" the current draft proposal, she said.

The omission has created a deadlock at COP31, which was due to conclude on Friday but has been extended as delegates seek consensus.

Morgan also blamed this year's Turkish presidency for failing to bring everybody together and instead letting countries that rely on fossil fuels dictate the terms of the agreement.

"Last year we had Brazil with a very strong presidency. They worked all year to bring countries together and they managed. It was not easy last year," Morgan said.

"This year we had presidencies that, in my view, were not well prepared. They did not bring countries together  (...) it's been shuttle diplomacy with no text for four days. That's not the way you get an agreement here," she added.

German Environment Minister Carsten Schneider has said the latest version of the draft agreement is "not acceptable."

Schneider said that the draft decision "not only fails to provide an adequate response but actually takes a step backward. A step backward from previous decisions on climate protection is the last thing Germany can support."

Schneider said that delegates had been working hard for days to reach a unanimous decision on "the world's greatest challenge: human-induced climate change," noting that this summer had "shown that the nature of this warming not only costs lives — as in Nepal — but also changes our world for the worse."

"It is up to us, as humanity, to do something about it. That is why a conference dedicated to climate protection — whose sole purpose is to protect the climate — must send a clear signal that we are taking action against this threat."

Schneider said he still believed most nations are willing to work to enact climate protection, but singled out those who still rely on fossil fuels to power their economies.

"The overwhelming majority of nations are committed to climate protection. Some, however, are still making huge profits from oil and gas and are unwilling to scale back their production or to pioneer a clear roadmap for reducing greenhouse gas emissions in this area," he added.

France has called a new draft deal proposed at COP31 a "backward" step.

French Ecological Transition Minister Monique Barbut criticized the deal for failing to mention fossil fuels, casting fresh uncertainty over whether nations will be able to reach an agreement.

"Time is ticking and we are not there yet," French Ecological Transition Minister Monique Barbut said in a statement.

COP31 President Murat Kurum has expressed his frustration over the deadlock at the summit.

The latest version of the draft deal again fails to mention fossil fuels, the main cause of global warming.

Over 40 hours after its scheduled conclusion and with no compromise in sight, the summit risks losing the numbers required to make any decision as delegates leave for home.

"If it is not adopted, I believe those who push the COP process into a deadlock will need to reflect on their position," Kurum said.

"It is our wish that this resolution be passed. We shall shortly be expressing our determination to ensure that this resolution is passed at the general meeting," he said.

"It is up to the parties involved. If there is sincerity here, and if there is a genuine desire to get things done, I believe that this can be achieved in this chamber. We shall see about that in a moment," Kurum added.

#142. Myanmar ruling gives new relevance to genocide convention and is ‘full of messages’ for Israel

Hailed as ‘a rare moment of justice’, judges’ decision sets out a roadmap for assessing the question of intent

The international court of justice (ICJ) verdict making Myanmar the first country in history to be found guilty of committing genocide does not just deliver justice for Rohingya victims. It also gives the 1948 genocide convention new relevance in governing the actions of nation states.

In their nine votes to six majority ruling, the panel of judges in The Hague set out a detailed roadmap for how the convention should be applied to real-world situations in which a government is accused of committing genocide against a whole population group, especially when it comes to assessing the question of intent.

The lengthy judgment will have been read particularly carefully by Israel, the only other country currently before the ICJ accused of genocide.

It is also relevant to Germany, which has been brought before the court by Nicaragua on the accusation it has facilitated and failed to prevent genocide in Gaza.

“I think it’s a judgment that brings the genocide convention alive,” said Philippe Sands, a leading human rights lawyer who was part of the Gambian legal team. “It gives the convention practical, real meaning. And if the alternative view had been taken, the convention would essentially become a dead letter.”

Amnesty International’s secretary general, Agnès Callamard, called the ruling “a rare moment of justice for survivors, victims and their families”. But it was also an assertion of humanitarian law in an increasingly lawless world.

“This is a good day for international law. We needed it,” said Dr Iva Vukušić, an assistant professor in international History at Utrecht University and a war crimes expert. “It is really important, clarifying a lot of law, and that’s going to be very helpful going forward.”

There will be a spillover from this ruling to its sister tribunal in The Hague, the international criminal court (ICC), providing a benchmark for when genocide charges could be made against individual perpetrators.

The Trump administration, instinctively hostile to international legal institutions, is also watching critically. The US ambassador to the UN told the ICJ before this judgment not to “lower the legal threshold for proving genocide” lest it be “weaponised” against US and allied soldiers.

The court did not directly lower the standard for demonstrating genocide, and the Gambia – which brought the case to The Hague – did not ask it to.

The judges kept the existing benchmark for judging genocide intent as the “only reasonable inference” of Myanmar’s actions and referred extensively to prior case law. But it went a long way towards clarifying how that standard should be applied in assessing atrocity crimes committed by states.

Before this case, only Serbia had faced the charge of genocide at the ICJ, in cases brought by Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina. In the Bosnian ruling in 2007, Belgrade was found to have failed to prevent the 1995 genocide in Srebrenica, but in both cases it was cleared of committing the genocide directly.

The new ICJ judgment finds the Myanmar military directly responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, a Muslim minority targeted in a ferocious counterinsurgency campaign that reached its peak in 2017.

In assessing the Myanmar campaign, the ICJ looked not just at the death toll of about 10,000 in assessing the scale of the atrocities, but at the total Rohingya population targeted in northern Rakhine state, 815,000 people. Significantly, it cast its net wider than just the death toll, considering mental damage done to the survivors, particularly victims of sexual violence, and the impact on reproduction in the Rohingya community.

Most importantly, the ICJ judgment provided a guide to interpreting the standard set by Serbian cases, in which a finding of genocide could be made only if there were not other plausible motives for mass killing.

In this judgment, the court ruled that Myanmar could have had other motives, such as the desire to displace or punish the Rohingya population, but that those motives could exist alongside genocidal intent. The judges said genocidal intent was the “only reasonable inference” from the “totality” of evidence.

In justifying that conclusion the judges pointed to several factors that international law experts say are relevant to the Gaza case, which is now hearing written pleadings and is expected to take at least another three years.

The common factors include the targeting of children, which the court described as striking at the “foundation” of the Rohingya as a group – the ICJ said up to 2,109 Rohingya children under the age of five had been killed, while more than 5,000 Palestinian children under five years old have been killed in the Gaza war.

Another incriminating factor cited by the court was the “bulldozing and large-scale terrain clearance” after the destruction of villages, followed by “rapid construction” including security installations, which has been the hallmark of Israeli army occupation of over 60% of the Gaza Strip.

“It’s full of messages for Israel,” said William Schabas, an international law professor and expert on genocide at Middlesex University. “There are obvious differences, of course, the evidence will be different, but if you exchanged the word Myanmar with Israel, you wouldn’t have to change much in the judgment.”

The fact that there were six dissenting voices on the bench suggested that substantial parts of the majority’s reasoning were not beyond dispute, and lawyers involved in the case cautioned that it did not necessarily mean that the same bench would find Israel guilty of genocide in Gaza. But the same lawyers added that the opposite was almost certainly true. If this case had failed, the case brought by South Africa against Israel would have been all but over.

#140. Nigerian youth have shown their power in the past - why aren't they shaping this election?

Bola Tinubu (in red hat) would be 79 at the end of a second term, if he is re-elected

Abdulhadi Dalhatu is something of a rarity in Nigeria politics.

At 38, he is among the young people seeking elected office in a country where about 80% of the population is under 40.

He hopes his age will stand in his favour as he campaigns for a seat in the state assembly in the northern Kano state in January's general elections.

"Younger people are the majority in this country and should be deciding the future," Dalhatu tells the BBC.

But Nigeria's political history suggests he faces an uphill battle.

Since the return to civilian rule in 1999, Goodluck Jonathan has been the youngest person elected president, taking office at the age of 53.

The current president, Bola Tinubu, is 74 and would be 79 by the end of a second term if he is re-elected in January.

His main challengers are also considerably older. Atiku Abubakar, seeking the presidency for the seventh time, is 79, while Peter Obi is 65.

Of the 18 presidential candidates, only one is under 40 - Moses Olusoji Adebisi, a 35-year-old businessman representing the Democratic Leadership Alliance (DLA).

Although he says he is confident of winning, few Nigerians appear to know much about him and he has not been seen much on the campaign trail.

The contrast is striking in a country with one of the world's youngest populations, where young people have repeatedly demonstrated their ability to shape political debate and mobilise for change.

In the previous election, Obi, then 61, galvanised millions of young Nigerians through a social media-driven movement known as the "Obedients".

His campaign promised a break from the established political order, appealing particularly to young people struggling to find jobs and earn a decent living.

But his failure to win the presidency left many of his supporters disillusioned.

"At the time we were so hopeful, but unfortunately the result wasn't what we wanted, which made many lose hope in the system," former Obedient Nonso Chukwudi tells the BBC.

The movement had built on the momentum of the EndSars protests in 2020, which grew into one of Nigeria's biggest youth-led movement in decades.

Initially sparked by anger over police brutality, the demonstrators developed into a wider call for political reform, accountability and greater youth participation in governance.

But the protests were eventually crushed by the authorities. About 56 were reportedly killed, although officials dispute this figure, while hundreds were arrested and some had their bank accounts frozen.

Both EndSars and the Obedients demonstrated the capacity of young Nigerians to mobilise around political causes.

Abdulhadi Dalhatu hopes to be president one day but bemoans the financial obstacles needed to contest

But turning that enthusiasm into sustained electoral participation has proved much harder.

Hajara Musa, 33, is among those who have lost faith in the political system. She has no plans to register or vote in the near future.

"I just feel like things are already determined and I'll just be wasting my time. It is going to be the same people all over again," she tells the BBC.

The electoral register appears to reflect this growing disillusionment.

Although the number of registered voters has increased by 10 million to over 103 million since the last election, enrolment of those under 35 has actually fallen, by about seven million.

Young people now account for just 29% of registered voters, down from 40% in 2023.

Nigeria's electoral commission, Inec, says it has made efforts to encourage more young people to register ahead of the 2027 general elections.

This includes appointing nine ambassadors from the entertainment industry, spanning film, music and comedy, to reach younger audiences.

But the figures suggest these efforts have yet to make a significant difference.

Dalhatu says he understands the frustration but argues that young Nigerians cannot expect change, if they stay away from the political process.

"Unfortunately, many prefer to just post on social media" instead of getting involved, he says.

Of course being young does not mean you are necessarily the best candidate.

For those who want to participate, there is a formidable obstacle to getting into politics - money.

Young people now account for just 29% of registered voters, down from 40% in 2023

Dalhatu dreams of becoming president one day, but says he has previously had to abandon campaigns because he could not raise enough funds.

The cost of running for office in Nigeria is notoriously high.

Under the Electoral Act, presidential candidates were allowed to spend up to 5bn naira (Â£2.8m; $3.7m) on campaigns ahead of the 2023 elections. That spending limit has since been doubled.

The cost of nomination forms alone puts the presidency beyond the reach of most aspiring young Nigerians.

The ruling All Progressive Congress (APC), along with the Peoples Democratic Party (PDP) and the African Democratic Congress (ADC) have each set their presidential nomination fee at 100m naira ($74,000; Â£56,000), making it extremely hard for new challengers to emerge.

Since the return to democracy in 1999, only two parties have governed Nigeria - the PDP from 1999 to 2015 and the APC since 2015.

These parties, alongside the ADC, formed last year by politicians who had defected from the other two, have established nationwide network of branches, supporters and campaigners.

Those aspiring candidates without the financial resources to secure nomination forms and compete in the internal party contests of the main parties have little chances of becoming president.

The barriers are also evident in the National Assembly.

Not one of Nigeria's 109 senators is under 40, while only 14 of the 360 members of the House of Representatives fall into that age group.

"I have been in politics for many years and I will tell you that young people in this country lack the resources to become president," says Dalhatu, who is running under Obi's Nigeria Democratic Congress.

Atiku Abubakar is seeking the presidency for a seventh time

Fatima Zahra Umar, a young politician in the APC who has previously tried to run for a seat in the House of Representatives, agrees.

"Young Nigerians are poor. Nigerian politics is cash-intensive, so participation is guided by money," she says, adding that young people are often content with having a bit of influence as a special advisor to a senior politician.

But political analyst Seun Ajayi believes the problems go beyond the financial barriers.

"We have some of the most brilliant young persons in the world, but they are either not interested in politics or feel satisfied when given a small role in government," he says.

"The old men are not going to sit idle and allow us to push them out. We have to fight for the big positions, but at the moment I don't see the zeal."

Umar believes changing attitudes towards young leadership is equally important.

"Culturally, we are not primed to trust or accept the brilliance of a younger person, so it will be very difficult," she says.

"Old men are still holding on to the system."

Some Nigerians took inspiration from the wave of success of young candidates in the recent midterm elections in the US, some of whom have roots in Africa.

"America Has Done It, Can Nigerians Do the Same?" entertainer Charly Boy wrote on X, in a post that was widely shared.

He said the US results served as a warning to Nigerian politicians that no matter how powerful the political machine, voters can change the direction of government.

He called on young people to interrogate candidates about their past promises: "Has life improved? Where are the jobs, security and affordable living?

"Democracy is not a spectator sport, nor is it about sharing rice," he wrote.

Can Nigeria's president regain voters' trust after painful first term?

Why Nigeria's one-time political giant may face extinction

Nigeria's mass wedding - and how telling lovers from fraudsters is proving tricky

#139. Iraq university’s removal of male professors teaching women sparks weeks of protests

Al-Qadisiyah University’s move a sign of the country’s slide into a Taliban-like gender segregation, say activists

The removal of male professors teaching female students at a university in southern Iraq has sparked protests and been criticised by activists as a Taliban-like gender segregation policy.

As many as 400 women are estimated to have joined protests over the past two weeks calling for the return of their professors at Al-Qadisiyah University in a move that appears to have surprised its administrators.

The university said the move was necessary to “prevent the mixing” of men and women and had been requested by the families of female students. But students said their families had known since before they enrolled that there were men on the teaching staff and supported their daughters’ protest.

Students said university bosses had mocked their protests and refused to discuss their concerns about the sudden loss of their academic supervisors. On social media, commentators also accused the women of seeking to keep male professors to “establish immoral relationships with them”.

“My classmates and I went to demonstrate, and the university president, Rahi al-Yasiri, blocked our path with his bodyguards, who then began making fun of us. He ordered them to take photographs of our faces, in an attempt to intimidate us and punish us later,” said Huda, 22, one of the female students.

Huda said the students tried to explain how men and women still worked together in society in Iraq. “Aren’t there doctors who treat both men and women? Why then do you prevent male professors from teaching us, too?” In response, Huda said Al-Yasiri replied: “Even if you hit your heads against the wall, I will not bring back the professors.”

The students said a large number of them were tied to graduation projects and research supervised by the professors. They said they had grown accustomed to their professors’ teaching methods, who also knew their students’ strengths and weaknesses, which made replacing them at this stage more complicated.

Female students said they had been told by the university that if they were not happy with the change, they should drop out.

An Iraqi-British feminist researcher, Balsam Mustafa, from the University of Cardiff, said the female students were “treated as if they are minors without the right to object to decisions affecting their academic lives”.

Mustafa said the removal of the male professors was a part of a worrying trend in women’s rights in the country, with “growing discussion about women’s clothing and chastity reflected in the celebration of female students who wear the abaya”.

An Iraqi lawyer and human rights activist, Israa Salman, said the restrictions on women was a “societal catastrophe”.

“Attempts are being made to suppress women’s talents and turn Iraqi society – a society that has produced remarkable minds and expertise, with women playing pioneering roles in many fields – into something resembling Kabul under Taliban rule.

“This contradicts Iraq’s constitution and the international treaties and conventions the country has signed and ratified,” said Salman.

#138. COP31 climate talks end without consensus on how to fund vulnerable countries or move the world away from coal, oil and gas use

After two weeks of tough talks, the COP31 climate summit has closed without a consensus on how to quit fossil fuels or fund countries struggling to adapt to a hotter world.

Almost two days into overtime, climate talks in the Turkish city of Antalya have collapsed as countries led by the European Union rejected a deal that critics say undermines the shift away from planet-heating fossil fuels.

After two weeks of tough negotiations, the EU said it could not support the proposed agreement, known as the Antalya Pact, put forward by the COP presidency, saying it does not respond to the science.

"We will not endorse a cover decision that does not even mention fossil fuels, the driver of climate change. And we will not accept negotiating backward in time," said Irish climate minister Darragh O'Brien, speaking on behalf of the EU on Sunday.

He said adopting a decision that omitted fossil fuels would be "unconscionable" and risked "deepening the chasm between global commitment and the action required."

Panama also rejected the deal, describing it as a step backwards in addressing the climate crisis, and adding that any decision must acknowledge "the truth about the collective failure on 1.5 degrees Celsius [2.7 degrees Fahrenheit]." That is the threshold outlined in the 2015 Paris Agreement to avert runaway climate change.

"Those who contributed to this crisis ... must shoulder the responsibility to deal with it," the Panamanian delegation said.

The Marshall Islands, which faces an existential threat from sea level rise, formally objected to the cover decision, saying "we will not compromise our future to benefit fossil fuel interests."   Chris ⁠Bowen, Australia's COP31 negotiating president ‌⁠said the breakdown in talks was very ‌disappointing. Russia, however, supported the decision to leave out wording on accelerating a phaseout of fossil fuels, arguing that they are still a necessary power source and that each country should be able to determine its own pathway on how to cut emissions.

Pakistan and Ghana, defended the agreement as a necessary compromise in the spirit of multilateralism. "We need to build bridges not barriers," said the Pakistan negotiator.

The marathon talks, held at a time when the window is rapidly closing to avoid the escalating impacts of climate change, including drought, deadly heat and flooding, also saw major disputes over financing.   Developing states blamed the collapse in talks on the EU and other wealthier nations for failing to scale up financing to help them adapt to climate-linked weather extremes and the damage they cause. The EU is the largest provider of climate finance and has resisted new adaptation finance targets.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Harjeet Singh of the Fill the Fund campaign accused wealthy countries of demanding greater climate ambition while refusing to stump up the cash to pay for it.

"You can't ask developing countries to transform their economies while they're drowning in disasters and debt," he added.

The final cover decision had proposed further discussions on "an implementation pathway for tripling adaptation finance by 2035" but without mentioning a figure. It noted that developing countries need some $300 billion annually for adaptation until 2035, and expressed concern about "the gap between climate finance flows and needs."

Outside formal negotiations, a separate initiative to Transition Away from Fossil Fuels (TAFF) has been gaining momentum.

Maina Talia, Tuvalu's minister for climate change and the environment, said TAFF was "moving beyond debating whether fossil fuels are simply mentioned in a text, to creating detailed plans for a global transformation."

At the same time, a growing number of states, including Ireland, Denmark, the Netherlands, France, Germany and Finland, have either completed or are working on so-called national roadmaps to wean themselves off fossil fuels. But Brandon Wu, director of Policy and Campaigns ActionAid USA, dismissed such plans as "meaningless" and "nothing more than restatements of inadequate existing policy" on the part of rich countries not stepping up on climate finance. "COP31 was one of the worst COPs ever, and it's because the EU and other developed countries came with zero spirit of solidarity, cooperation or compromise. They may as well have come to the COP shouting at vulnerable communities: 'we don't care if you die.'"

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The rejected cover decision had recognized that electrification can deliver emissions reductions, improve energy security and expand access to energy.

It included a call to increase the share of electricity in final energy demand from around 20% now to 35% by 2035 — a proposal heavily touted by co-host Turkey. Still, about 75 countries, including solar powerhouse China, adopted the pledge outside of the formal negotiations. It is non-binding and does not specify whether that additional electricity must come from clean sources. Critics say this leaves open the possibility that expanded electricity demand could be met by fossil fuels.

The talks in Turkey marked the first time a COP was taking place under the explicit knowledge that global temperatures will exceed 1.5 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels. Countries were split on how to deal with that so-called "overshoot."

The cover decision had called on states to commit to limiting overshoot , acknowledging that every increment of warming above current levels would "result in increasingly adverse, and in some cases irreversible, impacts and risks to human and natural systems.”

It also recognized the need for "deep, rapid ​and ‌sustained" emissions cuts and for action on ramping down potent greenhouse gas methane.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Another contenious issue at the talks and in the final draft decision was a call for "increasing" carbon capture and storage technologies that entails capturing CO2 and burying it. The text said the world should aim to reach "gigaton scale" by 2035.

Scientists say some carbon removal technologies will be necessary to curb emissions and bring temperatures back down. But critics argue they are unproven at scale, expensive and risk giving polluters an excuse to keep burning fossil fuels.

Vanuatu's climate minister Ralph Regenvanu said carbon capture and storage "had been singled out for special attention that it doesn't deserve."

The stakes were high at COP31. The summit followed a string of recent climate-related disasters, from deadly heat waves in Europe to a glacier collapse in Nepal that killed more than 1,400 people.

At the same time, geopolitical shocks that sent oil prices rising globally shifted the focus onto energy security and the future of fossil fuels.

Speaking near the end of the conference in Antalya, Ethiopian foreign minister Gedion Timothewos, who will preside over COP32, said his country aimed to "restore hope, rebuild trust and mobilize collective action" at the next summit to be held in 2027 in the Ethiopian capital Addis Ababa.

"We will work with you all to rebuild confidence in multilateral climate cooperation. This confidence must be earned through delivery. The decisions we take at COPs must advance climate action and implementation."

With reporting from Tim Schauenberg and Katharina Schantz in Antalya.

#136. How a teen gamer and his mother helped rescue a missing girl

Landon Tresp, 16, left, met the girl playing Among Us before alerting his mother, Khristina, 41, to the missing teen's predicament

Sixteen-year-old Landon Tresp was in the bedroom of his family's Colorado mountain home, playing an online game after finishing his daily chores, when he began chatting on his laptop with another player.

She was also a teenager, and the pair "kind of just clicked," he tells the BBC.

He told her he liked her name, and she confessed that it was an alias. She was a runaway, she said - and was being held against her will, hundreds of miles from home, though she didn't know exactly where. The girl, 17, and had been missing for more than a year.

Stunned, Landon felt "helpless," he says - and, after learning more details over two weeks of chatting, he decided to tell his mother, Khristina on 31 October this year. The 41-year-old nurse and mother of five was putting Halloween makeup on her younger children for trick-or-treating when he briefed her.

That set the mother and son on a path to track down his friend, contact her mother, cooperate with the FBI - and ultimately witness the rescue of the teen - only 24 hours after Landon first alerted Khristina to her plight.

"She had one opportunity to reach out her hand and ask for help, and she chose me," Khristina says of the girl, who was 15 when she went missing.

A screenshot shows Khristina on a call with the girl as she showed her the knife her alleged captor kept under his pillow

The Tresps have shared with BBC screenshots of communication with the girl and a photo matching the criminal complaint to corroborate their story.

Landon first told his mother about "Jenny" - whose identity the BBC is protecting - in mid-October, a few weeks before the rescue.

"He had mentioned something about maybe a week or so into talking to her that he met a girl online, and she was really sweet," says Khristina - who cautioned him that "you don't always know who's on the other side of the screen".

"She's just having a really hard time and looking for a friend," he told her.

But Landon was becoming increasingly concerned as Jenny told him more about her situation.

They'd met playing Among Us, a game Landon describes as "kind of like online space Clue".

After telling Landon that she was a runaway, "Jenny" said an older man had taken her across state lines and wouldn't let her leave. She was the subject of a missing person investigation, and she sent Landon links to stories about her disappearance. But she believed police were no longer looking for her.

Jenny was scared and desperate, and that's why Landon went to his mother.

Her maternal instincts on high alert, Khristina contacted Jenny herself on Discord, the encrypted platform.

During the conversation, Khristina said she told the Colorado mother how she'd been through "years of abuse in her life and lots and lots of problems".

Within hours, Jenny started calling Khristina "mama".

Among Us supports cross platform play online with 4-15 players on various devices

The Colorado mother knew things were serious when she read the missing persons articles. Jenny knew she was in Oregon and gave the name of a city, but she didn't know where she was being held exactly. Khristina knew she needed more. She told Jenny to find a piece of mail or another document in the house that had a full name on it, which the girl did.

"That's when I went to work," Khristina says, delving into tax and property records.

Khristina narrowed down addresses, then searched them on a real estate site to find pictures. She showed the photos to Jenny, who confirmed that the second one was where she was being held.

The "next thing she asked me was to please not tell anyone until [her captor] was gone - that he did have weapons in the house and she was very scared," Khristina said.

Jenny promised Khristina she'd call her back through the game platform when he left the next morning, and in the meantime Khristina began looking for the law enforcement agency that had been assigned to the missing teen's case. She contacted Jenny's mother on social media, spoke briefly to her on the phone and then got a call from an FBI agent.

"Have you seen her alive?'" the agent asked Khristina.

"And I said: 'I have; I video chatted with her, I've seen her, and she goes 'Oh my God, I cannot believe any of this'," Khristina recalled.

When Jenny called Khristina back after her alleged captor left home, the FBI monitored their conversation. According to the criminal complaint's record of the conversation, Jenny explained how she'd met Adam Hunter Harmel on social media in May 2025.

He'd told her he was disabled and his hips were "messed up," offering Jenny money if she'd help get him to Oregon.

After he picked her up in Utah, however, Harmel threw her phone into a river, drove her to Oregon and kept her in the basement. He forced her to sleep against the wall in his bed, with a knife under his pillow, to prevent her from escaping. He gave Jenny a cell phone with no ability to make calls, but it could connect to wifi, which is how she played the online game.

The 39-year-old "would become violent and aggressive", the document says, which included hitting her and sexually assaulting her.

He also allegedly forced Jenny to remove her braces with pliers so she wouldn't be identifiable.

As Khristina and the FBI heard all of this, law enforcement were staging to bring Jenny to safety, after being alerted to her whereabouts.

While Harmel was out of the home, officers were sent to the street and the address, and Khristina was prepping Jenny for her run to freedom.

She'd unsuccessfully tried escaping before, she told Khristina.

Law enforcement was "phenomenalâ¦giving me an update, you know, 'We're nine minutes out, we're seven minutes out,'" says Khristina, who was on the phone with Jenny the entire time, her son Landon watching at her side.

Khristina could hear other people in the home with Jenny, and she was afraid that someone would catch wind of what was happening and harm the girl.

But she kept any of those fears from Jenny.

"I want you to take three deep breaths with me," she told the teen. "When an officer tells me to go, and I relay that message to you, I want you to run up those stairs as fast as you can â¦ Grab that basement door.

"I want you to keep going down that hallway and grab that front doorâ¦ do not stop.'"

The mother and son watched breathlessly as Jenny ran into the arms of a state trooper.

Harmel is now in federal custody and has been charged in a criminal complaint with transportation with intent to engage in criminal sexual activity. Local Oregon media also reported he'd been accused of sexually abusing a six-year-old in the months before Jenny disappeared - and a sexual abuse protective order was granted against him in March 2025.

A lawyer for the defendant did not respond to a BBC request for comment.

Jenny, meanwhile, remains in daily communication with Khristina and Landon - who still enjoys playing online games with her.

"Sometimes we just sit on video chat, just so she knows she has someone that cares," Khristina says. "She was on video last night when I cooked dinner â¦it's nice to know that when she has a bad day or a bad moment, she can call me right away or call Landon right away."

"She's doing great for what she's gone through."

They haven't met in person yet, but they're in the process of trying to make that happen.

"I hope I can put my arms around her soon," Khristina says.

#135. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#133. Israel's Eisenkot condemns 'Jewish terrorism' in West Bank

Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot has condemned a recent series of arson attacks in the occupied West Bank as "Jewish terrorism." Israeli authorities have opened an investigation into the incidents.

Israeli authorities were on Sunday investigating arson attacks and vandalism against Palestinian properties and vehicles in the occupied West Bank which Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot condemned as "Jewish terrorism."

Images posted on social media showed vehicles in flames and slogans written on walls reading: "In blood and fire Judea has fallen, in blood and fire Judea will rise again," using a biblical name for the West Bank.

"Settlers attacked the northern area on the outskirts of the village at around two in the morning," Jihad al-Qaq, a local activist, told the AFP news agency.

"They set fire to around eight vehicles and wrote racist slogans on the walls of houses."

Israeli soldiers and police were deployed to the towns of Deir as-Sudan, Deir Abu Mashaal, Kafr Malik and Al-Tuwani following the reports, with the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) saying it "strongly condemns" the acts.

The suspected arson attacks were condemned by Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot, who is engaged in post-election negotiations to form a government to replace that of Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu.

"The night of arson … is Jewish terrorism in every sense of the word, and it must be called what it is," he said.

"Sabotage by extremist groups setting fire to vehicles and homes undermines regional stability, endangers lives, and severely damages the national security and foreign relations of the State of Israel," he said. Eisenkot used the biblical terms, Judea and Samaria, to refer to the West Bank territories in question.

More than 500,000 Israelis live in West Bank settlements, which are illegal under international law, alongside around 3 million Palestinians.

The West Bank has been occupied by Israel since 1967 but attacks against Palestinians have surged in the wake of Hamas' October 7, 2023 attack on Israel. Since then, more than 1,100 Palestinians in the West Bank have been killed by Israeli soldiers or settlers, according to data from the Palestinian Health Ministry.

Local resident Ahmad Ghneimat told the Associated Press that violence had increased after several Israeli settler outposts sprang up in the area. He said settlers have stolen sheep and violently harassed olive harvesters.

"We woke up at 2:30 at night to the sound of explosions [and] seven cars on fire," said Muntasir al-Malik, another resident. "We came out of the houses and panicked."

Eisenkot called for "severe sanctions" to be imposed on the individuals responsible.

Eisenkot's labeling of the violent settlers as "terrorists" follows the use of the same term by the United States' ambassador to Israel, Mike Huckabee, in September. US officials usually reserve the term for Palestinian militants.

#132. Briton swims length of strait of Hormuz to highlight environmental cost of war

UN patron Lewis Pugh saw ships ablaze and fighter jets overhead during three-day swim as Iran conflict continues

A British endurance swimmer has completed a three-day swim through the strait of Hormuz to highlight the environmental cost of the Iran war.

Lewis Pugh, a UN patron of the oceans, swam the 9.3-mile (15km) length of the waterway, which is a key route for oil shipments from the Middle East.

According to the Lewis Pugh Foundation, he witnessed several attacks on commercial shipping, explosions in the distance and fighter jets overhead.

An oil tanker was struck by a projectile about 2.5 miles from where he was swimming on the first day. On the second, he saw flames rising above a headland separating him from a burning liquefied petroleum gas carrier, Tara Gas, and later he passed close to the vessel while it was still on fire.

“My immediate thoughts were for the seafarers onboard,” Pugh said. “The flames were enormous. These vessels carry highly flammable gases, and an attack on one can have catastrophic consequences, not just for crew members but also on the surrounding marine environment.”

The 56-year-old undertook his first swim in a conflict zone to urge an end to the fighting, the reopening of the strait and action to address environmental damage caused by the conflict.

Believed to be the first person to attempt a long-distance swim in the strait since the conflict began in March, Pugh swam a route from Musandam Island, carving through the dramatic mountains and inlets of Oman’s Musandam peninsula and Iran’s spectacular mangrove forests, to reach the Cape of Hormuz at the peninsula’s northern tip.

He said: “War causes immense human suffering. Lives are lost, families are torn apart and homes, hospitals and schools can be destroyed in minutes. But there is another casualty that receives far too little attention: nature itself. And the damage can last long after the fighting has stopped.”

The small expedition team included a support boat and kayak that remained alongside Pugh during the swim.

Pugh said environmental damage caused by conflict often received less attention than the human cost. According to the UN, at least 175 vessels have been attacked since fighting began, and about 30 attacks have caused significant pollution.

The strait of Hormuz is one of the world’s most important shipping routes and is also home to wildlife including dugongs, sea turtles and whale sharks.

Pugh, who was born in Plymouth, said oil spills and other contamination could have long-term consequences for marine ecosystems including coral reefs, seagrass meadows and mangrove forests.

#129. Libya: Suspected drone attack hits Russian ship in Benghazi

A Russian vessel at an eastern Libyan naval base was hit by what is suspected to be a drone strike.

An explosion occurred early Sunday on a Russian ship at a naval base in Benghazi in eastern Libya due to a suspected drone attack.

"Initial investigations indicate that the explosion occurred on board a Russian ship docked in the port," the eastern-based Libyan administration said in a statement.

"It is suspected that the ship was attacked by drones."

According to medical sources, injuries were reported, but no deaths.

The statement added that, as part of a cooperation agreement between Russia and the eastern-based Libyan government, the vessel had stopped at the Benghazi port to refuel and resupply on its way back to Russia.

According to satellite imagery provider Vantor, the ship was the Merkury, a Russian Navy corvette. Imagery taken on Sunday revealed that the majority of the vessel had capsized and sunk, leaving only the bow visible above the water.

Since the 2011 uprising that overthrew longtime dictator Moammar Gadhafi, Libya has been divided between the UN-supported government in Tripoli and a rival administration in the east backed by military commander Khalifa Haftar.

The eastern-based administration said the port and naval base facilities in Benghazi, including the warehouses, were safe. They added that normal operations would resume once the fire was under control and the site was secured.

The authorities condemned the suspected attack as a violation of Libya's sovereignty and a threat to maritime safety. They said they would take legal and diplomatic measures against those responsible and would launch a joint investigation with Russia.

As of Sunday afternoon, no one has claimed responsibility for the suspected attack.

The administration also stressed that Libya was not a party to the war between Russia and Ukraine or to any armed conflict beyond its borders. It said that its relations with friendly countries were a sovereign matter.

It said it would not allow "Libyan ​ports, territory or territorial waters to be turned into an arena for settling scores or exporting international ​conflicts."

Meanwhile, later on Sunday, two Libyan military sources told Reuters that a commercial vessel had been targeted by a drone boat off the coast of Benghazi. They added that Libyan naval forces had rescued the crew

Libyan naval and air forces intercepted more than 12 naval drones, or drone boats, off the coast of Benghazi during the Sunday evening, according to Reuters sources.

The incident in Benghazi occurred as Ukrainian forces increased their long-range attacks against Russia, extending far beyond the front lines.

On Saturday, a drone hit a Russian cargo ship in the eastern Baltic Sea near Finnish waters. Finland's border guard said Ukraine carried out the attack.

In March, a Russian tanker was attacked in the Mediterranean near Maltese waters. Libyan officials at the time believed this to be a sea drone attack.
