#127. Netanyahu rival condemns ‘Jewish terrorism’ after settler attacks on Palestinian villages

Dozens of militant Israeli settlers raided communities on Saturday night, setting fire to vehicles and several homes

Arson attacks by militant Israeli settlers on Palestinian villages in the occupied West Bank have caused a political row in Israel, drawing condemnation from Gadi Eisenkot, the opposition leader, who described them as “Jewish terrorism”.

Dozens of settlers raided Palestinian communities overnight on Saturday, setting fire to about a dozen vehicles and several homes and scrawling racist graffiti. At least four communities were attacked. Israeli police said they had launched an investigation with forensic teams.

Photographs from Kafr Malik, near Ramallah, showed one slogan written in Hebrew that read: “This is the first revenge.” Several vehicles and homes had been set alight in the village.

Images from nearby Deir al-Sudan showed vehicles on fire. Palestinian reports said an agricultural building had been set ablaze in Deir Ballut and cars torched in Deir Abu Mashal.

An ⁠inconclusive election last month stripped Benjamin Netanyahu, the prime minister, of his majority. Eisenkot has been asked to form a government, though his prospects seem little better.

“Last night’s arson attacks in Judea and Samaria were acts of Jewish terrorism, in every sense of the term,” Eisenkot said, using the biblical name for the West Bank. “Extremist groups that set fire to vehicles and homes are undermining regional stability, putting lives at risk and doing grave damage to Israel’s national security and foreign relations.”

Referring to the far-right ministers Itamar Ben-Gvir and Bezalel Smotrich, he added: “The actions led by Ben-Gvir and Smotrich in the West Bank have brought the area to the brink of unchecked anarchy.”

Eisenkot, who has been given 28 days to form a government by the president, Isaac Herzog, with a possible 14-day extension, urged the government and security establishment to “use every tool at their disposal – including administrative detention and targeted operations by the Shin Bet – to stop the rioters and impose harsh sanctions on them”.

In an unprecedented rebuke on Sunday, several other members of the Israeli parliament criticised Netanyahu over escalating violence by militant settlers against Palestinians.

Yair Lapid, the leader of Yesh Atid, held Netanyahu’s government responsible for encouraging the attacks. “Jewish terrorism is the product of criminal negligence by the Israeli government,” he said on X, adding that ministers were deliberately encouraging the militant settlers’ violence “through their silence”.

Yair Golan, the leader of the Democrats party, warned of the possibility of a third intifada and described the West Bank as “on the brink of an explosion”.

“Anyone who wants to prevent a third intifada must replace the current government, which provides cover and support for Jewish terrorism and abandons security,” Golan said on X. “Jewish terrorism harms innocent Palestinians and Israeli army soldiers, and pushes Israel toward unprecedented international isolation.”

Noam Tibon, a retired army major general and Knesset member, gave a similar warning. “If Israel does not eliminate Jewish terrorism in the West Bank, it will destroy Israel,” he wrote.

Netanyahu has not yet responded to the accusations.

Australia announced sanctions on Wednesday against three people and four Israeli entities, including a construction company, over their role in the expansion of illegal settlements and human rights abuses against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank.

A day earlier the UK imposed sanctions on eight Israeli construction companies involved in building illegal settlements in the West Bank, which the UN regards as Palestinian territory under Israeli occupation since 1967.

Since the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attacks on Israel, there has been a surge of Israeli settlement expansion and violence against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank. More than 1,100 Palestinians have been killed there by Israeli forces or settlers.

On Tuesday, a 72-year-old Palestinian woman was shot dead in an overnight attack by Israeli militant settlers in a village near Jericho, where the group has recently moved in after taking over several Palestinian homes.

#125. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#123. Sudan: Drones hit UN building, residential area in Khartoum

The drones targeted Khartoum, damaging a UN building in the residential area. The UN agency deemed it unacceptable that humanitarian workers were exposed to such danger.

The United Nations reported on Sunday a drone attack on a residential area in Khartoum, Sudan's capital.

A UN building located in a civilian neighborhood was damaged in the attack, according to the UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA).

"It is unacceptable that we are put at such risk," said the UN's Resident and Humanitarian Coordinator in Sudan, Denise Brown.

The AFP news agency reported that explosions were heard around Khartoum Airport and in other parts of the city from approximately 2:00 a.m. to 4:30 a.m. local time.

According to a witness, a drone strike completely destroyed a house. There were no immediate reports of casualties.

Meanwhile, a Sudanese army source said the drones had been launched from Ethiopia.

Sudan has repeatedly accused Ethiopia of allowing attacks to be launched from its territory. However, Addis Ababa has denied these accusations.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Sudan has been engulfed in a civil war since April 2023. The army of de facto ruler Abdel-Fattah al-Burhan and the RSF militia led by Mohamed Hamdan Daglo have been fighting for control of the country.

The conflict has killed tens of thousands of people and displaced more than 14 million.

According to UN data, the use of drones has dramatically altered the course of the war this year, killing more than 1,100 people in the first six months.

The UN also said that of the hundreds of child casualties this year, two-thirds were killed or injured by drone strikes.

Just days ago, a Sudanese army drone strike killed 15 people, including four children, in a West Kordofan village. The army was advancing through the state and recapturing territory from the RSF.

#121. Europe is entering a ‘supercycle’ of national elections. Their outcomes could shake the EU’s foundations

As nationalist, Eurosceptic parties surge in four of the bloc’s biggest member states, the coming year’s polls could test its resilience like never before

In late 1998, Tony Blair was prime minister of Britain, Lionel Jospin was prime minister of France, and Gerhard Schröder had just been elected chancellor of Germany. The EU had 15 member states and pro-European, centre-left parties were in power – either alone or in coalitions – in all but two of them.

Brisk economic growth was lifting living standards. Eastward enlargement was under way. In Washington, Bill Clinton was pursuing the same brand of “third way” centrism as a middle path between free-market capitalism and traditional socialism. The EU felt like it had the tide of history behind it.

Not quite three decades later, the continent’s politics once again seem to be tilting in a single direction – except this time, the momentum belongs to the nationalist far right, and the outcome could be a significant unpicking of the postwar European consensus that built the EU.

Between the end of this month and the end of next year, an extraordinary electoral sequence will sweep the bloc. Voters in four of the EU’s five biggest member states, by population and economy, head to the polls, with parliamentary elections also due in Estonia, Finland, Greece and Slovakia, and regional ballots in Germany.

Spain kicks off the cycle with a snap general election on 29 November, with prime minister Pedro Sánchez’s socialist coalition facing probable defeat to rightwing parties in the shape of the conservative People’s party (PP) and far-right Vox, which already govern together in some Spanish regions.

Next come presidential and parliamentary elections in France in the spring. The clear frontrunner in the presidential race is Marine Le Pen of far-right the National Rally (RN), well ahead of the far-left firebrand Jean-Luc Mélenchon and a hopelessly divided mainstream opposition.

When Poland votes later next year, nationalist parties that between them command nearly half the national vote could well unseat the liberal coalition of prime minister, Donald Tusk, despite recent splits. And in Italy, Giorgia Meloni, whose Brothers of Italy party has far-right roots, hopes to secure a second term in elections due before the end of 2027 – while facing pressure on her right flank from the upstart National Future party.

Paradoxically, this nationalist surge arrives amid strong public support for the EU. Shocked by the chaos of Brexit and seeking reassurance in an increasingly alarming world, a record 74% of Europeans say their country benefits from membership, and very few Eurosceptic parties now advocate leaving outright.

The Eurosceptics and nationalists do, however, still view the bloc as a top-down project that undermines national sovereignty, and aim to transform it into an altogether looser association of sovereign nation-states. In the words of the Italian political scientist Natalie Tocci, that means this coming electoral supercycle could “dramatically change the face of Europe” – triggering not simply “a slow erosion of the European project but a hollowing out, if not an outright implosion”.

All elections differ and national specifics loom large. But analysts agree the underlying forces at work here are connected: sluggish growth, ageing populations, strained public finances and a spiralling cost of living have chipped away at support for Europe’s traditional catch-all centre-left and centre-right parties. The wave of youth-led protests sweeping Europe is only the most visible sign of deep voter discontent.

Meanwhile, nationalist parties have become increasingly adept at conjuring and exploiting an ever-evolving list of supposed threats and crises, from migration and vaccines to the green transition, support for Ukraine, globalisation, international elites and supranational institutions – narratives bolstered by Russian disinformation and overt backing for “patriotic” parties from Donald Trump’s White House. As a result, politics has fragmented, and polarised: nearly one in four voters now backs far-right parties – a share that has grown almost fivefold since the mid-1990s.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

The rise has not been linear: over the past year or so, while the far-right Alternative für Deutschland scored a historic regional election victory in eastern Germany, in the Netherlands Geert Wilders’s Freedom party lost nearly a third of its seats, Viktor Orbán’s Fidesz was soundly beaten in Hungary and the far-right Sweden Democrats shed support for the first time in 16 years.

But the underlying upward trend appears clear – fuelled above all, political scientists argue, by the normalisation and mainstreaming of far-right ideas, most notably on immigration. Centre-right parties are gradually dismantling “firewalls” that once barred cooperation with the radical right. At the European level, the centre-right European People’s party has increasingly turned to farther-right groups to roll back parts of the European green deal and tighten migration rules.

For the EU, the biggest impact of this election supercycle could be in the European Council of national leaders, which now features just three centre-left heads of government out of 27. If Eurosceptic leaders in France, Spain and Poland join those of Czechia, Italy, Romania, Slovakia and Slovenia by the end of 2027, they would not have the numbers to rewrite EU law – but they would constitute a formidable blocking minority.

EU budget negotiations, future enlargement, aid to Ukraine and deeper integration in essential fields such as defence, energy, finance and technology could all face paralysis, analysts warn.

Safety in numbers, notes Tocci, could change current leaders’ behaviour in the council. Meloni’s relative restraint has been dictated, in part, by her relative isolation among mainstream peers such as Tusk, Sánchez and France’s Emmanuel Macron (alongside Rome’s reliance on €200bn in EU recovery funds).

If she were instead sitting down with the likes of Le Pen, Poland’s far-right Law and Justice (PiS) leader, Jarosław Kaczyński, and a Vox-backed Spanish government, with National Future’s hardline Roberto Vannacci breathing down her neck at home and those EU funds winding down, she might have less incentive to play pro-EU ball.

Analysts agree, though, that the decisive battleground is France. Le Pen’s election platform sets her on a collision course with Brussels: she aims to cut €11bn from France’s EU budget contribution, hold a constitutional referendum to proclaim the supremacy of French over European law, and breach various single-market rules.

And France “carries very significant influence over the EU”, said Mujtaba Rahman of the Eurasia Group consultancy. Compared with Orbán, he notes, a president Le Pen would have “far more leverage to carry out the far-right playbook of subverting the EU’s law-based, supranational architecture”.

Until now, Brussels has contained disruptors such as Orbán largely because there were rarely more than two or three round the table. A cohesive Eurosceptic bloc would leave the EU unable to enforce rule-of-law standards, further integrate economic and security policies or, indeed, agree on anything much at all. The first victim would very probably be Ukraine, with Le Pen leading a blocking caucus against further aid.

Three decades on from 1998, Europe’s coming elections, concluded Stefan Lehne of the Carnegie Europe thinktank, could well turn into “key tests of the EU’s resilience”. The threat to the bloc might not be immediate or existential, he said, but the medium-term risks were significant.

“A stronger role for the radical right may be unlikely to dismantle the EU as it exists today,” Lehne said, but could “prevent it from becoming what it needs to be to deal with the challenges of the future”. And gradually but inevitably, prolonged failure to act collectively as and where needed would lead a “loss of relevance”.

#119. ‘Serious error of judgment’: outrage over military AI event at Berlin arts complex

Kraftwerk Berlin, part of same development as techno club Tresor, transformed into ‘war room’ for arms firm event

With their cavernous post-industrial venues, teeth-grinding four-by-four beats and bouncers that look like Blade Runner extras, Berlin’s nightclubs trade on a reputation for bleakness. A recent event at one of the city’s most legendary clubland institutions, however, has proven too dystopian even for seasoned techno-heads.

On 11 November, the Kraftwerk Berlin arts complex had its central hall transformed into what visitors described as something resembling a war room, with maps and aerial footage projected on to concrete walls, drones hanging from the ceiling, and guards in flak jackets patrolling the premises with sniffer dogs.

Those who flocked to the venue – which hosts the experimental music festival Atonal and is part of the same complex as the techno club Tresor – had come for the launch of Clarity, an AI targeting system developed by the German arms startup Helsing.

Kraftwerk Berlin this week apologised for a “serious error of judgment” and vowed to revise its commercial rental department’s booking policy, after outrage over the event rippled through Berlin’s music scene. The row has raised uncomfortable questions about the commercialisation of the German capital’s famed nightlife, and the culture sector’s traditional pacifism at a time of growing security threats.

The Munich-based Helsing was founded in 2021 and counts the Swedish tech entrepreneur and Spotify co-founder Daniel Ek among its investors and board co-chairs. On its website, it describes Clarity as an agentic intelligence platform designed to use drone, satellite and sensor data to lock on to targets.

It claims that human operators ​remain responsible for approving any strikes.

Helsing says Clarity is “developed and built in Europe under European control”. The market leader for battlefield software is Palantir’s Maven, the system that has powered US military strikes on Iran this year. European governments have been scrambling for alternatives to US-developed systems. France’s domestic intelligence service has already announced it is dropping Palantir for a homegrown rival.

But European governments’ boosting of their arms industries is also clashing with an anti-militaristic cultural consensus – nowhere more so than in the former cold war pressure cooker of Berlin, where nightclubs became spaces to dance away the anxieties of the arms race.

“The fact there is a war in Europe and that Ukraine needs German support should not mean that we switch off all our critical faculties,” said Michail Stangl, a Berlin-based music curator and DJ. “We are already seeing how AI-driven targeting systems are being used in Gaza, and it’s hard to imagine that once a company like Helsing develops a successful arms system, it would not sell it on to authoritarian regimes, domestic police, or organisations like Frontex to be used against migrants.”

Speaking to the German broadcaster RBB at its launch event, a Helsing spokesperson said none of its military systems were designed to identify persons, and that Clarity was built to seek out objects such as radar systems, launch pads and ships.

It also said Clarity’s agents specialised in “looking for patterns of behaviour”, which could be interpreted as relating to human activity. Helsing did not respond to a request for comment.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

The Clarity launch event was condemned in the strongest terms by Tresor, a nightclub and record label that acted as a bridge between post-Wall Berlin and Detroit’s electronic music scene in the 1990s. “It has no place in the spaces our community has built⁠,” it said in a statement.

Tresor’s founder, the Berlin nightlife entrepreneur Dietmar-Maria “Dimitri” Hegemann, is also the managing director of Kraftwerk Berlin. Hegemann has called the booking “unacceptable”, saying it was done by “a staff member at the company responsible for Kraftwerk’s commercial rental business”, without him being consulted. Given his central role at the arts complex, and the level of planning usually required for such a high-security event, this has surprised some Berlin clubland insiders.

That Berlin’s former underground music venues are rented out for commercial purposes is no longer a deviation from the norm. As recently as 19 October, Kraftwerk Berlin hosted an investment summit organised by the German government and featuring a podium discussion between the chancellor, Friedrich Merz, and the chip manufacturer Nvidia’s then chief executive, Jensen Huang.

In a Q&A released after the Clarity row, Kraftwerk Berlin said it had declined a proposed Palantir-sponsored event in 2022 after consulting its cultural teams.

Defenders of such commercial usages point out that many of Berlin’s nightclubs are struggling with rising rents and plummeting bar revenues. Kraftwerk Berlin may not be one of them, however. The total assets of its operating company, Schaltraum Berlin, were reported in June as having a value of €4.3m (£3.6m).

#118. Trump threatens ban on US firms building AI data centres overseas

Trump says he won't allow American companies to build data centres "outside" the US

Donald Trump has said he will ban US companies from building AI data centres overseas, threatening a "tremendous tax" for tech firms who do so.

"We're going to keep it all in this country," the US president said, while providing little detail on the potential policy he said others in his cabinet were hearing about for the first time.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

Tech giants including Meta and Google have entered agreements to build data centres overseas. It remains unclear how such a policy would be enforced if it was brought in.

"We're not going to allow our companies to build data centres outside of our country," Trump told reporters in a press briefing following his return from his trip to Asia.

"Because China will take all of them, and we're not allowing that to happen."

But whether that idea will become policy remains unclear.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story, but I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now, they're here," he said.

Trump floated the policy after saying the US was leading on data centre construction, and he would not allow China to beat the US when it came to AI - which he calls super intelligence, or SI.

It also follows an announcement earlier this week that would restrict the export of advanced computer chips and who can access the computing power provided by them.

Washington on Monday announced firms would require a licence to buy advanced chips, so US officials can monitor whether they are being used to serve Chinese companies or they are being smuggled into China.

The move will impact South East Asian countries that have invested heavily in data centres, as Chinese companies seek to rent computing power from overseas.

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Data centres are crucial to the AI boom. The vast warehouse-style buildings house the advanced computer chips and servers that are needed to run AI programmes such as Claude and ChatGPT.

As of April there were about 3,000 data centres across the US, with another 1,500 in various stages of approval and construction, according to the Pew Research Centre.

Many of them are built in rural areas, and concerns about issues including noise pollution and energy consumption helped drive Democratic candidates promising tougher rules on data centre construction to wins on 3 November.

"You ought to take advantage of the data centres. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but take advantage don't shut them off," Trump said.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy and they have everything. They're tremendous assets."

Large tech firms - often called AI hyperscalers due to the size of their investments in AI - have also been branching out, and Canada has made moves to step in to offer itself as an alternative location for these sites, with less local pushback against projects so far.

Meta has already broken ground on a data centre, external in the province of Alberta, and Anthropic has signed a long-term agreement to lease a proposed data centre in Nova Scotia.

The BBC has reached out to Meta for comment on Trump's remarks.

In September Google announced a â¬13bn ($15bn: Â£11bn) investment in AI infrastructure in Finland, which included funding for three data centres.

Last month, building work for two of those centres was halted due to concerns over the clearance of more than 300 hectares of forest.

Trump called out the build in Finland specifically, saying his administration was "not allowing that to happen".

#116. Turkey: Thousands protest closure of critical media outlets

Protesters gathered in Istanbul to show their support for press freedom in Turkey. The protest was called just days after the police raided Sozcu TV and arrested several senior staff members.

Several thousand people in Istanbul took to the streets on Sunday to protest the closure of several opposition-linked media outlets in Turkey.

Protesters chanted, cheered, and whistled as they marched several kilometers through the city, calling for press freedom and freedom of expression.

They carried a huge banner that read: "We want freedom of thought, freedom of the press, and freedom for journalists." The protesters were also carrying placards that read, "The press cannot be silenced."

Turkey's main opposition party, Yeni Parti, called for a protest just days after police raided Sozcu TV, one of the last opposition-aligned television channels.

Senior staff members were arrested during the raid. A government-appointed trustee was installed, and the TV company was placed under compulsory administration.

It was the third takeover in a year following similar actions against Tele1, Halk TV, Turkey's most-watched news channel, and the leading digital news website, T24.

Timur Soykan, a journalist who was briefly detained for criticizing the government, said that the government was doing everything in its power to silence journalists but was failing to do so.

Soykan asked the public to support Halk TV journalists, who plan to continue broadcasting on a new YouTube channel.

Over the past decade, press freedom has faced sustained pressure in the form of prosecutions, digital censorship, and government accusations that journalists are spreading "fake news."

In the last two months alone, Turkey has arrested several journalists for their reporting on a multibillion-dollar investment fund scandal. At least two of the journalists remain in pretrial detention.

According to the media rights group MLSA, twenty-five journalists are currently imprisoned in Turkey.

This year, Reporters Without Borders ranked Turkey 163rd out of 180 countries in its Press Freedom Index.

#113. Zelensky says Putin is a 'killer' after Trump calls Russian leader a friend

Watch: Trump defends calling Putin a 'friend'

Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky has said Vladimir Putin is a "killer" in response to Donald Trump referring to the Russian leader as his "friend" in China this week.

Speaking to CNN, Zelensky said it was up to the US president "how to call Putin" but "for us, he is an enemy... for us, he's a killer".

Before Thursday's historic trilateral meeting with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Putin, Trump called Putin his "friend" and said the two leaders have had a "great relationship for many years".

Russian attacks continued overnight, with two people killed, including a boy, and 52 injured in missile and drone strikes on Zelensky's hometown of Kryvyi Rih in the central Dnipropetrovsk region, officials said.

Ukraine's state emergency service DSNS said 11 people were also injured in the north-eastern Kharkiv region, and one person in Rivne, in the north-west.

On Friday, at least nine people were killed in Russian strikes targeting bridges in the capital Kyiv and railway stations in western Ukraine - with Zelensky calling the attack the "biggest this year" after an onslaught of 110 missiles, including 27 ballistic, and more than 400 drones.

The strikes came days after Ukraine had badly damaged the Crimean Bridge.

Hours after Friday's strikes on Ukraine, Trump told reporters at the White House that Putin would "love to end the war".

"They [Zelensky and Putin] both want to end it and it's a very good thing I have a relationship," he said on Friday.

Trump also doubled down on his description of the Russian leader as a friend but said he "can't speak" to whether Putin is a friend to the US.

However Zelensky said despite Trump's stance, he saw Putin as "a killer".

"I think mostly for the people in the world, he is a killer because he's killing - by the way- he's killing each day and night," he told CNN.

Zelensky also said speaking about Putin like he was a "friend" meant "he doesn't feel enough pressure".

The war of words came as neighbouring Poland said a Russian cruise missile was among three objects that violated its airspace in the south-east of the country early on Friday.

Polish Defence Minister WÅadysÅaw Kosiniak-Kamysz said the missile hit a rural area at Nowy Lipowiec about 50km (31 miles) from the Ukrainian border.

This was about 100km from where European troops - from 11 of the 36 member nations of the coalition of the willing - were completing a training exercise, Polish authorities said. Another cruise missile and a jet-powered drone were suspected to have hit two other locations, they added.

On Saturday, Polish Foreign Minister RadosÅaw Sikorski told the BBC the cruise missiles had guidance systems, so "it is very unlikely that they veered off course as a result of Ukrainian electronic warfare".

"This is a war in Europe that affects our security," he told Radio 4's Today programme, also referencing similar incidents in recent months across Europe.

Nobody was hurt and no damage has been reported, but Warsaw described the incident as a "provocation" from Moscow.

Nato Secretary General Mark Rutte condemned "grave violations of Polish airspace" and said the military alliance had scrambled its jets in response.

Russia has made no comment about the violations, which took place during Friday's massive attack on Ukraine.

Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

#112. UK-EU food deal hailed by British firms: ‘We’re pretty aligned anyway’

Exporters welcome end in sight for Brexit checks – but are frustrated that implementation will take so long

British producers have broadly welcomed the UK’s deal with the EU, designed to remove the vast majority of physical checks on food, plants and animal products.

The sanitary and phytosanitary (SPS) deal is expected to benefit Britain’s food exporters and will enable UK delicatessens and other small retailers to import European cheese and wine again, something that became economically unviable because of costly Brexit checks.

The agreement is due to take effect on 1 March 2028 and is also intended to reopen the EU market for UK products including fresh sausages, burgers, seed potatoes and molluscs.

However, several years after the UK left the bloc, upending many decades-old trading arrangements, some food exporters said they would remain cautious until the deal came into force.

“Nobody’s going to believe this until it has actually physically happened,” said Iain Barbour, the operations director of the Scottish seed potato producer Jamieson Brothers.

Seed potatoes were not included in the post-Brexit trade deal after the two sides disagreed over certification, halting exports from the UK to the EU despite Scottish seed potatoes’ high health status classification.

The exclusion particularly hit the Scottish industry, whose farmers were among the biggest exporters for the production of chips and crisps in the world, as well as growers in northern England. Scottish seed potato farmers previously accounted for three-quarters of UK production and the sector was worth about £112m a year.

Ireland and the EU represented a fifth of Jamieson Brothers’ trade before Brexit. The company particularly suffered from the loss of the Irish business, given it grew many varieties that were popular on plates in the north and south of Ireland.

While the company adapted its business to start packing work that would have previously been carried out in the EU, Barbour estimates this change cost about £500,000.

“We evolved, we worked with more partners and that’s what kept the business going,” Barbour said.

Barbour is looking forward to supplying his Irish customers once againHowever, he said it would take at least three years until he could revive varieties that were no longer being grown and were instead stored in a seed bank.

“A lot of Scottish farmers stopped producing varieties that were popular in Ireland as they weren’t desired in England. Varieties that had been going about 100 years were killed off in a couple of years,” he said. “So we’ll have to look at bringing them back.”

The planned implementation of the food deal in March 2028 would also be “a couple of months late for the trade”, given most sales happen at the start of the year and the crops needs to be grown in advance.

Barbour anticipates most growers will be wary and will only fully resume trading in 2029. “People are obviously going to be a little bit hesitant to grow anything until it’s 100% official,” he said.

British exporters of live bivalve molluscs – the collective term for shellfish including mussels, oysters, clams, cockles and scallops – hope the deal will give the sector a shot in the arm.

The industry is small and specialised. It was valued at less than £12m a year before Brexit, but it is crucial for some coastal communities.

Offshore Shellfish, a family-run business produces rope-grown mussels in the waters of Lyme Bay, Devon, has managed to continue exporting to its European customers despite the administrative burden and occasional blocks by French customs.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

The company exports the vast majority of the blue mussels it produces. It sends to the Netherlands for processing, before most end up in restaurants or on supermarket shelves in Belgium, where they are prized as a key ingredient in moules-frites, one of the country’s national dishes.

Sarah Holmyard, the commercial director of Offshore Shellfish, said the most important detail was an assurance that the deal would allow live bivalve molluscs to be exported even if they did not come from waters with the highest quality rating.

Offshore Shellfish’s farms are in waters which are considered “class A” for most of the year, but before the new SPS deal, any change in water quality to “class B” could have temporarily halted exports.

“That’s a huge win for us because we’ve been pushing and pushing and pushing for that,” Holmyard said.

However, the deal will come too late for those UK shellfish producers that have closed down since Brexit. Three out of four mussel fishing companies in the Menai strait, the channel that separates the north Wales mainland from the island of Anglesey, stopped trading after exports became too difficult.

The promised end of physical border checks on dairy, fish and meat products was welcomed by Rich Clothier, the managing director of Wyke Farms. The Somerset-based cheese and butter maker exports about a quarter of its cheddar to the EU, particularly France, Germany and Spain.

Dairy exports to the EU have been less contentious since Brexit than seed potatoes or mussels, but not without challenges. The removal of veterinary checks is viewed as a significant benefit by Clothier.

“It’s a very bureaucratic process to get everything signed off, in what is not a very modern sort of system,” he said. “The agreement will save a huge amount of cost and product waste as well, because these are perishable.

“Any reduction in friction saves cost, it makes business more efficient. But it is really frustrating that it’s going to take so long [to be implemented] because, to be honest, we’re all pretty aligned anyway.”

#110. Trafalgar Square Christmas tree felled in Norway

Karen Scarborough (left) and Anne Lindboe got to work with a two-handled saw

This year's Trafalgar Square Christmas tree has been cut down in a forest outside Oslo, ready to begin its journey to London.

The 21m (69ft) tree, nicknamed Jolly Cheery, is about 75 years old. It was felled on Friday by the Lord Mayor of Westminster, Karen Scarborough, and the Mayor of Oslo, Anne Lindboe.

Norway has sent a tree to the capital every year since 1947 as a thank you for Britain's support during World War Two, when King Haakon VII and the Norwegian government lived in exile in London.

Its lights will be switched on by the two mayors at a ceremony in the square on Thursday, 3 December.

Scarborough said: "It's my greatest honour to take part in this wonderful tradition for both the cities of Oslo and London.

"This tree is a powerful symbol of friendship and peace, reminding us to be there for one another during the festive period."

The 21m tree has been nicknamed Jolly Cheery

Scarborough thanked "the people of Oslo and Norway for this truly fabulous gift" and added: "With the care and dedication of the team, I'm sure it will look magnificent on Trafalgar Square."

Lindboe said: "Oslo has been sending a Christmas tree to London for almost eight decades, and every year we send a tree across the North Sea with the same message: we have not forgotten.

"The tradition has a powerful history, and its message remains just as relevant today.

"I hope Jolly Cheery spreads warmth and Christmas cheer to everyone who passes by, and reminds us how much it means to be there for one another, especially in difficult times."

Count the rings: Anne Lindboe (left) and Karen Scarborough (right) with a slice of the 75-year-old trunk

For the second year running, the tree was chosen with the help of a social media poll.

Organisers said it would travel by road in Norway and England using only electric and biodiesel vehicles, cutting the journey's carbon footprint by 80%. It will cross the North Sea on a regular cargo ship from the port of Brevik.

Once in London, it will be decorated in the traditional Norwegian style, with 25m strings of festoon lighting fitted with energy-efficient bulbs.

#109. Cop31 climate talks break down as key deal rejected

EU, UK and some of developing world object to main text that ‘failed to meet the moment’ and did not mention fossil fuels

The EU, the UK and allies in the developing world rejected what they called an unacceptable deal on the future of the climate, which failed even to mention fossil fuels, in a dramatic finish to the Cop31 UN talks in Turkey.

The proposed deal “failed to meet the moment”, said Ireland’s environment minister, Darragh O’Brien, speaking for the EU. “We will not endorse a decision that does not respond to the science and that actually risks deepening the chasm between global commitments and the action required. [It] does not even mention fossil fuels.”

As the talks, scheduled to end on Friday, dragged on into Sunday afternoon in the longest climate summit ever, it was clear that the “Antalya pact”, drafted by the Turkish and Australian joint presidencies of the summit, would not achieve the consensus needed to pass.

The Antalya pact invites countries to electrify their economies, calls for rich countries to provide finance to the poor to help them adapt to the impacts of the climate crisis, and calls on countries to minimise the “overshoot” of the 1.5C global heating threshold, of the Paris agreement.

But it failed to endorse the “transition away from fossil fuels” that more than 130 countries demanded, and included controversial provisions on “carbon capture and storage” (CCS) technology that some have said perpetuates fossil fuels.

The presidencies avoided outright collapse of the “conference of the parties” by passing decisions on various related matters, including the “just transition” to help workers in high-carbon industries shift to a low-carbon economy.

But Murat Kurum, Turkey’s Cop president, acknowledged there was no agreement on the draft “cover decision” text and so it had not been adopted.

“We have done our utmost; ultimately, we have not imposed this text on anyone, nor have we issued an ultimatum ⁠such as a ‘take it or leave it’ demand,” he said. “It is clear that there are parties in this hall who oppose this text. We recognise ⁠that there is no consensus on this document. Therefore, these decisions have not been adopted.”

Many countries, including the EU, UK and developing countries including small island states and some of the poorest countries, said they could not support the Antalya pact deal, which was supposed to be main outcome of the Cop.

In a dramatic intervention, O’Brien said on behalf of the EU that it could not accept the final text in its current form. “It fails to meet the moment … We will not endorse a cover decision or an Antalya pact that does not even mention fossil fuels, which is the key driver of climate change.” He said the EU would not accept a decision “that greenwashes fossil fuels”.

His intervention was met by applause from the hall, and was later followed by similar objections from Panama, the Marshall Islands and Norway.

“This feels like a real line in the sand moment. Countries are standing up to fossil fuel interests and saying no,” said Bill Hare, the chief executive of the advisory group Climate Analytics.

But Mohamed Adow, the director of the thinktank Power Shift Africa, who called it “the worst Cop ever”, said: “The EU has rejected this text, but it cannot wash its hands of the wreckage it helped create ... For countries facing failed harvests, destroyed homes and mounting debt, this amounts to abandonment by the wealthy nations most responsible for the crisis.

“Europe’s hypocrisy is staggering. Its governments demand fossil fuel roadmaps from others while expanding fossil fuels themselves.”

Saudi Arabia defended the decision to include CCS in the text. Other countries, including the Africa group, China and Pakistan, also did not object to the deal, despite some having reservations. Pakistan told the conference that just because a few items in the pact were objectionable to some, the rest should not be sacrificed: “Climate action is not a menu from which we can select only what suits us.”

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Ghana also had criticisms of the process but did not reject the final text: “Africa has always chosen to build multilateralism rather than walk away from it.”

Close to 200 countries have been meeting in Antalya for a fortnight under the joint presidencies of Turkey and Australia, with the overrunning talks marked by long periods of deadlock as countries failed to find common ground.

The Cop co-president, Australia’s climate change minister, Chris Bowen, defended his leadership. “Australia left nothing on the field,” he said, but the red lines the presidencies had tried to negotiate were “unbridgeable”.

Asked about the consensus-based system that broke down at the talks, Simon Stiell, the executive secretary of the UN framework convention on climate change, said: “It certainly fell short today,” while emphasising that a significant number of agenda items were passed, with “real progress and concrete results”.

But for many participants, these talks exposed a failure of collective will, particularly of the rich world, to confront the urgency of the climate crisis.

While extreme weather around the world has worsened this year, claiming tens of thousands of lives in heatwaves, in disasters such as the glacier collapse in Nepal, and in droughts and floods worsened by the supercharged El Niño, the slow speed of the climate negotiations appalled even veteran participants.

“There isn’t the sense of urgency to address these issues. That to me is very frustrating,” said Mary Robinson, a former president of Ireland and former UN climate envoy, as the talks neared their scheduled close. “We have co-presidents who haven’t really been stepping up and giving that leadership.”

The UN secretary general, António Guterres, said: “I am deeply disappointed that Cop31 has ended without consensus on an overarching outcome. I share the very profound frustration of all those who expected governments to rise to the moment the climate crisis demands. But this setback does not change the facts. There may be no consensus in the negotiating room. There is no ambiguity in the science.”

#107. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#106. US intelligence chief says he trusts Trump’s description of Putin as ‘my friend’

Jay Clayton was pressed on Trump’s characterization of Putin during media appearances on Sunday

Jay Clayton, the US director of national intelligence, said he trusts Donald Trump “completely” after the US president called Vladimir Putin “a friend” during their recent meeting in China.

Clayton made the remarks on Sunday, where he was pressed during media appearances on Trump’s warm characterization of the Russian president – who is viewed as a chief US adversary – following his trilateral summit with Putin and China’s Xi Jinping. After a backlash at home and aboard, Trump doubled down on Saturday and posted a photo of himself and Putin shaking hands with the caption “My Friend.”

On CNN’s State of the Union, Dana Bash asked Clayton whether he also thinks Putin is a friend to the US.

“The president is the greatest conductor of foreign policy in a very challenging time that we have seen,” said Clayton on Sunday. “Getting the three nuclear powers together and getting them to agree that they are not going to escalate, that they are going to communicate, that is historic. That is remarkable and, in that context, whatever language the president wants to use, I trust him completely.”

Bash pressed on whether Clayton personally agrees that Putin should be considered a friend.

“Dana, in different contexts, you can use different words. The president pulling off that summit, getting the three of them together, is just remarkable,” Clayton replied.

Speaking with ABC’s Jonathan Karl on This Week, Clayton made similar comments, saying that “using the term ‘friend’ is entirely appropriate” in the context of the summit.

“That conversation, that picture – historic, historic picture – was in the context of the three nuclear powers, the three main nuclear powers, getting together and saying, ‘We cannot have a nuclear incident,’” Clayton said on Sunday, referring to Trump’s post. “In that circle of that conversation, using the term ‘friend’ is entirely appropriate.”

Clayton also dodged when asked again whether he thinks Putin should be considered a friend to the United States.

“Is Vladimir Putin America’s friend? He is not America’s friend in Ukraine. Not at all. Not at all,” Clayton responded.

Last week, Trump sidestepped questions about his views on a friendship with Putin.

“I can’t speak to America,” Trump said in a press briefing. “I have fantastic relationships with Putin, that’s a very good thing.”

Trump’s characterization of Putin has sparked backlash from US lawmakers and officials abroad.

“Who is he? He’s the man who has abducted tens of thousands of children, killed tens of thousands of civilians, targeted civilian infrastructure across Ukraine, detained Americans wrongfully, and continues to do so daily,” Senate minority leader Chuck Schumer posted on social media.

“Embarrassing, despicable, and quite frankly pathetic,” Schumer added. “Senate Democrats will always stand with Ukraine and against Putin and autocratic dictators around the world.”

“It’s beyond me,” Senator Jeanne Shaheen said in an interview with MS NOW. “I don’t understand the president’s admiration and cozying up to Vladimir Putin, who is a war criminal.”

Trump’s comments also drew rebukes abroad, including from the deputy leader of the rightwing party Reform UK.

“It’s just wrong at every level … And it’s a grave mistake,” Richard Tice told Sky News. “He is not our friend, Putin. He is, as others have said, a murderous dictator. And no one is in any doubt about that.”

Trump’s description of ​Putin as a friend also drew criticism from European ​allies of Ukraine. “Mr ‌Putin is ​not our ​friend and will never be,” the Polish prime minister Donald Tusk said.

Separately, Clayton also faced questions about the recent spate of technology outages across the US and said Americans should expect more disruptions as companies and government agencies replace aging systems.

“And ​as part of that ongoing upgrade, we are going to have glitches, gaps, ⁠interruptions in service,” Clayton said on CNN’s State of the Union program. “That is ⁠going to happen.”

#104. Mamdani announces plan to close New York's Rikers Island jail by 2032

Perched on a 413 acre (167 hectare) island between Queens and the Bronx, the notorious Rikers Island jail complex earned a variety of nicknames over the years

New York City's official overseeing the closure of the city's most notorious jail hopes a new plan from Mayor Zohran Mamdani will lead to "a moment of transformation" for how people are detained in the largest US city.

Mamdani on Friday unveiled a plan to close the Rikers Island complex by 2032, to build four new jails and drastically reduce the city's detainee population.

Plans to shutter the troubled facility date back to 2019, when the city council first voted to close it by this year. But plans have been delayed over the years in part due to the logistical difficulties over where to place inmates.

Dana Kaplan - who Mamdani appointed as the city's "Close Rikers Czar" told the BBC News channel on Friday that his latest proposal is not just about getting rid of a decrepit facility.

It is also a "generational opportunity" to "build a different type of detention system" and figure out how to detain people more appropriately, she said.

The Rikers Island jail complex is perched on a 413 acre (167 hectare) island between Queens and the Bronx, and has earned a variety of nicknames over the years because of its dilapidated conditions, including Gladiator School and Torture Island. The jail has housed a number of high-profile inmates awaiting trial, including Tupac Shakur, DMX and Harvey Weinstein.

It houses more than 6,500 inmates at a given time. Mamdani hopes to reduce that number to 4,100 - a goal that has been met with skepticism by the New York City correction officers' union.

"[B]uilding a new system that puts an arbitrary cap on the City's available jail beds is ill-conceived, irresponsible and jeopardizes the safety of all New Yorkers, including our Officers," NYC Correction Officers' Benevolent Association said in a statement. "The City cannot keep the jail population at around 4,100 without irresponsibly releasing individuals who committed violent felony charges."

"It's actually possible to have fewer people in jail in New York City - and that is what we should be planning for," she said.

The jail complex has long faced backlash over staffing shortages, crumbling conditions and inmate deaths in the facility, including nine this year. Legislation required the jail to be shuttered by 2027, but Mamdani blamed lags under former Mayor Eric Adams' administration as well as the Covid pandemic for the delays.

Last year, under Adams, a federal judge removed Rikers from the city's control, appointing an independent outside official to make decisions concerning the facility.

In an 88-page report released on Friday, Mamdani said Rikers was a product of "deteriorating infrastructure" and that officials had a "moral imperative" to close it.

New York City Council must approve the updated timeline, with members, including the city's speaker, indicating they support the plan.

Mamdani's office said the city would open up four new jails - one in Brooklyn by 2029, two in the Bronx and Queens by 2031 and one in Manhattan by 2032.

The mayor's office argues the new, smaller facilities will provide inmates "access to daylight, fresh air and clean space" leading to better outcomes for detainees. The facilities will be easier for families to access as well, as travel to the remote Rikers can take hours.

Mamdani said his administration would work to reduce the number of people being held in jail across New York City, including by offering alternative programmes to detention for some awaiting trial, by making it easier for families to post bail online and by trying to speed up the pretrial legal process.

He also wants to open up hundreds of beds in therapeutic housing units for prisoners with serious mental health issues.

Kaplan said Mamdani's administration had already opened 100 therapeutic beds at Bellevue Hospital in Manhattan, moving 100 people off of Rikers Island.

The proposal involves a large investments: $146m (Â£111m) for mental health and substance use treatment and vocational and education programming inside and outside the jails.

#103. Potty-mouthed MPs ranked as Australian parliament sets new swearing record

One serial swearing offender, Jacqui Lambie, says it wouldn’t be an Australian parliament ‘without a few swear words thrown in’

Want to get more charts in your inbox every fortnight? Sign up for The Crunch here

When Liberal MP Garth Hamilton stormed out of parliament in June, slamming his hands on his desk and calling out “fucking bullshit”, the speaker of the house, Milton Dick, called it “a new low” in federal politics.

New analysis from Guardian Australia has found profanity and foul language has risen significantly in Australia’s parliament over the past 20 years, with some of today’s most prominent politicians among the chief culprits for swearing.

Before the year is even out, 2026 is the worst year on record for the use of swear words and language deemed “unparliamentary”. That number has risen by 78% since 2006.

Analysis of Hansard debate and procedure records has found that per million words spoken in parliament this year, approximately 37 have been profane – ranging from milder (“bloody”, “damn” and “crap”) to language considered strong, or even extreme.

While entitled to “parle hard”, language deemed unparliamentary by the speaker or Senate president is required to be withdrawn, or else MPs face ejection from the chamber.

Beyond the impact of pushing against regulation and etiquette, there is something to be said for the currency that outbursts gain when clipped and shared online – recently demonstrated by Greens senator Peter Whish-Wilson, who called for the country to “wake the fuck up” to One Nation’s use of social media algorithms in June.

But despite the politicians reaching for profanities at a record rate, Dick – the Labor MP who has been speaker of the house since 2022 – said he felt the tone and tenor of debate in parliament has gotten better in his time in the chair.

“Debate is fierce and strong and, apart from a few incidents, it has improved,” he said.

“Maybe it hasn’t appeared so because of the volume [of swearing], but when you analyse the words used … there are outbreaks of course, but I believe in everyone showing respect.”

And, to be fair, most of parliament’s most swear-y politicians are in the Senate, not the house.

Described as “the great Australian adjective” in 1894 by the Bulletin magazine, “bloody” appears to have suffered no fall from grace in the subsequent years, serving as the most commonly used swear word in parliament. Along with “damn”, “crap” and “bugger”, mild curses constitute 92% of all recorded bad language, with more severe terms such as “prick”, “shit” and “piss” used more sparingly.

Excluded from this figure are rare cases of colourful and creative terms that have been called for withdrawal. Harnessing the speaker’s unilateral power to flag unacceptable language, terms such as “bin chickens – used by Julian Hill, the MP for Bruce, in 2022 to describe the opposition in the house – have been ruled as unparliamentary.

Much of this potty-talk comes from the Senate, which since 2006 has seen a 177% increase in the use of bad language. Among active parliamentary participants (those who have spoken at least 100,000 words in this period), Bill Heffernan was the lead offender. Despite speaking just 141,000 words between 2006 and 2016, the former Liberal senator had an expletive use-rate of 877 per million words spoken, 43 times the rate of all other parliamentarians.

Jacqui Lambie is the second-most sweary politician, responsible for 6% of total swearing in parliament at a rate of 406 per million words spoken – more than 19 times the average. But, like most of the politicians on this list, Lambie’s swearing is overwhelmingly mild – mostly words like “crap” or “bloody”.

“I try really hard not to swear, especially in the Senate chamber. It upsets my mum, she doesn’t even like me saying ‘bloody’,” Lambie said when we came to her with the statistics.

Our data journalists showcase the most important charts and dataviz from the Guardian and around the web, free every fortnight

“It’s important to be respectful. But I am a straight shooter and sometimes things are just crap – and it’s hard not to say so. I don’t think it would be an Australian parliament without a few swear words thrown in.”

Not all occurrences of swearing in parliamentary records are “own-use” or, rather, swearing with intent. Quotes, such as reading out abuse a politician has received, or a quote from a document, account for some of it.

However, when excluding these cases our analysis shows that own-use swearing in parliament has nearly tripled over the last 20 years, from 6.4 per million words in 2006-9 to 18.9 in 2022-5.

Broken down by party, One Nation are the worst offenders, swearing for the sake of it at 7.4 times the rate of Labor – perhaps unsurprising, given that the party leader, Pauline Hanson, ranks fourth among discursively active politicians for use of foul language.

Dick said he had strived to improve the tone of discussion in parliament, believing that greater diversity in politics and in the chamber – with more women, more First Nations MPs and people from multicultural backgrounds – had also changed dynamics of debate.

Asked about recent heated moments in parliament, he said he recalled only two major moments in the lower house in recent years: Hamilton’s June storm-out and fellow Liberal MP Dan Tehan’s demand to “cut the crap” in August.

“I want to ensure, among our people and our democracy, that we fight hard to disagree more civilly … But I’m a big believer in upholding traditions and standards. When things get a bit heated, I’ll take firm action,” Dick said.

Politicians’ speeches from both houses of parliament were analysed for the years 2006 to 2026. For each speech, the number of swearwords were counted using the these categories:

Mild: bloody, damn, crap, bugger, bastard, bulldust, arse, mongrel, boofhead, bollocks

Strong: prick, shit, bullshit, piss, piss off, dickhead, arsehole, shithouse, wanker

Extreme: fucking, cunt, fuck, fuck off, fucker, fucked, motherfucker

To control for politicians’ tenure length and verbosity affecting swearing totals, swearing numbers were converted to a rate per million words by dividing the total swearwords by the total number of words spoken for that politician in that year, or for the total time period.

To separate swearing in a politician’s own voice from quotes, idioms and literal uses, an LLM was used to read the speech text surrounding each swearword and sort these into categories. A random sample of the results were manually checked, to achieve an agreement of 97% on whether a use was the speaker’s own. Therefore these figures are approximate.

#101. In a year of AI solving maths problems, an Anthropic worker says he may have cracked the biggest

Anthropic just went public with a major IPO this week

An Anthropic employee and mathematician claims to have used the company's artificial intelligence (AI) chatbot to prove the famed Riemann hypothesis.

Levent AlpÃ¶ge said, external on Friday, a day after Anthropic made its US stock market debut, that over the course of about six weeks, he used the Claude chatbot to create a detailed proof of the maths problem.

If found to be correct, the proof would be among the most significant in history. AlpÃ¶ge described it as "one dawn across science of something far greater".

The claim comes as AI firms like OpenAI have in recent months announced scores of solutions to major higher maths problems that have eluded scholars for decades.

As for the Riemann hypothesis proof put forward on Friday, it was found using a non-public version of Anthropic's Claude.

It is a nearly 170-year-old maths problem that deals with prime numbers and is widely considered to be the single most famous problem in maths, in large part due to how complex it is.

Named after the mathematician Bernhard Riemann, the hypothesis puts forward that prime numbers have a determined order along the number line.

Prime numbers are the bedrock of higher maths, leaving breakthroughs related to them to have broad impact.

AlpÃ¶ge said that he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei on Sunday, asking him directly about how he could announce the discovery.

"The precise timing is horrible," AlpÃ¶ge said, referring to Anthropic's public stock listing. "If I could have, I'd have finished this all much earlier."

"I am clearly unilaterally responsible for feeling I just could not live if I waited even longer. There is an obvious cynical view, to do with the timing, but it is false," he added.

The complete textual proof that AlpÃ¶ge put forward in Lean, a computer language used for confirming maths proofs, is over 250,000 pages long.

Terence Tao, a prominent mathematician, said on Friday, external that verification of the proof is still some way off.

"I imagine the digestion process will take some time, though in this case at least we have a human author to communicate with to assist with this task," Tao said.

No such human was obviously a part of major maths proofs made earlier this year using AI.

OpenAI in September claimed that employees using a non-public version of its AI tools were able to solve part of a notoriously difficult problem in just 88 hours. The problem was part of the Navier-Stokes equations, which concern how fluids move.

More than 10,000 AI agents, or computer programmed bots that operate somewhat autonomously, were put to the task as an AI training exercise.

The proof that OpenAI released on Navier-Stokes has yet to be confirmed as accurate, and it set off criticism within academic and higher maths communities and organisations about the use of AI to solve maths problems.

Anthropic has also claimed to have made other progress and solutions in maths, external and physics, external.

A group of maths scholars said, external AI companies boasting of major solutions to show how sophisticated their commercial products had become was "detrimental to the science of mathematics".

"We are witnessing a general threat to intellectual work, with misalignment between the outcome of the use of AI and its initial purpose," the scholars said in a group letter.

A month later, OpenAI released, external what it claimed were solutions, proofs, or major progress on 372 more outstanding higher maths problems.

It will take months, if not longer, for those claims to be reviewed by experts to prove their validity.

#100. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#98. Datacentre bosses met Australian state premiers and ministers almost 120 times in three years, analysis finds

Transparency International says Queensland’s David Crisafulli was ‘by far the most frequently accessed’ state leader, with 13 meetings

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Bosses at 10 datacentre companies secured almost 120 meetings with premiers and ministers in five states over a three-year period, with experts declaring this is “just the tip of the iceberg”.

Analysis by Transparency International Australia argues these meetings, when combined with political donations, demonstrate “unfettered access” to decision-makers that creates an “uneven playing field”.

The report found donations from major datacentre companies and their contracted lobbying firms nearly tripled over the past three years from $685,000 to $1.8m, coinciding with increased investment across Australia.

The analysis found the Queensland leader, David Crisafulli, was “by far the most frequently accessed state premier”, with public diaries showing he has met datacentre executives 13 times in the past three years.

This included three meetings in as many weeks in August and September with Australia’s largest datacentre operator, NextDC – roughly one month before the company’s expansion in Melbourne was halted after its power provider delayed construction of new power poles due to community backlash.

“Those meetings took place shortly before the Queensland premier announced a dedicated framework to fast-track datacentre approvals by taking them out of the hands of local councils,” the Transparency International report said.

Crisafulli has embraced datacentre investment and flew to the United States in September to secure a $31.9bn deal with the AI giant Anthropic for what could become Australia’s largest datacentre. His office was contacted for comment.

During this three-year period, 119 meetings with ministers or premiers were held in New South Wales, Victoria, Tasmania, Queensland and the ACT. These are the only jurisdictions to publicly release ministerial diaries. The meetings were mostly held in 2026.

“These numbers are just the tip of the iceberg,” said Clancy Moore, the chief executive of Transparency International Australia.

“What we don’t know is how many meetings companies like NextDC, OpenAI and Anthropic and their third-party lobbyists are having with the prime minister and ministers in our federal parliament.

“Australia’s lobbying laws also don’t cover in-house lobbyists or government relations staff so there’s very little oversight of their activities too.”

The report also found Australian company Firmus Technologies secured 10 meetings with Tasmanian ministers and the premier this year alone. During this period, the company’s expansion in the state has faced sustained backlash from local communities who feel they were not properly consulted.

The three companies that secured the most meetings with ministers were Amazon Web Services, Macquarie Technology Group and NextDC.

Last month, Guardian Australia reported on political donations from Amazon and Macquarie to both major parties, closely aligning with the announcement of multimillion-dollar government contracts or planning developments. Both companies say these donations were not aimed at influencing policy and that their timing was coincidental.

A shareholder at NextDC’s annual general meeting on Friday said the company had “a lot of lobbying going on in Canberra” but called on the company to better lobby the public on the benefits of datacentres.

The company’s chair, Stephen Flynn, said he was “not sure we are great at lobbying as an industry or as a company” but the world had now changed.

“We thought we were just going along and [being] invisible, in the way if you think about all telecommunications systems … [it] is kind of invisible. We just take it for granted. But we can’t do that. We can’t do that as we go forward.”

The report has also cited examples of four senior public servants at federal government departments quitting to assume leadership positions at OpenAI, AirTrunk and CDC. Three of these appointments were made this year.

In September, the president of the Australian Professional Government Relations Association, Sally Mackenzie, said the scale of engagement between industry and ministers was a sign of the system working as intended.

“What we’re seeing with AI and datacentres is that dynamic playing out at real pace, because the stakes for industry, government and Australians more broadly are so high,” Mackenzie said.

“Lobbying is an important part of the democratic process. It ensures that all stakeholders have the ability to communicate with government.”
