#97. Tech stock sell-off drags markets down as Anthropic shares slide

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic's shares have dropped back towards their initial price after a soaring first day on the stock market, as a wider tech sell-off dragged markets down.

The sell-off comes as concerns remain over the speed and size of artificial intelligence investment, but also as some large companies including Nvidia and SpaceX faced specific issues that worried investors.

Those pressures combined to weigh on US markets through Friday, with all key US indexes closing sharply down on the day, furthering earlier falls over the week.

Both the S&P 500 and the Dow Jones Industrial Average were pushed into correction territory, a point where they are trading at 10% or more down from a recent high.

The S&P 500 closed down 3.7%, the Dow Jones Industrial Average was down 1.9% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.3%. However, all three indexes were up on where they were a year ago.

The speed at which AI investment has been growing - with more than $1 trillion spent in the last year alone by a small handful of major tech companies - has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble. Questions are being raised over whether those investments will pay off, and when.

Investors were also spooked after the Democrats' resounding win in the US midterms in early November, which sparked fears of a reduction in AI development.

Despite a positive first day of trading, Anthropic's shares fell on Friday, dropping at one point by more than 12% to about $85, close to its initial public offering (IPO) price of $83. It closed down 8.5%, reversing half of the gains made on Thursday.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's introduction to the Nasdaq stock exchange on Thursday was a crucial test for the industry and was largely seen as a success, with trading starting at $111.50 before easing to end the day up 17% at $97.17.

Analysts said volatility was to be expected in the first days or even weeks of Anthropic's trading, as the company was the first frontier AI firm to go public so had no direct peers.

Some have also noted the similarities with Elon Musk's SpaceX, which at the time of its debut in June was the largest IPO on record. Shares in the rocket and AI company experienced large swings in its first few weeks of trading.

Ed Moya, a strategist at market intelligence firm AlphaSense, said Anthropic's blockbuster IPO did not appear to have restored confidence in the broader AI market.

While AI demand was still very strong, Moya told the BBC that investors were trying to answer the question of how much were they willing to pay for that growth.

"As we've gone through the past past few weeks, it seems that there's been more risks that have surfaced," he said.

"Right now you're starting to see Wall Street is trying to shift from rewarding growth at any price, to demanding stronger cash flow and a clearer path to profitability."

Shares in some of the largest US tech companies also fell on Friday as the market sell-off continued.

Nvidia shares had plummeted by 13.7% by the close of trade, following Reuters reports - unconfirmed by the BBC - the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission over its accounting practices.

Musk's firms Tesla and SpaceX both experienced falls of more than 7.6%. SpaceX shares were hit after it announced a bond sale to finance what the company said was the largest purchase of Nvidia chips on record.

Meta closed down more than 5% and Amazon was down more than 4%, while Apple and Alphabet experienced smaller falls.

Europe's main stock markets - which contain fewer tech stocks - also took a hit.

Germany's Dax index closed down 2.6%, France's CAC 40 was down 1.9% and the UK's FTSE 100 ended trading 1% lower.

Earlier on Friday, tech-heavy Asian markets saw steep falls.

South Korea's Kospi and Japan's Nikkei 225 indexes closed down about 5%, with shares in memory chip makers Sk Hynix, Kioxia and Tokyo Electron, and other tech firms such as Samsung Electronics, seeing losses.

Taiwan's stock market dropped about 6% on Friday, taking the weekly fall to more than 17% and what was reported to be its biggest weekly fall since 1990.

#95. Cop31 left Chris Bowen stuck between petrostates and climate reality. But the f-word failure is not his alone

Australia’s climate change minister will face a torrent of criticism over his role, but disagreement over fossil fuels proved an unbridgeable sticking point

After delivering a major speech to the UN climate talks in Turkey last week, the Australian prime minister, Anthony Albanese, dismissed criticisms that he hadn’t managed to name the problem that is overwhelmingly driving catastrophic global heating – burning fossil fuels.

“You could pick out any number of things that were not in what was a short speech,” he later said.

If Albanese really thought the head of a leading coal and gas exporter failing to address the issue head-on was inconsequential or trivial, then his climate change minister, Chris Bowen, will have a story for him after the finals days of the fractious talks that became the longest UN climate summit ever.

Those talks failed spectacularly to nail its prime ambition – an “Antalya pact” that was meant to drive investment in renewable energy and electrification, boost finance to help developing countries facing worsening climate damage, and acknowledge the world is bursting through a goal of limiting the increase in global temperatures to 1.5C, with alarming consequences across the planet.

As president of negotiations, Bowen barely slept for three days, and on the final night caught only a few moments of shut-eye on a couch in the office where a parade of negotiators had been coming to sit and talk to him.

Many of them raised a “red line” they said was non-negotiable – that any agreement from the summit must refer to the need for the world to “transition away from fossil fuels” that had previously been agreed at talks in Dubai three years ago. Others, including Saudi Arabia, didn’t want the words “fossil fuels” mentioned at all.

A compromise was proposed in the two final drafts of what was meant to be a “cover decision” – an overarching agreement that all countries would back. Drawn up by Bowen and his verbose Turkish counterpart, Cop31 president Murat Kurum, it suggested countries would “recall” the decision in Dubai “in its entirety” – effectively acknowledge its existence – but not say fossil fuels directly.

The European Union, Panama, the Marshall Islands, Vanuatu, Norway and Belize were among the countries that said no. Ireland’s climate minister, Darragh O’Brien, said the omission was “unconscionable” and the EU could not support it. Climate campaigners agreed.

With the scheduled Friday finish of the summit a distant memory, Bowen left the stage of the plenary hall late on Sunday afternoon when it was clear too many countries could not accept the text that he had helped put on the table.

Speaking to journalists, Bowen said “Australia left nothing on the field” but the red lines he and his staff tried to negotiate were “unbridgeable”. In particular, the way a transition away from fossil fuels was mentioned.

Some countries said they couldn’t support a pact that didn’t mention it, and others “insisted if it was mentioned, they would veto”. “I would have been happy with transition away from fossil fuels being in the text, but that was not going to get support,” Bowen said.

Bowen said despite the breakdown, “in the real world” the transition away from fossil fuels was inevitable and irreversible – and happening at speed. A promised electrification target – that 35% of final energy will come from electricity by 2035, a step that analysis has found could significantly cut fossil fuel use – had the backing of more than 90 countries and was not a deal-breaker.

The climate minister will be presented with a torrent of criticism that the talks failed and he failed. Much domestic news coverage has focused on the cost of Cop31 to Australian taxpayers, rather than the substantive issues being dealt with.

In Antalya, the narrative out of the talks was quite different. Some said the rejection of the pact was a line in the sand that showed the world was no longer willing to accept lukewarm responses on the climate crisis that bent to the whims of fossil fuel interests and petrostates. Some nations are setting up voluntary pacts outside the UN negotiations to try to get the results they need.

Despite some claims in the dramatic final hours, the talks did not actually collapse. Many less controversial elements of the convention’s myriad processes were passed.

But the talks did fail to agree to the Antalya pact that Bowen and Kurum had spent so much political capital on. Some of this failure may be down to the Cop process itself – its reliance on consensus to pass agreements creates plenty of opportunities for countries to block and delay.

Luke Menzel, the chief executive of the Energy Efficiency Council, a membership group focused on electrification in Australia, was at the talks for the full two weeks. He says the reality is “this process has been fracturing for a long time” and had become much tougher after years of wars, rising geopolitical tension and a fuel price crisis caused by conflict in Iran.

“It turns out Antalya is where the conference finally fractured. In many ways it is remarkable we have had as many Cops as we have had in a row that have achieved a consensus,” he says.

Many climate experts and high-profile figures were left questioning whether the Cop process needs major revision. The former US vice-president and long-time climate campaigner Al Gore was among them, arguing it was “an outrage” that “a handful of petrostates have veto power over any mention of fossil fuels” and concluding “it has been clear for years that the Cop process is in need of significant reform”.

Some campaigners sharply criticised Australia’s role. Denise Cauchi, the chief executive of Climate Action Network Australia, said it had “failed to broker an agreement” and “caved” to the hundreds of lobbyists that were pushing for controversial carbon capture and storage technology to be promoted in the pact. Its attempted inclusion was seen as a sop to fossil fuel interests.

But some from Pacific nations, who see the breach of the 1.5C temperature goal as existential and whose interests Bowen had promised to champion, said the failure in Antalya is shared more widely.

In the chaotic final plenary session, the Fijian climate change minister, Lynda Tabuya, addressed Bowen directly as the rest of the world listened.

“You have tried your utmost best, and we have seen it. Your door has always been open,” she said. “This work is hard – sleepless nights, and over hours and days of constant negotiations. This is not easy but we do it and we do it for our people.

“The failure here is not yours to carry. It is the responsibility of all parties.”

#92. French police officers sentenced for beating of music producer Michel Zecler

The three officers were seen on video beating up music producer Michel Zecler in his studio

Three French police officers have been convicted of beating up a black music producer six years ago in Paris in what the court upheld as an aggravated circumstance of racism.

The three were also found guilty of signing a falsified police report stating that the producer - Michel Zecler, 47 - had violently resisted their attempt to arrest him.

Their lies were exposed because the whole scene at Zecler's recording studio was caught on CCTV, revealing the producer had suffered some 30 punches, kicks and baton strikes while offering little resistance at all.

Aurelien Lapersonne, 29, Philippe Tondeux, 50, and Pierre Plantier, 37, all received five year sentences, with different amounts suspended.

Lapersonne had three years of his sentence suspended and Tondeux three and a half years suspended. Plantier had four years suspended, and will serve his one year with an ankle monitor.

All were told they could never serve in the police again.

The case became a cause cÃ©lÃ¨bre for critics of the police, who say it exposed systematic racism among officers. With police again facing criticism for alleged use of excessive force in stopping recent protests, the verdict was hotly awaited by Zecler's supporters.

Much depended in the two-week trial on Zecler's own testimony that the officers called him a racial slur during the beating.

This was denied by the three policemen, who in court admitted the beating and the faked report but denied there was a racial motivation. But the court preferred the evidence of the producer, because - unlike the police - everything else he said was verifiably true.

Because the racist element was accepted by the court, the sentences were toughened.

The case was heard by a panel of five judges.

The incident took place during the Covid lockdown in 2020. Zecler was accused by the officers for not wearing a mask when doing so was obligatory as he approached his studio on foot.

They later claimed his behaviour aroused their suspicions, and they thought he might be carrying drugs. No evidence of this emerged.

After Zecler entered the lobby, the police followed - without a warrant - and tried to bring him outside. Zecler refused to go out, and the beating ensued. Zecler cried for help and some people appeared from the basement and pushed the police outside.

The officers then smashed a glass pane and sent a tear-gas grenade into the building, forcing those inside to come out - after which Zecler was again hit. He was then arrested.

During the trial Zecler said had it not been for the CCTV video, he would have been in court not as a victim but as a potential criminal.

In their last words before the court, all three police officers apologised for what they had done. Tondeux said he was sorry "for the image of the police that I have given".

#88. Molly Meldrum, Countdown host and promoter of Australian music, dies aged 83

Much-loved broadcaster played a critical role in exposing rest of the world to Australian music – and vice versa

Molly Meldrum, the Australian music journalist, broadcaster and producer, has died at the age of 83.

He died peacefully in the early hours of Monday morning at a hospital in Melbourne, his longtime friend and manager Mark Klemens confirmed to the Guardian.

“We have lost a true Australian icon, a man who opened doors for so many and entertained millions of people here and around the world,” Klemens said. “He was unconventional and wonderfully unpredictable, but always caring, always funny and always down to earth, with a passion the rest of us could only envy. He made the most of his life, and boy, was it a full one. Rest in peace, Molly.”

“Affectionately dubbed ‘the Guru’, Molly was the heartbeat, the champion and the spirit of Australian music. He shaped not only the sound of a nation but also the dreams of generations who grew up with his voice guiding them,” his partner Yan Wongngam and family said in a statement, issued by Klemens.

“Yan cared for Molly with great love and devotion, especially through his final years. The family is deeply grateful to him, to Molly’s many loyal friends, and to the staff at Epworth Richmond for their wonderful care.”

The Stetson-wearing Countdown presenter is credited with engineering the breakthrough of Abba, Blondie and Madonna in Australia. During his time on the wildly popular Sunday evening ABC music show from 1974 to 1987, he championed songs including Blondie’s In the Flesh, John Mellencamp’s I Need a Lover, Duran Duran’s Planet Earth and Madonna’s Holiday, transforming then unknown tracks into Top 10 hits in Australia.

Meldrum also passionately promoted Australian performers, giving exposure on Countdown to dozens who went on to local or international fame, including INXS, Kylie Minogue, Skyhooks and Mental as Anything.

Elton John once described Meldrum as the “single most important influence in Australian pop music”.

Meldrum never disguised his unabashed fervour as a fan, even while forming decades-long friendships with an array of global stars.

He developed an obsession with the Beatles after their explosion on to the world stage, and eventually interviewed his pop idol, John Lennon, who fed him the scoop of the band’s impending break-up. But Meldrum did nothing with the incendiary information because he “was so in awe” of Lennon.

Born Ian Meldrum in Orbost, Victoria, to a family he called “quite dysfunctional”, the larger-than-life character moved to live with an aunt and uncle in Shepparton from the age of 12. He went from wanting to be a conductor to becoming entranced with the idea of producing music and later lived with the family of his close friend, the singer Ronnie Burns.

He was one of the first openly gay personalities on Australian television and was known for his bumbling interview technique and for his chaotic and colourful scuffles. On the 40th anniversary of Countdown in 2014, Prince Charles recalled a shambolic 1977 interview with Meldrum, telling the ABC: “There is an old show business saying which warns never to work with animals or children, but nobody prepared me for Molly Meldrum.”

Meldrum began appearing on camera on Countdown in mid-1975, delivering the weekly Rock Report, redubbed Humdrum by a guest host, John Paul Young. While reviewing albums, Meldrum would often hold LPs up awkwardly, making them hard to see under the glare of stage lights. ABC policy prohibited hosts endorsing products, so “do yourself a favour” became Meldrum’s catchphrase.

In the 1980s Meldrum landed various presenting gigs on television: the 1985 Oz for Africa concert, the Australian leg of Live Aid; the ABC interview show The Meldrum Tapes and a regular music segment, Molly’s Melodrama, on the variety show Hey Hey It’s Saturday.

In 2011 Meldrum fell from a ladder in the back yard of his Melbourne home and was placed in intensive care in a critical condition, undergoing surgery for head and spinal injuries. After four months he recovered enough to begin appearing in public again.

In 1986 he was made a member of the Order of Australia, with a citation for “service to the fostering of international relief and to youth”. In 2012 he was inducted into the Logie Hall of Fame. In 2014, when Meldrum became the first non-recording artist to be inducted into the Aria Hall of Fame, Minogue said the Australian music industry was “blessed to have had you in our lives for over four decades”.

Elton John was similarly unreserved in his praise, saying in 2019: “I don’t think anyone has done more for music in Australia than Molly Meldrum.”

Meldrum is survived by his partner, Yan Wongngam, his adopted son and his two younger brothers.

#87. Denmark accuses Russia of plotting to assassinate US-Ukrainian rapper

Denmark has accused Russian intelligence services of plotting to assassinate a Ukrainian-American near Copenhagen, saying they had "planned" and "initiated" the attempt.

Denmark's security and intelligence agency (PET) said it had stopped individuals who were intending to kill the person, named by Danish media as rapper Kyivstoner, at a concert in September. Two people were charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service, and pleaded not guilty.

Denmark's foreign minister said Russia's defence attachÃ© will now be expelled.

Russia's embassy in Denmark called the allegations "unsubstantiated" and said Copenhagen failed to provide evidence of Moscow's involvement.

In a statement, the embassy claimed a "Danish model" had emerged, which it described as a "Russian trace" allegedly being discovered and "exploited for a propaganda spectacle". It accused Denmark of "provocations" intending to "frighten the Danish population in order to justify... a prolonged confrontation with Russia".

Russian state media cite the foreign ministry in Moscow as saying it will respond to the expulsion of its official "accordingly".

In a statement on Saturday, European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said: "I strongly condemn the assassination attempt against a US-Ukrainian citizen in Denmark, orchestrated by Russian intelligence services.

"This was an attack on EU soil. And it is unacceptable," she said, adding that "Russia's hybrid threats are becoming less and less hybrid, and more and more real."

The rapper's real name is Albert Vasiliev.

PET said the individuals who allegedly tried to assassinate him were associated with a banned Danish gang named Loyal to Familia. The group is "known for extensive, serious and violent criminal activity", according to the agency.

PET, which worked with police on the case, said three individuals were stopped while travelling by car near the music venue where Kyivstoner was set to perform on 25 September.

Firearms and knives were found in the vehicle, PET said.

Since then, two people linked to the case have been charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

Both individuals pleaded not guilty to the charges, local media reported.

In July, Russia's FSB security service accused Vasiliev and the Ukrainian authorities of organising a "terrorist attack" on military facilities and personnel in Russia. The rapper denied this allegation on social media.

In a statement on Friday, Danish Foreign Minister Lars LÃ¸kke Rasmussen said the alleged assassination attempt was Russian "intimidation" and an attempt to make Denmark "waver" in its support for Ukraine.

Russia's defence attachÃ© must leave the country within seven days, Rasmussen said.

He also said Denmark wants the EU to restrict the ability of Russians to travel throughout Europe, saying even though Copenhagen was already very reluctant to issue visas, Russians could still get a Schengen visa in another country and travel to Denmark.

Copenhagen wants these visas to apply only in the country that has issued them, he added.

#85. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#83. Deal with EU not dependent on tuition fees agreement, Miliband says

Watch: Miliband spoke to the BBC following the summit in Brussels

A deal agreed between the UK and the European Union on Friday will come into force regardless of whether the two sides can negotiate a separate tuition fees agreement, Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband has told the BBC.

The measures agreed in Brussels include scrapping most checks on British food exports in return for the UK following some EU regulations from 2028, and a new youth mobility scheme.

However, a proposal from the bloc to lower the level of tuition fees paid by EU students in the UK has not been agreed.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she was "confident" a solution would be found, with Prime Minister Andy Burnham adding "me too" during a press conference announcing the deal.

Meanwhile, asked in his interview with the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme whether rejoining the EU would be looked at, Miliband said "all the options will be on the table".

It came after the prime minister said that he would discuss possible ideas for even closer ties with the bloc in the coming months.

What is the EU-UK youth experience scheme and when will it start?

Burnham gets his first taste of EU dealmaking and seems hungry for more

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

UK and EU leaders have hailed the closer cooperation agreement, with Burnham calling the deal a "win-win" that could lower the cost of living and reduce red tape.

The prime minister said the Treasury believes the deal could boost the UK economy by up to Â£17bn a year.

EU sources had earlier suggested that the bloc might hold up applying the wider deal until it was agreed that EU students who wanted to study in the UK would pay the same fees as British students.

Miliband said it was "absolutely clear" the agreements on food exports, energy and youth mobility would apply, even though there has not been a deal on tuition fees.

"President von der Leyen... was very clear about this at the press conference we just had. She said there are those three agreements and then there's a separate piece of paper and a separate issue, which is looking at the issue of tuition," he said.

Asked again whether the agreements would definitely become law, the foreign secretary said: "Yes, absolutely."

"The dates are set out in 2028 when these things are coming in, we're introducing legislation in Parliament, so definitely."

Miliband said there would be "no cost" to the UK taxpayer for anything agreed at Friday's summit, adding that there would be no new contributions to the EU budget.

He said previously announced agreements on EU schemes like Erasmus+ were a "separate decision" and the summit "was not about cost to the taxpayer".

Speaking on Friday, Burnham argued that polling suggests the UK public "want closer ties" with the EU and the deal "delivers that".

Miliband told the BBC that "of course all the options will be on the table" when pressed on whether rejoining the bloc would be considered, adding that "we will look at the benefits and the costs of all the different options".

He said that while he accepted the result of the 2016 Brexit referendum, "the world has changed massively".

"Europe is changing too, so we've got to look at what that means. It's not about this parliament, we're respecting the red lines in this parliament," he added.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the deal was "not fair", arguing that UK farmers would have to follow rules set in Brussels and young people from the EU would "compete" with young Britons struggling to get jobs at home.

She also claimed Burnham had "plucked" the Â£17bn figure out of thin air, and said the only modelling she had seen of how the agreement could boost the economy put the figure at "0.0%".

Reform UK leader Nigel Farage said Friday marked a "betrayal of the British people" and a good day for the EU to "try and humiliate" the UK.

Farage, who was a leading figure in the 2016 campaign to get Britain out of the EU, wrote on social media: "Like every prime minister before him, Burnham is a total fraud."

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey said there needed to be more than "tweaks" to the UK's relationship to the EU.

"A youth mobility scheme is a big step forward, but it's nowhere near enough. We need a whole new deal that actually works for Britain, and fast."

Speaking to BBC Radio 4's Today programme, the University of Manchester's vice chancellor Professor Duncan Ivison said closer UK-EU relations were a "good thing" but "the issue of tuition fees is something we do have to figure out because there is a cost".

He warned that EU students being allowed to pay domestic university fees would "take Â£500m, we estimate, out of the higher education sector at a time where there's a lot of pressure on the sector".

He added: "If EU students come and are paying domestic fees, it's not clear we'd be able to increase the number of places for EU students anyway."

Environment Secretary Dame Angela Eagle said the Treasury and Department for Education would work with universities to find a solution during negotiations.

She told BBC Breakfast: "I don't think anybody wants to do deals that negatively impact anything. We want win-win deals."

#81. Australian politics live: Tehan says Bowen should resign; Taylor pays tribute to ‘kind, gentle’ Meldrum

Liberals demand Bowen resign over Cop ‘disaster’

The shadow energy minister, Dan Tehan, isn’t pulling punches on the failure of the Cop climate summit to reach agreement on a deal.

“Chris Bowen must resign today,” Tehan said in a statement.

double quotation markAs the lead negotiator, Chris Bowen is personally responsible for this catastrophic diplomatic disaster.

The Coalition, and Tehan specifically, have consistently poured scorn on Australia’s initial bid to host the conference, and then on Bowen’s installation as president of negotiations. The failure of the summit to reach a consensus is being heralded by the Coalition as vindication of its doubts.

double quotation markAustralian taxpayers have funded Chris Bowen’s $150m Cop pretensions, only for the so-called president to embarrass Australia on the world stage by making jokes about his penis. Of course, the one thing Chris Bowen has not failed to do is spend taxpayers’ money.

If Chris Bowen does not resign immediately, the prime minister should sack him.

Joseph Daoud, the man who won an auction to play tennis against the prime minister so he could criticise reforms to the capital gains tax is back lobbying against the reforms at Parliament House.

Daoud formed “stop the ambition tax”, a self proclaimed grassroots movement of small biz owners against the CGT changes, which is campaigning out the front of Parliament House today.

Liberal leader Angus Taylor and Nationals leader Matt Canavan are appearing with Daoud out the front of Parliament too.

Canavan makes some claims that the CGT changes are just the start for “socialists” who supposedly want to control everyone. It’s a stretch to say the least … Canavan must have been doing some yoga this morning.

double quotation markThis is not just about taxes, it’s not just about a budget strategy. This is socialism, ladies and gentlemen. That’s what they are. They are socialists. And they want to control you.

The address becomes a bit of a press conference with Taylor then turning his attention to COP.

He says it’s the second “flop” for the government after the failed Voice referendum.

double quotation markThis is the second major flop from this government. This effectively, this climate change cop flop is voiced 2.0. The government has now spent half a billion dollars on two major initiatives that have delivered absolutely nothing, but division and hopelessness for the Australian people.

Tributes are flowing for Molly Meldrum, the Australian music journalist, broadcaster and producer, who has died at the age of 83. He died in the early hours of Monday morning at a hospital in Melbourne.

On social media, the Liberal leader, Angus Taylor, wrote:

double quotation markLike so many, I remember watching Molly on Countdown and Hey Hey it’s Saturday. Donning his cowboy hat, Molly became a beloved and recognisable face on Australian television. Kind, gentle and cheerful, Molly’s qualities beamed into our living rooms.

Vale, an Aussie rock music icon.”

Cop breakdown a ‘comprehensive failure’ by Labor, Greens say

The Greens have joined the pile-on against Labor after the Cop climate conference, at which Chris Bowen was a co-president, failed to reach consensus.

The minor party is pretty mad that the government and the proposed deal failed to mention fossil fuels.

double quotation markThe prime minister went to a global climate conference and delivered a keynote speech, which failed to mention fossil fuels, and that gave comfort and strength to the fossil fuel industry, who ran that narrative for the whole of Cop31.

At the end of Cop31, we have the Albanese Labor government choosing to side with Saudi Arabia and Trump, and not mention fossil fuels, rather than back in Europe and our Pacific members.

Language and dress standards down in parliament, Hume says

The deputy Liberal leader, Jane Hume, says she can see the standards of behaviour declining in parliament, after a report from my Guardian colleagues that found swearing has drastically increased in the building over the last 20 years.

Don’t ‘jump at shadows’: Albanese bats off defence bill criticism

Anthony Albanese has tried to bat off concern over a defence bill that critics warn could have serious implications for democracy.

My colleague Sarah Basford Canales explains the situation here:

The long and the short of it is the government, with the support of the Coalition, will pass a bill that will criminalise photographs of defence sites and give security officials the ability to search and detain people suspected of trying to enter designated zones.

The Greens and civil rights groups warn that could capture peaceful protesters and other civilians standing outside defence zones.

Albanese is dismissive of the criticisms:

double quotation markPeople shouldn’t jump at shadows, and that is what I’ve seen some of the commentary at doing, reaching absurd proportions. It will be accountable, obviously, for things that are designated, but we live in unsafe times.

I find it remarkable in 2026 as a question over whether we need to designate areas as being protected and we live in times where we know there are more conflicts in the world today than at any time since World War II.

Cop breakdown evidence of ‘difficult geopolitical times’: Albanese

The prime minister has been forced to defend Australia’s central involvement in the Cop climate talks that broke down and could not reach consensus.

But he continues to back the involvement, saying it was necessary to remain a key player in the Pacific.

double quotation markClimate change is the first, second, and third priority of Pacific leaders. But there’s a link as well between environmental security and national security. You’ll see some of that played out this week. In order to have credibility, the entry fee is action on climate change.

Private health insurance legislation being negotiated in ‘good faith’

Keeping PBS medications to $25 is likely to be a slam dunk for the government – it’s politically easy policy.

A much trickier battle is ahead though, with the government still searching for support to increase private health insurance rebates for over 65s. The Coalition have said there’s no shot they will support that bill.

The health minister, Mark Butler, says the government is negotiating in good faith with the Greens – who want pensioners carved out.

double quotation markWe’re in negotiations with the Greens party. Have been over the last little while and, yes, we would hope to have that legislation dealt with in the Senate this week.

We conduct negotiations under this prime minister in good faith. We listen to the Senate. We understand we don’t have the numbers in the Senate to pass this bill right now in the form that we introduced it. So, of course, we’re negotiating to see what we can achieve.

Anthony Albanese is back in the country after appearances at international summits in China and the Philippines.

He has called a press conference to announce the government will introduce legislation to keep PBS-listed medication priced at $25 until the end of the decade.

Labor is under significant pressure as the cost of living rises – so the PM is hopeful this will bring some relief to both household purses and his government.

double quotation markToday we will introduce legislation to extend the freeze of the cost of PBS medicines at just $25 until the end of the decade.

This helps Australians now with the cost of medicines but it helps to keep our nation healthier as well, and we understand the cost of essential medicines can put pressure on household budgets and that’s why we’re giving Australians access to cheaper medicines.

Under the former government, when we came to office, PBS co-payments soared, but now medicines would have cost over $50 per script for general patients.

New nest of red imported fire ants found and destroyed in Sydney

The NSW government discovered a new nest of red imported fire ants in Sydney over the weekend, with findings suggesting the nest could be about a year old.

Officials destroyed the colony, found in Auburn in the city’s west, after it was deemed to contained red imported fire ants, a major invasive species that poses threats to both human health and the environment.

A separate nest was discovered two months ago at the Enfield logistics centre, more than 6km from the site found this weekend. The new nest was found outside a biosecurity control zone and officials are investigating if it is a different infestation originating from another source. Detection dogs are set to be on site soon and a new biosecurity control order is being developed.

Tara Moriarty, the NSW minister for agriculture, said:

double quotation markHuman movement of materials is the primary way fire ants spread, which is why we’re asking everyone to stay alert and report anything suspicious.

Everyone has a part to play in biosecurity, and I thank the member of the public for reporting this site in Auburn.

The Coalition’s big problem now is that they’re worried the scheme creates an oversupply of gas in the domestic market by design, which would lower gas prices.

They say that could deter new gas investment or even lead to gas slowly dying off.

Hume says the government has already made key concessions by carving out WA, so she’s hopeful there can be a deal.

double quotation markWe want a gas reservation. We’ve been saying that from the start but it can’t be at any price. The government has moved a long way. I think the carveout of WA was a significant move and a good start. But there’s more that can be done.

We want to make sure that we get the calibration of this right. We can’t afford an oversupply domestically that would have detrimental effects on prices for investors. We need new gas in the system, more gas in the system, more investment in gas.

Jane Hume has not missed an opportunity to stick the knife into Chris Bowen after the UN Cop summit failed to reach a consensus after historically long talks.

Sitting in the hot seat after Murray Watt in the RN Breakfast studio, Hume says Bowen failed overseas, while Australians have also faced rising energy prices.

double quotation markNot only has he failed in these negotiations as the lead negotiator but he’s also failed Australians at the time when he has spent $150m just to be there, just to get this presidency in the first place.

Our energy prices have gone up. Our emissions have flatlined. I think that’s kind of disappointing. He’s really got a job to do at home.

Labor wants gas reservation scheme passed this week

Labor is trying its hand at playing off the Coalition and Greens to get its gas reservation scheme through this week.

The environment minister, Murray Watt, says the Coalition was the party most likely to support the legislation but is now being a bit “intransigent” so Labor could instead make a deal with the Greens.

At face value it looks like a tactic to try to strong-arm the Coalition back to the negotiation table because it’s a policy similar to what they themselves had proposed before the last election under Peter Dutton.

And the government has already made a major concession by carving out WA from the scheme.

double quotation markI think, up until last week, we certainly felt that the Coalition was the more likely party but we’re seeing the same kind of intransigence from them that we saw last year with the EPBC reforms. But this week is an important week and this is the week that the Coalition can decide if they support cheaper gas. That’s certainly the intention.

I think it’s also worth noting one other group that has evaded scrutiny on this is Pauline Hanson’s One Nation party. They have traditionally supported a gas reservation but, mysteriously, shortly after Gina Rinehart, who’s a big gas producer, has supported their party, they’ve flipped their position.

#77. Man convicted of starting Palisades fire in Los Angeles

Homes along Los Angeles' Sunset Boulevard could be seen engulfed in flames during the fires of January 2025

A jury in Los Angeles has found a 30-year-old man guilty of starting one of the most destructive wildfires in California's history.

Jonathan Rinderknecht was convicted of arson after starting the fires that swept through Pacific Palisades and parts of Malibu in January last year, killing 12 people and destroying more than 6,800 buildings in the US state.

The conviction was handed down nearly five months after deadlocked jurors led to the judge declaring a mistrial.

Rinderknecht, who had pleaded not guilty, was convicted of two federal charges. He is due to be sentenced on 17 March when he faces between five and 20 years in prison on each charge.

Jonathan Rinderknecht's arrest in October last year

Rinderknecht was found guilty of lighting a fire on 1 January last year which burned undetected deep in root systems before flaring back up six days later.

One of the charges was for starting the original brush fire, while the other centred on the damage caused when it reignited into a catastrophic blaze.

"We were determined to hold him accountable, and today's guilty verdict delivers a measure of justice for the families who lost loved ones and the thousands whose lives were upended," said Bill Essayli, who leads the US Attorney's Office for the Central District of California.

Rinderknecht is a Los Angeles resident and former ride-share delivery driver.

Defence attorney Steve Haney had argued at trial that prosecutors lacked sufficient physical evidence or records to prove their case.

Lawyers for the defendant said they were "disappointed" by the verdict but respected the jury's decision.

Los Angeles Mayor Karen Bass, who was widely criticised for her handling of the fires, said in a statement that "no verdict can bring back the lives lost or erase the pain families have endured".

"So many are still struggling to rebuild," she said. "For those still displaced, the devastation remains a daily reality."

Prosecutors had argued that Rinderknecht was driven by revenge, anger and loneliness because he had nowhere to go for the holiday.

They cited geolocation data from his iPhone, security camera footage that showed his car's location and witness testimony from passengers.

They alleged that Rinderknecht had a "deeply entrenched belief that the wealthy were destroying the world" and in turn purposely started the blaze in the affluent neighbourhood.

The fire was one of several that ignited back-to-back across the LA region in January, which in total left 31 people dead and destroyed 16,000 structures.

#76. Nasdaq erases all 2026 gains as AI sell-off dampens Anthropic’s second day of trading

The Nasdaq lost trillions in value, with the AI startup’s IPO failing to buoy it as investors question huge sums being poured into AI buildout

Who are Anthropic’s seven co-founders, now worth $180bn?

As Anthropic’s stock dropped during its second day of trading, the wider stock market dipped with it. On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite erased all its gains for 2026 and closed down 5% from its value on 1 January. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 closed in correction territory.

Anthropic’s record-breaking IPO failed to stem a global sell-off in AI-linked companies, and the tech-heavy Nasdaq erased some $4tn in market value this week, according to the Wall Street Journal.

Shares in the frontier AI company behind the Claude chatbot, which floated in the US on Thursday, fell by 9% on Friday, ceding most of the gains from the biggest initial public offering in history. They dropped to $88.30, towards the $83 level at which Anthropic priced its stock market debut.

Anthropic’s first two days as a listed company have been volatile. Its shares jumped by 17% on Thursday, after briefly surging by almost 40% when trading in the firm began.

US technology stocks followed Anthropic down on Friday, with the memory chipmaker SanDisk falling 14% and rival Micron down 12%. Nvidia fell 14%, eliminating hundreds of billions of dollars in market cap.

Neil Wilson, a UK investor strategist at Saxo, said there was “just relentless selling in tech”. He warned: “Anthropic shares tumbled to take an $85 handle as the post-IPO euphoria gives way to the reality of a steep sell-off across the tech and AI space that shows no sign of easing just yet.”

Global markets slumped on Friday too; Asian stock markets recorded their worst week since 2020, as shares in big chip companies fell. In Taiwan, stocks dropped 6.7% to hit their lowest point since mid-April, with the chip maker TSMC down about 6%.

The scale of the decline in Taiwan prompted the chair of its stock exchange to pledge to ensure the stable operation ⁠of capital markets, and reassure investors they should remain confident in the fundamentals of Taiwanese companies.

In Japan, the Nikkei index fell 5%, while South Korea’s tech-heavy Kospi index lost 5.5%. Shares in the tech investor SoftBank had their worst day in more than two years, down 14.5% in Tokyo, amid worries around its aggressive bets on AI.

European stock markets posted their biggest weekly loss since March 2025, with the pan-European Stoxx 600 dropping 1.9% on Friday. London’s blue-chip stock index hit a five-month low.

The sell-off followed losses on Wall Street on Thursday, where the main indices all fell by more than 2% on a day dominated by Anthropic’s IPO.

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said Anthropic could be “draining market liquidity”, as investors sell shares in other companies to raise funds to pay for stock in the newly floated AI firm.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

“The slide from yesterday’s highs sends a message that there might be more at work here than investors realise, and while brokers are scrambling to buy more, that liquidity has to come from somewhere, and the other tech names may be the first ones to feel the pressure,” Beauchamp said.

Anthropic’s flotation has failed to ease worries around the AI trade, as investors increasingly question whether companies can deliver the returns necessary to justify the billions of dollars being poured into the AI buildout.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior market analyst at Capital.com, said: “The IPO demonstrated that demand for AI exposure remains strong, but it has not been enough to overcome concerns about elevated valuations, profitability and the sustainability of investment spending.

“The broader market mood has shifted from buying the dip towards questioning whether the correction has further to run. The weakness in equal-weighted indices suggests that selling pressure is no longer confined to a handful of technology giants, while Europe’s recent outperformance has also faded.”

Nvidia shares fell byafter California served the chip company with a subpoena as part of an investigation into whether its advanced semiconductors ended up in China.

Britain’s FTSE 100 share index dropped by 5.1% this week, its biggest weekly fall since the start of the Iran war.

Fears that US central bankers may raise interest rates to fight inflation have lifted the dollar to a 19-month high, and pushed down the gold price – with gold on track for its worst week since March.

Bitcoin hit its lowest level in four months, dropping below the $60,000 point for the first time since early July.

#75. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#72. Zelensky says Crimean Bridge strikes 'fair response' as he meets UK's Streeting

Wes Streeting told Volodymyr Zelensky he was in Kyiv to show that Europe was united in its support for Ukraine

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has justified Kyiv's repeated attacks on a critical bridge linking Russia to occupied Crimea, calling it a "fair response" to 109 separate Russian attacks on bridges in Ukraine.

In comments to the BBC in Kyiv, Zelensky said the intense attacks on Ukraine by Russia that have followed the strikes on the Crimean Bridge were only a "signal that Vladimir Putin does not want to stop. He wants a reason to escalate".

He rejected the claim by the Russian president that the bridge - which links Russia to the peninsula it illegally annexed from Ukraine - was "exclusively civilian" in use.

"There are facts, not just the words of Putin. We can see," he said.

The Ukrainian president pointed to images online of tanks and other Russian military hardware using the route from Russia into occupied Crimea as a supply line and for logistics.

"If Putin used the internet, he could see that."

The Ukrainian president added that Russia had carried out attacks with missiles and drones on bridges in Ukraine.

This week's combined missile-and-drone strikes by Ukrainian forces destroyed the central road section of the Crimean Bridge, collapsing part of it together with one of the two support arches into the sea and rendering the structure unusable for road traffic.

Russian attacks on Ukraine continued on Friday evening and overnight: a seven-year-old boy was killed in missile and drone strikes on Zelensky's hometown of Kryvyi Rih in the central Dnipropetrovsk region. Local officials say he had been in a playground at the time.

A 20-year-old female medic was also killed. A local official added that when rescuers rushed to the scene, the same site was targeted again with jet powered drones. Another 58 people were injured in the city.

Ukraine's state emergency service DSNS said 11 people were also injured in the north-eastern Kharkiv region, and one person in Rivne, in the north-west.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Footage shows Ukrainian missile approach Crimean Bridge

Zelensky was speaking ahead of a meeting in Kyiv with UK Defence Secretary Wes Streeting, who told the Ukrainian president he was here to show that Europe was united in its support.

"The more Putin tries to divide us, the more resolute we become," Streeting said.

He said that Russian missiles hitting Poland on Friday, during military exercises of the so-called Coalition of the Willing, was an "outrageous provocation".

The timing, Streeting said, seemed "more than a coincidence".

Asked by the BBC about Nato's response, he said the best way was to "do more for Ukraine" to defend itself against Putin who he called a "cold-blooded killer".

Streeting's description of Putin chimed with the comments made earlier by Zelensky, who described the Kremlin leader a "killer". This was done in response to US President Donald Trump referring this week to Putin as his "friend".

Streeting's visit to Kyiv came after European leaders meeting in Berlin on Thursday alongside US envoys said they were ready for negotiations with Moscow on ending the war in Ukraine.

"Europe has a clear and consistent and united negotiating position," UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham said, while German Chancellor Friedrich Merz stressed that Moscow's "maximalist demands are not realistic".

Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner, one of the two US envoys who were in Berlin, said the US president - who had also dialled into the meeting from Air Force One - wanted to make a deal with Moscow "with Europe by his side".

Russia's Vladimir Putin has so far rejected any European role in the US-led peace talks - but earlier this week he invited European representatives to Moscow as it wanted to clarify "what Europe will bring to these talks".

That meeting in the German capital came hours after Putin had held historic trilateral talks in Shenzhen, China, with Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping.

Zelensky said he had been "sceptical" ahead of the latest round of talks, when US envoys claimed that Russia was prepared to make compromises to end the war.

"I thought [Putin] would find reason to escalate."

Speaking to the BBC, Streeting added: "Putin has absolutely no business playing the victim. He is the aggressor in this war. He provoked an illegal invasion of Ukraine. And all of us, right throughout the world, need to judge Putin not on his words, but on his actions."

In a separate development on Saturday, Finland said it "detected a drone strike on a Russian merchant vessel" in the eastern Gulf of Finland, "in an international maritime area".

The Finnish defence ministry said in a statement that the vessel was damaged, without giving any further details.

It added that "temporary restrictions on aviation and maritime areas in the eastern Gulf of Finland" were now reinstated for safety reasons.

Russia has not publicly commented on the issue.

#70. Trump threatens ‘tremendous penalties’ for US tech firms that build datacenters abroad

President gives wide-ranging White House press conference and says he has ‘fantastic relationships’ with Putin and Xi

Donald Trump threatened on Friday to impose “tremendous penalties” on American tech companies that build datacenters outside the US, though he gave no details and told reporters he had not yet informed his own officials about the plan.

In a discursive press conference from the White House briefing room, Trump also said he would “look into” the report that Iran had been planning to possibly detonate a “dirty bomb” in Washington DC, and backed JD Vance over comments the vice-president made blaming Republican midterm losses on the Iran war.

Trump also expounded on his friendship with the Russian president Vladimir Putin, whom he met alongside China’s Xi Jinping at the Apec summit on Thursday, adding he had “fantastic relationships” with both autocratic leaders.

At the White House, Trump told reporters he wanted datacenters built by US companies to stay on US soil.

“We’re not allowing datacenters to go into other countries, because China will take all of them, and we’re not allowing that to happen. And we’re going to put tremendous penalties on companies that try to do it,” he said.

He said Americans should welcome datacenters, and urged communities to extract concessions from developers, repeating a point he made before the midterms.

“You ought to take advantage of the datacenters. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but don’t shut them off,” he said.

With many Americans turning against the datacenters being built near their homes, the US tech industry is making a show of conciliation by promising to take fewer government handouts, to be more transparent, and to give more money to towns and cities willing to allow server warehouses to be built.

The Trump administration on Monday issued new rules to restrict remote access to advanced US computing chips and servers, in a move it said was designed to limit China’s ability to use US chips to train its artificial intelligence models – though a House committee said on Wednesday the US had signed off on sales of nearly 900,000 AI chips to China in six months.

China’s ability to obtain advanced AI chips made by the US firm Nvidia, in which Trump is an investor, has been spotlighted in recent weeks after its CEO, Jensen Huang, was fired over his affair with a woman alleged to be spying for China.

In the same press conference, Trump was asked about reports Iran was planning to potentially detonate an explosive laced with nuclear material – a so-called “dirty bomb” – in Washington DC. The president called it a “rumour” and said: “If they do, we’re going to fight.”

He added: “We know a lot about what’s happening in Iran. We know everything they say. We know who they are, where they are. We’ll find out about it.”

Responding to a question about reports of a rift with Vance, Trump said the vice-president’s remark blaming the Republican collapse on the Iran war was “a very accurate statement”.

“I didn’t say I was unhappy with it,” the president said. “I would have added one other thing if it were asked … would you do it again? And absolutely, the answer is yes, because we cannot allow Iran to have a nuclear weapon.”

Trump was asked whether Vladimir Putin could really be considered a friend to America since Russia is a close ally of Iran.

“I can’t speak to America,” he said, adding: “I consider [that] I have fantastic relationships with Putin. That’s a very good thing. I have fantastic relationships with President Xi. That’s a good thing, and our country is safe because of that.”

He praised Putin as he repeated his grievances over the Department of Justice’s investigation into the Russian attempts to influence US voters ahead of the 2016 presidential election.

“I’ve been dealing with Putin … [since] the fake Russia, Russia, Russia hoax,” he said.

Trump also spoke about two prominent Democrats. He said Gina Hinojosa, who won the race for Texas governor over Trump’s Republican ally Greg Abbott, called him. “She was really nice,” Trump said. “It was a very productive call. And she wants to have a strong border, and I think we’ll be able to work something out.”

And he said Hakeem Jeffries – who Trump previously called a “thug” but is now in line to be the next speaker of the House, and the first Black speaker – is “a smart guy. He’s a streetwise guy.”

#67. Lock of former US President George Washington's hair sells for $4,000

George Washington died in 1799 after serving for two terms as America's first president

A framed lock of hair from America's first president, George Washington, has been sold at auction for $4,000 (Â£3,058).

The hair once belonged to John Gadsby, who entertained Washington in 1799 just before the latter's death the same year, and was later obtained by a collector called John K Lattimer from a descendent of Gadsby's, according to Richmond Auctions, which sold the item.

Saturday's sale is not the most expensive for Washington hair at auction. Three strands thought to have been removed from him close to or after his death stored in a locket sold for nearly $40,000 in 2021.

Gadsby, a resident of what in 1799 was the town of Alexandria in Virginia, entertained Washington for dinner shortly before his death later that year, according to a testament given in 1928 by Gadsby's granddaughter, Julia Ten Eyck McBlair, and which was posted on the Richmond Auctions website.

McBlair stated that her grandfather came into possession of Washington's hair "in view of the friendship and association with his last days" and had it framed and hung "under General Washington's portrait in our home in Washington City, there I have seen it all my life".

Gadsby hosted Washington's two birthday celebrations in 1798 and 1799, Richmond Auctions states.

Washington led American forces to victory against the British in the War of Independence and helped draft the US Constitution before becoming the country's first president in 1788. He stepped down in 1797 after serving two terms.

Several locks of Washington's hair have been preserved, including at least 50 examples that are in a collection at his former residence, Mount Vernon, which is now a museum.

"Public displays of hair from friends or loved ones peaked in the 19th century, but had long been popular among affluent families like the Washingtons," a blog on the Mount Vernon website states.

As well as lockets of his hair, other items associated with Washington have also done well at auction.

In July, a signed letter by Washington from 1776, in which he asks for soldiers for the Continental Army to fight the British, sold for $109,800.

#64. China commits to low-carbon electrification and help for others to improve

Exclusive: Countries at Cop31 are being urged to commit to deriving 35% of their energy from electricity by 2035

China is pushing for rapid electrification of its economy using low-carbon energy and will help other countries do the same, a senior member of the Chinese delegation at the Cop31 UN climate summit has said.

Wang Yi, a professor of energy and environment policy at the Chinese Academy of Sciences and a senior adviser to China’s government, said: “End-use electrification is a very clear direction. We can follow this direction while making the source of electricity clean. That’s the way to go forward.”

China is the biggest emitter of greenhouse gases by far, producing more than a quarter of the world’s total, so its push for electrification is significant.

At Cop31, countries are being asked to sign up to a target of deriving 35% of final energy consumption from electricity by 2035. Australia, the president of the negotiations, and the host nation Turkey have pushed the target and on Friday night Australia and China signed a joint statement endorsing the “35:35” goal.

But in the second draft text of the probable outcome from Cop31, released on Thursday, the electrification pledge was downgraded to one of a suite of voluntary options countries can take up to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions.

In an interview with the Guardian at the talks, Wang said: “China will do our best to realise clean end-use electrification, and also help other countries to improve. China has significantly reduced the cost of clean technology and products and we would like to share our experience with other countries.”

Experts have said electrification is the next vital step in the shift to a low-carbon economy, as the only way to diminish the close to 80% of fossil fuels still used for primary energy, mostly for transport, heating and industry.

While civil society organisations want any commitment made on electrification to specify that it must be met from clean sources, a pledge that leaves the choice to countries is likely to have more widespread support, as many – including China and India – are resistant to anything they feel smacks of “top down” interference in national plans.

But campaigners argue that allowing individual countries to decide risks leaving the door open to more gas-fired power plants. Some compromise language is also possible that would urge countries to decarbonise their power.

Last year, electricity made up nearly 30% of China’s energy use and it is on track to make that 35% by 2030. Electricity is several times more efficient than fossil fuels, meaning that it takes far less energy to run electric vehicles, heat pumps and industrial processes than their fossil fuel counterparts.

Wang said China was happy with the 2035 target, but that it would be harder to meet if the electricity was required to come entirely from low-carbon sources. “For this target, no problem. But if you think about ensuring clean electrification, that may be a little bit difficult, even for China. For other countries, it’s even more difficult,” he said.

China still has a large number of coal-fired power stations and is planning some new ones, though fewer than before. Wang said this would be used for back-up during the transition to clean energy.

One of the key issues at Cop31 is the “transition away from fossil fuels”, which was one of the resolutions passed at the Cop28 summit in Dubai in 2023, but which countries have failed to flesh out since. The phrase was included in the first draft of the “cover decision” outcome from Cop31, presented on Monday, but excised from the second draftissued on Thursday, to the dismay of many countries and campaigners.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Wang said: “We agree with paragraph 28 of the UAE consensus [the transition resolution]. But maybe different countries have different roadmaps. It’s very difficult to have only one unified roadmap for every country. So maybe we will help other countries to have their own country-based solutions, to realise their own national way of establishing a new renewable-based power system.”

China is also concerned about trade barriers to its clean technology exports, including the tariffs that the US under Donald Trump has placed on Chinese goods, and the “carbon border adjustment mechanism” (CBAM) imposed by the EU. The CBAM penalises imports from countries with looser regulations on carbon than the EU, which forces companies in high-carbon sectors to pay for their carbon emissions in order to level the playing field.

Some smaller developing countries have criticised the EU for its CBAM, warning that it will hamper their manufacturing industries. The EU said it had processes in place to ensure smaller developing countries were not hurt.

Wang called for more work on trade issues at Cops. “They must come together to find a unified approach,” he said. “China and other developing countries, including the global south, disagree [with the EU] on this. But that’s why we should continue to strengthen our dialogue.”

Jiangwen Guo, a senior research fellow at the Chatham House thinktank, said other countries could learn from China. “China has very consistent top-down policies [and] sets a clear direction with high consistency, then follows with learning by doing,” she said. “In the great energy transition, there is no one ahead of China. China is creating the model for the globe.”

Negotiators at Cop31, which was scheduled to end at 6pm on Friday, were sent home late on Friday after the talks stalled, with a new draft text of a “cover decision” marking the main outcome of the negotiations not thought likely to emerge until some time on Saturday morning. The talks are likely to continue into Saturday and potentially beyond.

#62. FBI reportedly investigating apparent hack of its criminal database

The FBI is investigating an apparent breach of its criminal information database, US media reports, citing two people briefed on the issue.

The National Crime Information Center (NCIC) database is used by federal and local law enforcement to share information on warrants and background checks.

It would not be the first time hackers have targeted the FBI, the domestic intelligence and security service of the US. The agency did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.

When asked about the breach in a Fox News interview on Saturday, President Donald Trump said he had "just heard" about the hack. "I will say that it's not good. I don't like that," he said.

"But maybe we'll learn things that we would've never learned otherwise," Trump added.

This apparent breach of FBI database comes after a string of tech outages which hit banking, air traffic control and other sectors in the US last week. Officials have said they were not caused by cyber-attacks, but the blackouts have sparked scrutiny from lawmakers.

White House National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross was fired this week, with Trump saying he was not good enough.

Dan Lorhmann, a former analyst for the National Security Agency (NSA), told the BBC News Channel that the NCIC's data included "critical information relating to nationwide criminal activity" and needed to be protected.

He added that while US security agencies including the FBI have "an amazing ability" to keep such information safe" and are "globally respected", the level of cyber-attacks is rising globally.

"There's a lot of different reasons why that's happening but I do have faith in our experts in government," Lorhmann said.

In September, the FBI said it was investigating an apparent breach by a cyber-crime group ShinyHunters, which claimed it had stolen sensitive information on all bureau staff - around 38,000 people.

After that hack, one former FBI agent told the BBC he feared for the safety of current employees, whose personal information could soon become available online to criminals and hostile nation-state hackers.

In a separate development, NBC reported an apparent cyberattack against the US Department of Agriculture (USDA).

Employees were informed on Thursday that its farm payment system had been targeted, NBC said.

The USDA has not publicly commented on the issue.
