#59. Fire at confectionery factory in Indonesia leaves at least three dead and dozens missing

Relatives of the missing victims flocked to the factory’s entrance, seeking updates about their loved ones

A fire at a confectionery factory west of Jakarta has killed three people with 36 remaining missing, an Indonesian rescue official says.

Drone footage showed large parts of the factory destroyed, with the roof collapsed in places and smoke still rising from the complex. Photos and videos shared by rescuers show officers entering the charred building in search of the missing victims on Sunday, with small fires still burning in some spots.

Relatives of the missing victims flocked near the factory’s entrance, seeking updates about their loved ones, footage aired by local broadcasters showed.

The Fonusa Agung Mulia factory, located in Cikande in Indonesia’s Banten province about 50km from the capital, caught fire on Saturday afternoon, search and rescue official Rizky Dwianto said on Sunday.

“All elements of the joint SAR [search-and-rescue] team are making maximum effort to locate the victims while prioritising the safety of personnel in the field,” he added.

Rizky said four people were injured and taken to a nearby hospital and health clinic.

Firefighters were alerted about 4.30pm on Saturday and found the building already engulfed in flames when they arrived, fire official Tubagus Machfudz told local broadcaster Kompas TV.

“When we arrived … a large area was already on fire. So we could not determine whether the company’s protection system was functioning properly or not,” he said.

The Banten province governor Andra Soni told reporters overnight that an estimated 60 people were inside the factory when it caught fire.

The cause of the incident is still under investigation, the local disaster agency said in an Instagram post on Sunday.

Deadly fires are not uncommon in Indonesia.

In December last year, a blaze that was likely caused by a drone battery explosion tore through a seven-storey building in Jakarta and killed at least 22 people, police said.

In 2023, at least 13 people were killed in the country’s east after an explosion at a nickel-processing plant.

#58. Italian jeweller and businessman Paolo Bulgari dies aged 89

Italian jeweller Paolo Bulgari, who helped turn his family's business into a global luxury brand, has died in Rome aged age 89.

His family announced the news, remembering him as "the greatest jeweller of our time", according to Italian media.

Paolo Bulgari was Chairman of the Bvlgari Group since 1984 and its jewellery pieces have adorned Hollywood stars and royalty including Diana, Princess of Wales.

The company Bvlgari posted an online tribute to the "true creative soul", whose work would "continue to inspire generations to come".

The firm, Bulgari, was set up by his grandfather, Greek silversmith Sotirios Voulgaris, in Rome in the late 19th century - who later adapted his name to Bulgari in Italian.

Originally from Paramythia, a mountain village in the Epirus region of northwestern Greece renowned for its silver jewellery, Voulgaris fled Ottoman rule and settled in Naples before opening a shop in Rome.

Paolo Bulgari joined the company shortly after leaving school, becoming an expert in gemstones - eventually leading the business and overseeing international growth.

The Bulgari family favoured bold gemstones, rich yellow gold, generous proportions and unexpected colour combinations to set them apart from the delicate asethetic associated with Parisian jewellery houses.

The business broke into luxury markets and diversified beyond jewellery into watches, perfumes, leather goods and hotels.

"Paolo Bulgari began collaborating with his father in 1960, specialising in the knowledge of precious stones, of which he was one of the world's leading experts, and refining the sensibility and aesthetic sense that are at the root of the Bvlgari style," the Bvlgari company posted on social media., external

"He poured his heart into his craft and into nurturing the talents of young designers, leaving an indelible mark on the world of jewellery.

"Thank you, Mr. Paolo, your legacy as a brilliant beacon of creative inspiration will continue to inspire generations to come."

Paolo Bulgari alongside David Bowie and his wife Iman in September 1991 in Versailles, France.

The post included a quote from Paolo Bulgari: "Everything can be a source of inspiration. There are no rules in creativity. Never."

The designer brand's celebrity clients have included Elizabeth Taylor, Audrey Hepburn, Marilyn Monroe, Ingrid Bergman, Sophie Loren, Anne Hathaway, Zendaya and Rihanna.

Actor Zendaya wearing a Bvlgari necklace at the 2025 Golden Globe awards.

In 2011 the family sold its controlling stake to the French luxury group LVMH, in a deal worth about 3.7 billion euros.

In 2019, he and his wife established the Paolo Bulgari Foundation to support young people through schooling and job training in disadvantaged neighbourhoods on the outskirts of Rome.

The funeral will take place in Rome on Monday, 23 November in the Basilica of Santa Croce in Gerusalemme.

#54. South Korean minister quits after AI cheating scandal engulfs university exams

Choi Kyo-jin says he ‘failed to prepare adequately’ for entrance exams following last-minute scramble to tighten measures

South Korea’s education minister has resigned after an AI-assisted cheating scandal that erupted in the lead-up to key university entrance exams last week.

The country has been gripped by fears of AI-assisted exam cheating since last Sunday, when the top scorer in a national tax accountancy exam confessed to using a miniature camera and smartphone to achieve a near-perfect score, just four days before the crucial university entrance exam, known as the suneung.

The cheat’s confession sent officials scrambling to roll out a slew of preventive measures for the university entrance exams, including blocking public wifi in testing areas.

The unprecedented crackdown included doubling the number of supervisors and sending emergency alerts to detect any smartphones illegally smuggled into the exam centres.

Students were also told to turn up with pencils only. Erasers were provided by the test centres, as authorities tried to reduce the chances of concealed digital devices disguised as stationery.

In addition, exam-takers were asked to avoid wearing clothes with buttons. Those who failed to comply were told they would have stickers placed over the buttons to prevent the use of hidden cameras.

The government promised a reward of at least $75,000 (£57,300) for anyone reporting cheating or misconduct, with reduced penalties for those who turned in their accomplices.

But the education minister, Choi Kyo-jin, struggled to reassure the public, and teachers’ unions criticised the government response as belated.

“The exam concluded without any major incidents … However, as the minister in charge, I do not believe I am immune from public criticism that we failed to prepare adequately in advance,” Choi wrote on X. “Now that the exam is over, I take full responsibility and hereby offer my resignation.”

The president, Lee Jae Myung, accepted his resignation, the presidential office said.

The annual, eight-hour suneung determines which university a candidate can attend and, in turn, their future prospects. It is widely seen as a ticket to prestigious universities, well-paid jobs and even better marriage prospects in the highly competitive country.

Asked during a parliamentary hearing on Monday whether he was confident that authorities could catch would-be cheaters on exam day, Choi, 72, replied: “Due to my age, I am not confident I could.” His response quickly drew a public backlash.

Choi did not appear at press conferences in the days leading up to Thursday’s exam, and a vice-minister instead announced the preventive measures.

The person who cheated in the national tax accountancy exam said in a video posted to his lawyer’s YouTube account that he had photographed the questions with a concealed microcamera, sent them to an AI system and read the answers from a smartphone. “I cheated in the tax accountant exam,” he said. “My top placing is not the result of legitimate effort.”

The scandal has rattled a ‌country where standardised tests largely dictate social mobility and career trajectories.

South Korea goes to great lengths to ensure the smooth running of the suneung. Aircraft takeoffs and landings are rescheduled during the 25-minute English listening paper to minimise noise, while artillery fire and tank movements are suspended. The stock market opens an hour late and cars are stopped 200 metres from every centre.

In the aftermath of the cheating scandal, the government announced that smart devices would have to be turned off and handed in for the suneung, while bags left in corridors would be checked for any devices that were still on. Exam officials also inspected all candidates’ glasses and watches, as well as their collars and sleeves for concealed cameras.

The government also sent emergency alerts to every phone in the country, which sounded at full volume and could not be silenced, in the hope of catching out anyone who had not handed in their device.

Officials said 111 cases of misconduct involving electronic devices were identified during Thursday’s exam, nearly half of which were detected through the emergency alerts.

A high-school student had filed a constitutional petition challenging the government’s decision to go ahead with the exam as planned. But a court rejected the request, citing insufficient evidence of irreparable harm.

Under the tightened measures, students caught cheating can be barred from retaking the exam for 10 years and face criminal prosecution.

#52. Mexican drug lord on FBI's 10 most wanted list arrested in shoot-out

Watch: Suspected drug lord 'El Chapo Isidro' transferred to Mexico's capital

A Mexican drug lord who was on the FBI's 10 most wanted list has been arrested following a shoot-out with Mexican authorities.

Fausto Isidro "Chapo Isidro" Meza Flores and seven others were detained by Mexican special forces in Guasave, Sinaloa on Friday, Mexico's defence secretariat said.

Seven suspects were killed in the operation, as were four members of the Mexican army who were travelling to reinforce security in Guasave, the secretariat added.

Meza Flores was wanted in Mexico on organised crime charges, while he has been accused of "flooding the US with fentanyl, cocaine, heroin and other deadly drugs" by the FBI. The US is seeking his extradition.

Mexico has stepped up efforts to tackle drug-related crime after coming under pressure from US President Donald Trump since he returned to the White House.

Meza Flores, the leader of the Meza Flores Organisation, also known as the Guasave Cartel, is thought to have been active in the drug trafficking trade since the 1990s but managed to evade authorities till now by maintaining a relatively low profile.

Fausto Isidro "Chapo Isidro" Meza Flores was detained by Mexican special forces on Friday

Using US intelligence on his whereabouts, Mexican special forces descended on his location using "stealth and surprise", establishing a perimeter to prevent him from escaping and reinforcements arriving, Mexico's defence secretariat said.

However, they "came under attack from gunfire and explosives" before entering the property and arresting Meza Flores, the secretariat continued.

Videos circulating online appear to show helicopters firing streams of bullets towards the ground in Guasave. Others appear to show people at a baseball stadium fleeing as they hear shooting sounds.

Eight people in total were arrested, including Meza Flores' second-in-command, who is also his cousin, and his brother-in-law, who is the group's financial operator, the secretariat added.

Watch: Moment Mexican reporter takes cover on air as gunshots ring out nearby

Meza Flores, his cousin, and his brother-in-law were flown to Mexico City to face prosecution, the defence secretariat said. The other five remained in the state of Sinaloa.

Authorities also said they had seized multiple weapons during the operation, including a grenade launcher, as well as drugs and cash.

The US Consulate General in the city of Hermosillo said it was "monitoring reports of ongoing security operations, armed clashes, road blockades, and vehicle fires that occurred overnight in Ahome, GuamÃºchil, Guasave, and Navolato" following the arrest of Meza-Flores.â¯

It advised US citizens to remain alert for potential violence in the area and for residents to remain sheltered in place.

When Meza Flores was added to the FBI's most-wanted list in 2025, he was accused of "heinous crimes" including kidnapping, extortion and torture.

Then-acting FBI director Brian Driscoll said Meza Flores and his organisation had also unleashed a "scourge of illicit drugs" into the US via its southern border with Mexico.

He was initially charged with drug trafficking in the US in 2012 before being charged again in 2019.

The 44-year-old is alleged to have conspired to manufacture and distribute large quantities of cocaine, heroin, methamphetamine and marijuana between 2005 and 2019.

A number of vehicles were pictured burning on a highway near Los Mochis, Sinaloa, after Fausto Isidro "Chapo Isidro" Meza Flores was arrested

Meza Flores was originally a high-ranking member of the BeltrÃ¡n Leyva Organization, but a split between rival factions led to a surge of violence and prompted him to establish his own cartel by 2013.

His organisation has also engaged in what US officials described as an "extremely violent turf war" with the Sinaloa Cartel, one of the most powerful drug trafficking gangs, which operates in the same region.

Meza Flores was added to the FBI's most-wanted list after Mexican authorities reportedly linked his cartel to 20 million fentanyl pills they seized in 2024.

Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum has taken a tougher stance on the country's cartels in a bid to resist US intervention on Mexican soil. Mexico has welcomed closer collaboration but resisted US boots on the ground.

Trump made stemming the flow of drugs into the US across its southern border one of his key election pledges.

#49. Race to be next UN secretary general exposes divisions among world powers

Debate about dominance of permanent five members could reignite if major powers block Costa Rican frontrunner

The race to become the next UN secretary general could reach a decisive moment on Friday when a UN security council vote is likely to reveal whether the Costa Rican frontrunner for the post would be blocked by the big powers. If so, the setback will reignite a debate about the dominance of the permanent five (P5) members at the United Nations.

Rebeca Grynspan, an economist and UN insider who is from a family of Polish-Jewish wartime refugees to Costa Rica, has been in the lead in all but one of the eight straw polls of the 15 UN security council members since the first secret ballot was held on 30 July. Each member goes down the list of candidates, casting an “encourage”, “discourage” or “no opinion” vote for each candidate.

Despite countries not being required to reveal how they voted, it is thought Grynspan’s candidature has been resisted by Russia and the US, two of the five permanent security council members that can ultimately wield a veto against a candidate. Knowledge among the security council delegations of how countries are voting is down to private intelligence, leaks and a process of elimination.

The intensive lobbying over the job has spread across the globe, but Friday is especially important since the poll is slated to differentiate the voting of the P5 from the 10 elected members (E10) of the security council. To be recommended as secretary general to the UN general assembly, a candidate needs nine votes and no vetoes. In Friday’s colour-coded secret ballot, the P5 will cast red ballots and the E10 white.

One diplomat said: “The electoral system means it is important how many votes you win, but equally how many of certain votes you don’t lose.”

The protracted 140-day deadlock confirms the polarisation that has hampered the incumbent secretary general, António Guterres, but also belies claims the role is now an irrelevance.

Friday’s secret ballot may also reveal whether the United Arab Emirates’ campaign for its diplomat, Lana Nusseibeh, is about to blow up in its face due to the hostility to the UAE’s brand of active, commercially driven diplomacy, not just in Sudan, but also Somalia, Western Sahara, Libya and Yemen.

A late entrant, in the two straw polls in which she has contested so far, Nusseibeh secured only seven “encourage” votes, six “discourage” and two “no opinions”. This is despite prestigious big-power backing and her acknowledged personal diplomatic skills.

Nusseibeh has US and Russian backing. The French foreign ministry may have misgivings about the UAE, but Emmanuel Macron, the French president, travelled to the UAE in the midst of French riots to see the UAE president, Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan, and secure 100,000 metric tons of diesel a month.

In the UK, the foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, has kept his counsel, but he shares a Polish-Jewish refugee heritage with Grynspan and cannot be an admirer of the UAE’s human rights record or its evasion of EU sanctions on Russia. The prime minister, Andy Burnham, has UAE connections from his time as mayor of Manchester, and Nusseibeh is a fellow alumnus of the University of Cambridge. At the UN she worked closely with the UK in an informal group called “Friends of the Future of the UN”.

Yet 68 British MPs, including the Labour chair of the international development select committee, Sarah Champion, last week signed a letter calling on Burnham to veto Nusseibeh, describing her as “a tainted and compromised figure”.

Some of the opposition to her from elected security council members is not wholly political. Latin American countries believe an informal rotation system means it is their turn to lead the UN. As a result Panama and Colombia, currently on the security council, will oppose her. China has also been supportive of this rotation principle.

But others deeply object to what they consider to be the UAE’s dollar diplomacy and preparedness to use its extraordinary wealth to exacerbate and exploit pre-existing divisions in a country to gain a commercial foothold. Somalia, for instance, has a seat on the Security Council, and its president, Hassan Sheikh Mohamud, this week went on TV to accuse the UAE of buying ports, establishing military bases and supporting armed forces outside Somalia’s national security framework without federal approval.

It is also possible that Finland and Latvia, the two elected members with the greatest stake in Ukraine, are unimpressed that the UAE gave Vladimir Putin red carpet treatment when he visited Abu Dhabi in December 2023, nine months after the international criminal court had issued an arrest warrant for child trafficking. Greece, less engaged in Ukraine, may be more flexible.

Since the last poll, Nusseibeh has been to Bahrain, Britain and Pakistan, a clue as to where extra votes may be harvested. She also had a “thoughtful exchange” with Zeng Jixin, the Chinese ambassador to the UAE.

The two hurdles are whether someone so deep inside the UAE inner circle would have the courage in office to show true independence, and whether her active denials of UAE involvement in arming the RSF in Sudan leave her irretrievably tainted. From a Palestinian intellectual family, she insists she would make peace in Sudan her number one priority.

In the last ballot on 5 November, Grynspan won 12 votes in favour and three negative. But on 16 October – the only colour-coded ballot so far – which distinguishes between permanent and elected members – she received two discourage votes from permanent members, the equivalent of a veto. Nusseibeh had not joined the race at that point.

In an administration as personalised as the US, it has been hard for Grynspan to gain influence, with many sensing that Mike Waltz, the US ambassador to the UN, is the key influencer.

Grynspan has been reluctant to make the fact she is Jewish a feature of her candidacy, but the election of a Jewish person to run the UN might be seen as something the US could embrace as transformative. Her age – she is 70 – could be considered a drawback among those calling for a younger generation to renew the institution.

Although there are other Latin American candidates, Grynspan is seen as a more viable candidate than her rival, the Argentine Rafael Grossi, the head of the UN weapons inspectorate. Russia has opposed Grossi, objecting to his interventions over the Zaporizhzhia nuclear plant in Ukraine.

One way or the other, the logjam has to be broken soon.

#48. Miley Cyrus marries Maxx Morando in California ceremony

Miley Cyrus has married her partner of four years Maxx Morando during a ceremony in Palm Springs, California.

A string of black and white photos shared by the Flowers singer on Instagram showed the pair kissing while surrounded by white roses.

Her mother, Trisha Cyrus, and sister, Noah Cyrus, could be seen in the background of one image. Her father Billy Ray Cyrus was not in the picture but was at the ceremony with his partner, actress Liz Hurley.

Grammy-winning Cyrus, 33, wore a white silk dress designed by Anthony Vaccarello for Saint Laurent, while 28-year-old drummer Morando said "I do" in a black suit from the same fashion house.

In one of the photographs, which was the first in a series of images shared by the singer in an Instagram post, the newly weds posed next to a golden retriever.

The post was captioned with "M+M" followed by a white heart and the ceremony date, 21 November.

Miley's sister Brandi Cyrus, singer Doechii and fashion designer Donatella Versace were among those to congratulate the couple in the comments.

The ceremony reportedly took place at a resort formerly owned by Fly Me To The Moon singer Frank Sinatra.

Guests stuck to an all-black dress code, according to reports.

'Angel by my side' - Miley Cyrus pays tribute to godmother Dolly Parton

Miley Cyrus confirms engagement to musician Maxx Morando

The American singer confirmed her engagement to the 28-year-old drummer during an interview with US TV show Good Morning America last year.

She said Morando had got down on one knee for the proposal during a trip to Asia.

"I am not easy to surprise because I love to control every situation and I had completely surrendered and I'm telling you, I was so, so, so surprised," she said.

During an interview with Wonderland, Morando said he "had to be really sneaky" to keep his proposal plans a secret.

"We went to Tokyo first for a couple days, and then on to Kyoto. I think she had a sense that it was coming, so I was waiting until the very last moment of the very last day to throw her off the scent as much as I could," he said.

Cyrus, 33, had previously been married to Australian actor Liam Hemsworth but the couple separated in 2019.

The singer, whose hits include Party in the U.S.A and Wrecking Ball, married Hunger Games star Hemsworth in 2018 after the couple were in a relationship for a decade.

Speaking to British Vogue in 2023, Cyrus said she and 28-year-old Morando met after being "put on a blind date".

Morando is a drummer with LA-based band Liily. The band participated in NBC's Miley's New Year's Eve Party - hosted by Cyrus - in 2022, alongside musicians including Cyrus's late godmother Dolly Parton and David Byrne.

#47. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#42. ‘Weak and dizzy’: the secretive ICE force-feeding tactics that ended a Cuban father’s hunger strike

Exclusive: Luis Alejandro Cora Pérez says in messages to Guardian from inside Texas facility pain of force-feeding caused him to break strike

The text message that came from inside the Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) detention center was stark: “I have spent five days eating regular food. If I don’t eat it, they put the tube in me to feed me.”

A Cuban asylum seeker, Luis Alejandro Cora Pérez, 32, said in messages to the Guardian this month that he was still weak and dizzy after three months without eating, but that he had broken his hunger strike because being force-fed was just too painful.

The Trump administration responded to requests for comment by denying it had force-fed Cora Pérez. But the detainee sent more details – and his is far from an isolated case.

Cora Pérez was one of two men on hunger striker at a Texas facility for whom ICE sought and received a federal judge’s permission to force-feed in recent weeks, a Guardian investigation found.

Cora Pérez and the other man were locked up inside the Port Isabel service processing center, an ICE detention facility in south Texas, just a few miles from the US-Mexico border, on the coast near where Elon Musk’s SpaceX launches rockets. Cora Pérez began a hunger strike in August to protest against his prolonged detention by federal immigration officials while his case plays out. At first he only drank water, then after three weeks, he says he began to drink one Ensure nutritional shake per day.

On 30 October, court records show ICE received permission to forcibly feed and hydrate him, and to restrain him while doing so.

The next day he says six guards at the center held him down, while medical staffers inserted a feeding tube through his nose and down his throat, and pumped nutritional liquid into his stomach – a process he called “traumatizing, both physically and psychologically”.

On 3 November, ICE then received permission from a federal judge to force-feed another hunger striker at Port Isabel, from Uzbekistan, federal court records show. The DHS says the Uzbek man has not been subjected to the practice, however.

On Wednesday night, after weeks of communications, Cora Pérez was suddenly transferred to another detention center in Texas. His relative says neither of them were alerted to any plans to transfer him, or a reason for it.

The DHS did not specify why Cora Pérez was transferred, adding that ICE makes custody determinations on a “case-by-case” basis based on “various factors”.

The revelations come as ICE’s detention practices continue to come under fire amid allegations of mistreatment, and as an increasing number of sporadic hunger strikes have been breaking out in ICE facilities across the country.

The two men’s cases in Texas, revealed here for the first time, are some of the most recent instances of government lawyers seeking a federal judge’s permission, as required by ICE policy, and receiving the go-ahead to force-feed detained hunger strikers. The Guardian’s reporting is based on court records, interviews, dozens of messages exchanged and an interview conducted with Cora Pérez via a facility phone. It further reveals how the Trump administration is increasingly obtaining such court orders to force-feed detained immigrants, a secretive practice widely condemned as torture by human rights organizations and medical ethics groups.

Detainees driven to hunger strike are typically using it as a last resort to protest against poor conditions and the Trump administration’s preference for arrest and prolonged detention while their civil immigration cases proceed through the system.

Cora Pérez said detention center staff inserted the feeding tube and left it in his body, a not uncommon practice, according to experts, and introduced nutrition – first a “clear liquid”, followed by a “milky liquid”, he recalled – through the tube twice more that day, before he cracked and said he broke his hunger strike, citing what he called “horrific torture”. He began voluntarily drinking nutritional shakes the following day, he said.

“ICE does not hold back on anything, and they do anything they want, like we have seen, even killing their own US citizens,” he wrote to the Guardian, referring to the fatal shootings of Renee Good by an ICE officer and Alex Pretti by a border patrol officer in January of this year during Trump’s pivotal immigration crackdown on Minneapolis.

Detained hunger strikers subjected to involuntary medical procedures, including force-feeding, have rarely spoken out about their cases or revealed their identities, much less while still detained. Cora Pérez’s messages offer a rare glimpse at conditions inside a detention center’s restricted medical unit where hunger strikers and suicidal detainees are held.

“They just took [the Uzbek man] in an ambulance after [him] fainting in front of the medical lobby where they take our vitals, our weight and our temperature,” Cora Pérez said in an alarmed message last Monday morning.

The Uzbek man was returned the following day to the facility, Cora Pérez said.

“I see him every day because I see him in the room in front of me, but they don’t let us talk to each other,” he added.

The Guardian independently confirmed the Uzbek’s man identity but is not publicly naming him, because efforts to contact him have been unsuccessful. The man arrived at the Mexico-US border in April 2022 and was released into the country while navigating his immigration case. He was arrested by border patrol officials in April of this year and placed in detention. The man does not appear to have any criminal record and the DHS did not point to any criminal history.

In a statement, DHS said that after they received the court’s permission to force-feed the Uzbek man, he “has voluntarily taken in fluids and there has been NO force-feeding or forced hydration”.

Security operations at the sprawling Port Isabel facility in Los Fresnos, Texas, are run by a private prison company, Akima Global Services, while healthcare is primarily handled by government workers.

“ICE respects detainees’ rights to exercise self-expression and medical autonomy by refusing nourishment and medical care,” the DHS said in a statement. “ICE Health Services Corps may provide involuntary medical treatment only to prevent imminent life-threatening harm or death. Medical staff may provide treatment only to medically stabilize the patient, bringing the detainee outside the imminent harm, incapacitation, or death.”

The US attorney’s office in the southern district of Texas, which represented ICE in its force-feeding request, did not respond to multiple requests for comment. Virginia-headquartered Akima Global Services, and its parent company Nana Regional Corporation, based in Alaska, did not respond to a request for comment.

Force-feeding cases are shrouded in secrecy. After a detainee launches a hunger strike, ICE often sequesters them in medical isolation cells for monitoring, according to ICE’s detention standards. The DHS said that ICE continues to offer hunger strikers three meals per day.

After a certain period of not eating, which varies per case, ICE will request a federal judge’s permission to force-feed the hunger striker. Many court records, and sometimes entire court dockets, are sealed and hidden from public view. In Cora Pérez and the Uzbek man’s cases, like in others, they did not have any legal representation to challenge the force-feeding requests.

The Guardian found at least five instances in which ICE requested permission to force-feed hunger strikers in Port Isabel since January 2025. The federal judge who ruled on Cora Perez and the Uzbek man, Rolando Olvera, has similarly ordered in at least three other cases, records show. Olvera’s judicial assistant said the court had no comment for this article.

ICE is known to have requested permission to force-feed at least 21 detainees across the US since January 2025, the Guardian found, but the number is probably higher since an unknown number of records and dockets remain sealed, unsearchable and not feasible to locate. Most of the cases coinciding with the second Trump administration and tracked by the Guardian are in Texas and Florida, with others in Georgia, Arizona and Washington state. Under the Biden administration, ICE force-feeding requests were also made in Louisiana and California.

Amy Zeidan, an emergency medicine physician based in Atlanta and an expert on healthcare in immigration detention, said force-feeding hunger strikers can amount to “torture”, and lead to significant psychological distress. She also said it was a violation of medical ethics.

“Essentially it’s like inserting a straw into your nose,” Zeidan said. “You have a gag reflex, so most times people will gag, and that’s really uncomfortable. And people will even vomit repeatedly if it continues to irritate their gag reflex.”

Zeidan added that the forceful insertion of a feeding tube can lead to the swelling of the nostrils and the throat, and even tear them, leading to bleeding.

“It is like torture – somebody is doing a painful procedure to you that you don’t consent to,” Zeidan added. “Autonomy is a fundamental principle of medical ethics. We can’t do something to patients if they don’t want to, even if we think it’s good for them.”

There are numerous cells and rooms inside the Port Isabel medical unit, including isolation cells and shared rooms for ill detainees, according to Cora Pérez. In recent weeks, there were five people in the Port Isabel medical isolation cells used for hunger striking or suicidal detainees, he added. He described the cells as “practically freezing”, constantly surveilled with a security camera, and with just a bed, toilet and sink. As well as himself, he says he observed the Uzbek man, a Middle Eastern man, a “tall” eastern European man and a Black man of unknown nationality in the isolation cells. Unit detainees are escorted by facility staff to showers and sometimes receive yard and television access, he said.

Cora Pérez worries for the other detainees. The Black man, whose identity or nationality he does not know, seems to have “serious mental health problems”, Cora Pérez observed.

“He harms himself and spends the night yelling and hitting the door, and does not let anyone sleep,” he said.

The Guardian has independently confirmed the eastern European and Middle Eastern men’s identities, but is not publicly naming them because efforts to contact them have been unsuccessful. The DHS did not comment on the other men inside the medical isolation unit, saying they could not comment without the Guardian providing their identities.

Cora Pérez had left his son and parents in Cuba and requested asylum at the Mexico-US border in 2023, according to a relative who spoke to the Guardian on condition of anonymity due to fear of retaliation by immigration authorities. Cora Pérez went to live with the relative in Florida, where he worked in maintenance at a hotel and as a food delivery worker, he said.

In March 2025, Cora Pérez was arrested by local police and later convicted of two misdemeanors, including “breach of peace” and “obstructing an officer without violence”, according to local records. He was sentenced to two months in jail, his relative said. But ICE placed a hold on him and as he was being released in June 2025, immigration officials took him into custody – a common system – and soon transferred him to the Adelanto detention center, a privately-run facility in California.

Days in Adelanto turned to weeks, then months, then over a year, according to a federal court order denying one of his petitions requesting his release. His multiple requests to immigration and ordinary federal courts for release from detention, while he fights his asylum case, have been unsuccessful.

“All his claims will be heard by an immigration judge, and he will receive due process. This administration will not ignore the rule of law,” DHS said. “Criminal illegal aliens are not welcome in the US.”

In August, he stopped eating to protest against his protracted detention.

“I have spent two birthdays behind ICE’s bars,” he wrote. “The reasons I did the hunger strike in Adelanto are for my political asylum, for the time I have spent detained, [and] because I have a 14-year-old son who is starving to death in Cuba because he does not have my help.

“Also due to the mistreatment by the officials,” he added.

Cora Pérez did not tell his family about the hunger strike, so as not to further worry them. It was not until he was transferred to Port Isabel in late September that he confessed to his relative he had been on a prolonged hunger strike.

“He doesn’t share details so that I won’t get upset or worry,” the relative said in a phone interview.

Cora Pérez’s mother, who is in Cuba, “does not know anything, because she is sick, and with his detention she has gotten worse”, the relative added, between tears. “I don’t want her nor his grandmother to know anything about it,” the relative added.

On 30 October, the federal prosecutors requested permission for ICE to forcibly feed and hydrate Cora Pérez. Hours later, a federal judge approved the procedures without hearing from Cora Pérez. Cora Pérez told the Guardian in various instances he felt it was unfair he was not given the opportunity to present his case to the judge before the judge approved his force-feeding.

“It’s very disturbing because the judge is basically making a decision on information that is only provided by one side,” said Linus Chan, a professor of clinical law and the director of the detainee rights clinic at the University of Minnesota law school. “That just seems ridiculous.”

Cora Pérez was force-fed the following day, he said in his messages and in a phone call, adding that he did not physically resist because he “had seen a lot of mistreatment of others detained”.

“[ICE does] what they want with us, whatever they want, because a federal judge gave them permission,” he added. “I have suffered on the island [of Cuba] and now at the hands of ICE.”

After asking Cora Pérez about the DHS’s denials that he was force-fed, he repeated that he was. “Brother, ICE says and does whatever they want. They deny, punish, retaliate and do everything at their own will. They have even sent Cubans to Africa,” he said.

The number of people detained by ICE has soared under the Trump administration. And due to policy and legal changes, tens of thousands of people are forced to stay in detention for long periods while they fight their immigration cases.

Chan, from the University of Minnesota, says prolonged detention and delays in their cases lead to logical conclusion. “That’s exactly when people protest,” he said.

“And the only form of protest, for many of these people, is a hunger strike,” he said.

As Trump’s hardline immigration crackdown continues and people are detained as a first resort while their cases play out, Chan said: “Hunger strikes will become more common.”

#41. US Army to detonate tonnes of unstable explosives in Iowa despite court order

The US Army plans to move ahead with detonating five tonnes of degraded explosives in Iowa despite a ruling to halt it indefinitely, a new court filing shows.

"To protect its personnel and the surrounding communities, the Army needs to take swift action," the justice department notified the court on Saturday. "Protection of human health and the environment demand action now, not delay."

The court had ordered a pause after environmental groups sued to stop the detonation, arguing the army failed to consider safer alternatives.

The detonation was originally scheduled for Friday at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown. The army now plans to act by 24 November under an emergency exemption.

In the new filing, the Department of the Army argued that a controlled detonation was necessary to protect the public from an unplanned explosion, a risk it said would become more likely as temperatures were set to fall in the coming days.

"There is an immediate risk that the lead azide at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant will explode as temperatures are expected to drop below 30 degrees Fahrenheit (-1C) in the next several days," the filing states.

It argued that the army had removed the doors to the bunker where the barrels were stored in preparation for the detonation and that made the explosives more "vulnerable to temperature changes".

The government has not released details for the rescheduled detonation.

Earlier, the army had warned residents to leave their homes for six hours on the original scheduled day of the explosion, for the duration of the explosion and until the dust settled. An advisory warned "all humans" to avoid being outside in the affected areas while the demolition detonation was set to take place.

Lead azide is a highly volatile compound used as a detonator. Earlier this year, the IAAAP found that the material had degraded over time to the point where it was now too risky to move the material, according to Iowa Public Radio.

On Friday, the military said it was working with the Department of Health and Human Services and the EPA to ensure the explosion was done in the safest manner possible.

Military and environmental officials determined that detonating it in place was the safest way to eliminate the explosive safety hazard.

The EPA, which granted an emergency permit to detonate 68 drums of the material, said it did so because the explosion risk "presented an imminent and substantial threat to human health and the environment".

One of the Environmental groups suing to pause the detonation, the Iowa Environmental Council, argued that many Iowans living within the risk-zone were unaware that the explosion was scheduled to happen.

"There is no safe level of lead exposure, and Iowans were given just days to prepare for the November 20 detonation," the organisation said in a press statement.

The Iowa Environmental Council and Sierra Club, which filed the lawsuit on Thursday that prompted the court-ordered pause, filed a new motion on Sunday in response to the army's filing.

The group asks the court to pause proceedings so that it may submit additional information before the scheduled detonation.

The motion includes declarations from environmental experts and local officials who support pausing the detonation, citing a lack of public notice and questioning the Army's claim that delay would cause significant risks.

US army warns detonation of unstable explosives will create toxic plume

#37. Haitians excited but uneasy as El Salvador troops fight gangs

Foreign combatants in Port-au-Prince welcomed by many after years of gang control but some fear offensive could exacerbate volatile situation

A bullet-peppered mural of the Haitian striker Emmanuel Sanon covers the facade of Port-au-Prince’s main football ground. But more than two years have passed since any sports stars graced the pitch.

In 2024, as the surrounding city was almost entirely captured by heavily armed gangs, Sylvio Cator stadium – where football greats including Brazil’s Ronaldo and Roberto Carlos once played – also fell under criminal control, its dilapidated red and blue stands used as an outpost by fighters working for a gang boss called Izo. Haiti’s national team, the Grenadiers, found itself homeless and was forced to play its World Cup qualifying games abroad.

That changed last week, as Haitian police and troops from El Salvador reconquered the stadium. Nayib Bukele, El Salvador’s authoritarian president, called the development a step towards delivering Haiti from a criminal insurrection that has displaced more than 1.5 million people and killed thousands.

“We now have the upper hand on the ground,” claimed Bukele, who has sent more than 2,000 soldiers to Haiti since mid-October.

Outside the Sylvio Cator stadium, named after an acclaimed Haitian athlete who won a silver medal at the 1928 Olympics, there were joyous scenes as citizens celebrated a foreign politician they hope will prove an unlikely saviour.

“There’s a wind of hope blowing through our country and, thanks to God, we will get through this. Long live Bukele!” said a 55-year-old decorator who asked to be named as Bòs Baton.

Stood by Sanon’s battle-scarred fresco, Baton recalled being driven from an area called Poste-Marchand after criminals set his home ablaze during an armed uprising that started in February 2024. “Today, we are happy because there are places we couldn’t go to before that are accessible – and it’s thanks to Bukele’s intervention that we can be here today,” Baton said.

Identical sentiments have been voiced on the streets of El Salvador’s capital, San Salvador, in recent years as a result of Bukele’s controversial anti-gang crusade, which has freed millions from gang rule – albeit at a high price.

Since that crackdown began in early 2022, more than 90,000 Salvadorans have been jailed as part of a mass incarceration campaign that has slashed murder rates but also fuelled fears that El Salvador’s democracy has died.

Bukele’s domestic clampdown has made the millennial strongman a darling for rightwing politicians and voters across Latin America and the Caribbean, who hold his actions up as proof that, with an iron fist and a malleable approach to human rights, rampant criminality can be tamed.

Now, as Bukele seeks to extend his seven-year presidency in an election in February, he is hoping to burnish his gang-busting credentials overseas.

“It’s an operation that projects force, that projects Bukele’s image as the saviour … and positions El Salvador as a key reference in the region for [how] … to deal with gangs and security,” said Tiziano Breda, a Latin America regional expert from the Armed Conflict Location and Event Data project.

The extent of Bukele’s ambitions in Haiti is unclear. The operation’s trigger was the 18 October abduction of four Salvadoran aviation technicians who worked for a UN-backed security force. On Monday, Bukele’s vice-president, Félix Ulloa, announced Salvadoran troops would withdraw as soon as the four men were rescued, “because this is not an occupying force – we are not there to stay”.

But Ulloa also said his government was responding altruistically “to a cry for help from the Haitian people and government”, suggesting its mission could be extended.

Bukele has also hinted his plans go beyond short-lived ground operations to recover his citizens, or their remains. “We will triumph. God will give us victory,” the politician vowed this week.

Many Haitians hope his combatants will stay and have been thrilled by cinematic social media clips posted by Bukele showing helicopter strikes on a gang compound and the mansion of a gangster nicknamed Taliban.

Banners and billboards around Port-au-Prince celebrate Bukele as a 21st-century liberator and feature slogans such as “The bandits won’t get a wink of sleep!” and “We’re tired! Give us Bukele!”

Standing outside the liberated football ground, bricklayer Venel Saindor recalled how criminals torched his home in Carrefour-Feuilles three years ago. “It’s impossible to enter my neighbourhood because the thieves are still there. They shoot at us,” the 56-year-old explained, lifting his shirt to reveal a bullet scar above his right nipple.

“We need Bukele to help us return to our homes,” added Saindor, as a Salvadoran soldier with a belt-fed heavy machine gun kept watch from the stadium’s terraces.

Monique Clesca, a writer and activist, echoed the bricklayer’s enthusiasm. “Haitians are elated … [and] I feel elated also … It is a moment of hope,” she said on Tuesday, as thousands of demonstrators poured on to the streets to champion Bukele, chanting: “Viv Bukele!” and “Bukele is not playing games!”

As a sea of protesters swept towards the prime minister’s offices, people from all walks of life spoke of how armed groups had destroyed their lives and their collective hope that Bukele may provide a solution.

“I used to drive an old bus. The bandits burned it. They burned my house down too,” said Wilné Persona, a 50-year-old driver, adding: “Bukele is the one we are waiting for … because we can no longer bear the situation.”

A food vendor called Claudy Mayas urged “Papa Bukele” to stand his ground. “Bukele, I bless you, your wife and your children,” said Mayas, 51. “The Haitian people are with you, just as you are with us.”

Clesca attributed the palpable excitement to the widespread perception that Bukele’s “decisive” action had exposed the incompetence, inefficiency and indifference of Haitian and foreign leaders. “Basically, what Bukele has done is show that leadership is about resolving a problem for your population,” she said, praising how the stadium and other areas were recaptured in less than a month.

Amid the excitement there is also unease. Some fear Bukele’s offensive could exacerbate an already volatile situation in and around Haiti’s capital, much of which resembles a war zone. This week, there was heavy fighting between Salvadoran troops, police and gangs in Village of God, the shantytown that is Izo’s main base. On Thursday, police said officers had killed at least 34 suspected criminals during an operation near the stadium.

Some suspect El Salvador’s president, a social media whiz with 22 million followers on X and TikTok, could be exaggerating his troops’ role in recent successes with help from a strike team of online propagandists and fans. “Nayib Bukele is gang kryptonite,” one US enthusiast gushed this week, calling his intervention “a miracle”.

Others fret over Bukele’s ability to distinguish between gang members and civilians, given the indiscriminate nature of his mass detention campaign in El Salvador, where thousands of innocent people have been jailed without due process.

Clesca downplayed concerns over the toll Bukele’s crackdown has taken on democracy and the rule of law in El Salvador, which now has the world’s highest per capita incarceration rate. Haitian politics was so contaminated with organised crime, she argued, “it’s almost like the mafia has taken over 10 Downing Street”.

“If you’re talking about [an] assault on democracy, Haiti is … almost a case study that is … even worse, more flagrant, than El Salvador,” Clesca said.

The security situation was so dire that urgent action was needed. “Let’s just be honest … in Haiti, we’re being brutalised,” Clesca said. “Girls are being taken as sexual slaves. They’re being gang raped. So somewhere along the line, we have to say: ‘Haiti helped [South American independence hero] Simón Bolívar [who received shelter and support from Haiti in the early 1800s] … and now, Bukele is helping us’.”

At Tuesday’s rally, protesters spoke of El Salvador’s president in divine terms. “Today, we see Bukele as a saviour, as someone sent by God,” said one man, before adding a word of caution that spoke to countless past interventions that have ended in frustration and failure.

“There is one thing I will say to President Bukele,” he said. “He must not start with us and leave us halfway.”

#36. Democrats win largest House majority since 1992 after flipping North Carolina seat

Chuck Hubbard is a former journalist whose first political campaign was in 2022

The Democratic Party has won the largest majority in the US House of Representatives since 1992, after Democrat Chuck Hubbard unseated Republican Representative Virginia Foxx in North Carolina.

The race was called on Sunday, with Hubbard beating Foxx by 330 votes with 99% counted. It was one of the closest contests of the recent midterm elections, in which Democrats also secured the Senate.

The party has now won at least 258 House seats in the midterms compared to Republicans, who hold at least 173 seats, with four races still yet to be called.

Neither party had won as many House races since 1992, when Bill Clinton won the presidency and Democrats won a total of 258 seats.

Sunday's result came after Foxx, who has represented North Carolina's 5th Congressional District since 2005, requested a recount. This is allowed under state law when the margin of victory is under 1%.

She conceded on Sunday, saying in a statement: "After running a campaign focused on my track record of commonsense solutions and conservative values, I'm humbled and grateful that so many North Carolina voters responded by supporting my candidacy, even if we came up just a few votes short."

Virginia Foxx spent a decade in the North Carolina Senate before representing the state in the House of Representatives

Hubbard, meanwhile, wrote on X that he had spoken to Foxx, "who graciously conceded following the results of the recount process".

He added: "I deeply appreciate her commitment to a smooth transition of the office and constituent casework. We may not always agree, but we both love North Carolina and the people of the Fifth District."

Hubbard's win, which comes after he lost to Foxx in the 2024 midterms by nine percentage points, followed a number of Democratic victories in deeply Republican districts including South Carolina, Texas, Alaska and Iowa.

Hubbard, like many Democrats running in the midterms, focused on the rising cost of living in the US during the war with Iran - a top issue for voters.

President Donald Trump himself - as well as Vice President JD Vance - have suggested inflation caused by the war in Iran was probably to blame for the Republican losses.

Hubbard has also said he will also prioritise working families and small businesses, supporting public education and expanding access to affordable healthcare, according to his website.

The remaining House races yet to be called are in Tennessee and Minnesota, while two elections in Louisiana will go to a runoff in December. In Tennessee's fourth district, Democrat Victoria Broderick leads by fewer than 300 votes, and in Minnesota's first district, Democrat Jake Johnson is winning by fewer than 500 votes.

Democrats may use their upcoming majority in the House and control of the Senate to investigate conflicts of interest and other actions by the president and those close to him, while the Senate also has the power to confirm or block the president's cabinet and judicial nominations. They will also be able to pass legislation through Congress easily, though Trump still has to give final sign off for it to become law.

However, even with control of both chambers of Congress, they will still have limits on their power as Trump can veto legislation.
