#32. Alleged Mexican drug cartel boss ‘El Chapo Isidro’ arrested in special forces raid

Fausto Isidro Meza Flores was one of FBI’s most wanted fugitives and evaded capture for more than a decade

The Mexican military captured one of the FBI’s 10 most wanted fugitives during a special forces raid in the Mexican state of Sinaloa on Friday night.

Fausto Isidro Meza Flores, known as “El Chapo Isidro”, was arrested along with two other alleged leaders of his gang after a gun battle that triggered widespread panic in the city of Guasave.

The alleged leader of a major drug-trafficking cartel, the Meza Flores Transnational Criminal Organization (TCO), he had managed to evade arrest for more than a decade, even though there was a reward of up to $5m (£3.8m) on his head.

His FBI “most wanted” poster states his organisation “is allegedly responsible for the possession, distribution and importation of large quantities of heroin, methamphetamine, cocaine, fentanyl and marijuana into the United States, and has predominately controlled drug production, transportation and distribution in several locations throughout Mexico”.

Mexico’s defence ministry also alleged he is the leader of the Beltrán Leyva criminal organisation, which is allied to the Mayos, one of two rival factions in the powerful Sinaloa cartel.

Meza Flores’s alleged second-in-command, Germán N, whose alias is “Serafín”, and José N, an alleged financial operator for the Beltrán Leyva group, were also arrested.

The operation to capture the men was carried out by “elite military forces” with help from US intelligence, according to Mexico’s defence ministry.

After arriving in the “target area … using stealth and surprise”, the ministry said the arresting force came under attack “with gunfire and explosives” but managed to enter a house and make the arrests.

The men were then evacuated to Mexico City on a Mexican air force plane to be handed over to prosecutors.

Videos and photos show civilians fleeing gunfire at a baseball game at Kuroda Park stadium in Guasave, as well as burnt-out cars.

A barrage of rapid fire from what appeared to be Mexican security force helicopters hit the ground, according to CNN, and players from the Venados de Mazatlán and Algodoneros de Guasave teams abandoned the field mid-game.

Four Mexican army infantry soldiers died in the operation, the defence ministry said in a statement posted on X that extended the institution’s condolences to their families.

It added that security forces killed seven attackers and arrested eight alleged criminals in the operation, including three who were transferred to Mexico City and five who were turned over to the attorney general’s office in Culiacán, Sinaloa.

Overnight on Friday the ​military operation triggered a wave of violence in the state of Sinaloa, including armed ‌clashes between Mexican security forces and cartel members, who ‌set up flaming roadblocks in some areas.

By ​Saturday the violence had substantially subsided. Sinaloa state authorities ​said “the situation is under control and being addressed by all levels of government”.

Meza Flores was originally indicted in 2012, and in 2013 the US Treasury placed sanctions on him under the Kingpin Act, freezing his assets.

In 2017, the US Department of State’s Narcotics Rewards Program began offering up to $5m as a reward for information leading directly to his arrest or conviction.

He has been charged with multiple counts of drug-trafficking violations, and use and possession of a firearm in the US, and was wanted in Mexico for organised crime and offences against public health.

#30. Three killed and dozens missing after fire at Indonesian factory

Watch: Drone footage shows aftermath of deadly fire at Indonesian factory

At least three people have been killed after a fire at a confectionery factory in western Java on Saturday, with the death toll expected to rise.

Firefighters were still tackling some flames on Sunday, while rescuers searched for around 36 missing in parts of the damaged building where workers were believed to be trapped.

Indonesia's National Search and Rescue Agency said on Sunday 15 survivors had been located, and four others taken to hospital and a local clinic with undisclosed injuries.

Rescuers recovered 24 body bags from the fire as of Sunday evening, as search operations continue to locate missing people.

Banten Governor, Andra Soni, told media about 60 people were believed to be inside the factory when it caught fire on Saturday evening.

The factory is in Serang district, Banten province, about 50km (31km) west of Jakarta. Authorities have not yet determined the fire's cause.

Photos and footage of the scene showed the large factory taken over by flames during the night, firefighters battling the flames, and first responders retrieving at least one body from the wreckage on Sunday morning.

Local media reported that the search had been called off for the night late on Sunday, and would resume on Monday.

"All members of the joint SAR team are making every effort to locate the missing victims while prioritising the safety of personnel on the ground," the rescue agency said.

Relatives of the missing workers had gathered outside the factory waiting for updates, according to local broadcasts cited by AFP.

Firefighters had been called to the building at 16:30 local time (09:30 GMT) on Saturday - but it was already engulfed in flames, broadcaster Kompas TV reported, citing fire official Tubugas Machfudz.

"When we arrived (on location)... a large area was already on fire. So we could not determine whether the company's protection system was functioning properly or not," he said.

The factory is owned by PT Fonusa Agung Mulia, according to search and rescue authorities.

On LinkedIn the company describes itself as a subsidiary of UNIFAM, one of Indonesia's largest food and beverage companies.

#26. South Korea education minister resigns over exam cheating scandal

How South Korea tackles cheating on its college entrance exam

South Korea's Education Minister Choi Kyo-jin has resigned over an exam cheating scandal that shocked the nation.

He said he took full responsibility for the confusion caused by last-minute measures aimed at stopping AI-assisted cheating during Thursday's annual college entrance exam, known as Suneung.

A week ago, the top scorer in the national tax accountancy exam admitted using a mini-camera, mobile phone and AI tools. This prompted officials to act urgently to detect hidden devices, including sending emergency alerts to every single mobile phone in the country.

The Suneung takes up to nine hours to complete, and it plays a big role in career trajectories of millions of South Koreans.

South Korean high school pupils in Daegu check their answers, a day after the annual college entrance exam

"As the minister in charge, I believe I cannot escape criticism that we failed to prepare in advance," Choi was quoted as saying on Sunday by South Korea's Yonhap's news agency.

"Now that the exam has concluded, I feel a deep sense of responsibility and am offering my resignation," the minister added.

South Korean President Lee Jae Myung - who has been calling for an overhaul of the current exam system - later accepted Choi's resignation.

The education ministry earlier said 111 candidates had been found with unauthorised smart devices during this year's Suneung, which was taken by about 500,000 students.

Concerns over academic dishonesty during the fiercely competitive exam had been brewing in recent weeks, after reports of candidates cheating with the help of AI devices.

These fears came to a boil last weekend, when Han Won-seok, the top tax accountancy exam scorer in April, admitted to cheating.

His confession came amid speculation over his unusually high score, with just one out of 160 questions wrong.

"I used a microcamera to photograph the test questions and sent them to an AI, then checked my answers on my smartphone to commit the cheating," the 34-year-old said in a video message.

This prompted the authorities to introduce urgent measures, with just days ahead of the Suneung.

This included sending nationwide emergency alerts on the exam day.

Three alerts were sent between 08:00 and 15:00 local time on Thursday, causing phones across the country to blare out in unison - even if they were on silent mode, and even if they belonged to tourists using data roaming.

The phones did not ring out if they were switched off or on airplane mode.

It was the first time that South Korea used its national disaster alert system to catch exam cheaters.

Candidates also had to cover up the buttons on their shirts and jackets to block any hidden cameras, and authorities conducted inspections of eyewear.

All this made even honest test takers paranoid.

Ahead of the Suneung, one high school senior told the Korea Times he was worried his horn-rimmed spectacles would be mistaken for smart glasses.

Another told Yonhap he usually wore glasses but made an effort to put on contact lenses for the Suneung to avoid raising suspicions.

#24. Bank of England directors illegally profited from post-abolition enslavement in Cuba

New research finds that the British-led Cobre Mining Company helped industrialise Manchester via copper deposits and enslaved African people

Slave-trade wealth was embedded in Britain’s financial system, research finds

British businesses run by long-serving directors of the Bank of England profited from “brutal” enslavement in Cuba for decades after slavery’s abolition in the British empire, according to research.

Men and boys were enslaved in prison conditions at the El Cobre copper ore mine near Santiago de Cuba, which supplied British firms during the Industrial Revolution, the historian Chris Evans has found.

In the 1840s, Cuba was the most important foreign source of copper ore for the UK. Copper was critical to Manchester’s cotton industry, railways and telegraphy.

Previous research has revealed that dozens of financiers involved in the founding in 1694 of the Bank of England invested in enslavement.

Now, Evans’s research reveals that ties between key figures in the UK’s financial system and enslavement of African peoples continued even after the government outlawed it.

Britain abolished its trade in enslaved people in 1807 and passed legislation abolishing slavery in most of its empire in 1833.

However, as Britain’s Slavery Abolition Act was coming into force, “City of London figures” were developing mining of El Cobre’s copper ore deposits, an operation which depended on enslavement in Spanish-controlled Cuba.

Key players in the highly lucrative operation included the Bank of England directors Charles Pascoe Grenfell (1790-1867) and William Thompson (1792-1854), acting in a private capacity.

Enslaved copper miners at El Cobre were likely to have been trafficked to Cuba by illegal voyages from the Bight of Biafra, in modern-day Nigeria, as Evans details in his book, Slavery’s Long Goodbye – Capitalism, Nationalism and Christianity in the Age of British Emancipation.

The death rate at El Cobre means it is likely that thousands of Africans were exploited there. Their life stories remain largely undocumented. At the time, slavery was legal in Cuba but the importation of enslaved people was not.

As a partner in the Cobre mining and export company, Grenfell was a driving force in enslaved miners’ exploitation.

Cobre was “likely to be the largest slave enterprise in the western hemisphere” by the 1840s, Evans said. Investors who held Cobre shares from the outset received £102 in dividends for every £40 share between 1836 and 1865.

Grenfell was also a partner in a Swansea-based smelter Pascoe, Grenfell & Sons, which bought imports from the mine, in an era when the Welsh port was the centre of the global copper trade.

Meanwhile, Thompson, a former lord mayor of London, exploited enslaved miners through the ore export firm he directed, the Royal Santiago Company.

The UK is facing increased pressure to make a formal apology to Caribbean and African nations for Britain’s centuries-long perpetuation of transatlantic chattel slavery. Commonwealth leaders say “structured dialogue” with the UK prime minister, Andy Burnham, will begin in the next year.

While the Tory leader Kemi Badenoch has claimed Britain has nothing to apologise for, scholarship – dating back to the Trinidadian statesman Eric Williams’s 1944 book, Capitalism and Slavery – reveals the global impact of the colonialism and chattel enslavement that was integral to British capitalism’s success.

Evans, a former professor at the University of South Wales, said: “As Britain ostentatiously winds down its own slave system in the 1830s, British capital is boosting slavery elsewhere in the western hemisphere.

“The illegal trade, involving French and Spanish merchants, was flourishing and substantial. There were British (anti-slavery) patrols off the coast of Cuba, but they’re relatively few in number and intercept only a very small proportion of slave ships.”

Conditions at El Cobre were “extremely brutal”, with daily violence and torture. “Slave catchers” were employed to snatch miners who tried to escape to the mountains.

Starting with the Guardian’s own history, Guardian journalists explore the legacies of enslavement and reparative justice around the world

In 1817, under British pressure, Spanish authorities signed a treaty that banned trafficking enslaved Africans to Cuba. But as Cuba’s sugar and coffee plantations relied on enslaved African workers, local colonial authorities ignored it, with 25,000 captives arriving in 1859 alone.

In 1843, pressure from the British and Foreign Anti-Slavery Society prompted legislation intended to prohibit British subjects from owning enslaved people in other jurisdictions, after undercover investigations exposed British mining companies’ activities in Brazil and Cuba, including at El Cobre.

But after lobbying by mining interests, parliament diluted the legislation, allowing companies to retain enslaved workers as their “property” and hire others. The concessions prompted abolitionists to withdraw their support for the law, which failed to end the exploitation.

Neither Grenfell nor Thompson appears to have faced legal penalties for their involvement. Grenfell became the Liberal MP for Preston in the late 1840s, later boasting that his father had been an abolitionist. Grenfell remained a B of E director until 1864.

Enslavement at El Cobre finally ended after Cuban revolutionaries captured the town from Spanish forces in November 1868. The rebel commander proclaimed the miners’ emancipation on New Year’s Eve, and formerly enslaved workers later joined Cuba’s liberation army.

Evans’s book explores Britain’s overlapping histories of slavery, capitalism and missionary Christianity through the lives of Grenfell and two of his cousins.

Evans added: “The British in the early 19th century had started to understand slavery as a national sin.

“In the classical age of abolition, you could do things like abstain from eating sugar from the West Indies.

“But with copper, you can’t do that because it’s embodied in the industrial world, and, in the age of joint-stock capitalism, you have many British people who invest in mining companies who genuinely didn’t know these companies were using enslaved workers.

“Slavery was rendered invisible by the complexity of industrial society. But British engagement with slavery continued long after Emancipation. It involved the mercantile-financial elite of London as well as hundreds, possibly thousands, of rank-and-file investors from the Victorian middle class.”

A Bank of England spokesperson said: “We have previously acknowledged the role of former governors and directors in the slave trade.”

#22. Russia-bound ship towed away after it was attacked in Gulf of Finland

Finland says work is continuing on the Sparta II ship on Sunday

A Russian cargo vessel that was hit by a drone attack in the eastern Gulf of Finland is being towed away, authorities have said.

The Sparta II had been bound for Russia's Baltic exclave Kaliningrad when it was hit on Saturday morning, sparking a fire aboard.

It then drifted into Finnish waters and its crew were evacuated to Russia. Finnish defence forces said Ukraine was likely behind the attack. Kyiv has yet to comment.

Finland's Border Guard said on Sunday afternoon that fire was no longer visible from the outside but thermal cameras showed that some containers on the ship were still warm.

The Sparta II was "slightly listing", Finnish authorities added, but the "situation is stable" and no fuel or other "hazardous substance leaks" had been detected.

Kaliningrad Governor Alexei Besprozvannykh said two drones appeared to have been used in the strike and that there was "no threat" to his territory's supplies.

The Sparta II was sanctioned by the US in 2022 and Ukraine earlier this year, and is run by a Russian company, according to the Ukrainian and US governments.

The company says it was established within the structure of Russia's defence ministry in 2011 and since 2015 has been operating within the military construction complex.

Finland's coast guard said it had been alerted to the attack at around 08:50 local time (06:50 GMT) on Saturday and that the cargo vessel had been located approximately 50km west of the island of Suursaari in the Gulf of Finland.

This prompted "temporary aviation and maritime restrictions" around the area, Finnish border officials said.

"War is spilling over into our immediate vicinity," Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo said. "Ukraine has the right to fight back against the aggressor."

Shortly after the incident, Finland's defence ministry said a Russian helicopter was suspected of violating the country's airspace, potentially linked to "rescue or tracking operations".

While Besprozvannykh initially said the crew of the Sparta II intended to repair the ship and carry on their journey, the Finnish Border Guard later said they had been evacuated to another Russian vessel that was en route to Russia.

Jukka-Pekka Lumilahti, director of maritime operations at the Finnish Border Guard, said during a news conference on Sunday that one Sparta II crew member had suffered minor injuries.

The Russian rescue vessel Murman is leading towing operations with the support of a Finnish guard ship and a Estonian naval patrol vessel.

Kaliningrad is a Russian exclave that sits between Poland and Lithuania.

Finnish Defence Minister Antti HÃ¤kkÃ¤nen said that the Border Guard was responsible for the investigation into the attack and was co-operating with other relevant authorities.

Nato has also been informed, the defence ministry said.

The Baltic Sea has become a flashpoint in the Russia-Ukraine war, triggered when Russia launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022. Ukraine has recently ramped up its long-range attacks against Russia beyond the front lines.

Earlier this month, a Ukrainian drone strike hit a Russian warship in the Gulf of Finland.

#20. Fears grow that US will put Nobel-winning judge under sanctions after Myanmar genocide ruling

A ruling against Myanmar could set precedent for court’s judgment on Israel’s actions in Gaza

Fears are growing that the US could impose sanctions on judges at the international court of justice, including this year’s Nobel peace prize winner, Navi Pillay, if the court rules on Thursday that the Myanmar government committed genocide against the Rohingya.

The ICJ has never found a state responsible for committing genocide, and a significant factor in previous cases has been how to prove “intent” in a crime defined as acts committed with the “intent to destroy, in whole or in part” a national, ethnical, racial or religious group.

Lawyers representing Rohingya survivors say patterns of behaviour by Myanmar’s military, including killing people as they fled, show clear attempts to destroy the group during assaults on Rakhine state in 2016 and 2017.

It is the role of independent judges to interpret the genocide convention. However, judges at the court have taken advice on how to protect their assets from potential US sanctions, as a genocide ruling could be interpreted by Washington as lowering the “legal threshold” for the crime.

The Foundation for Defense of Democracies, an influential thinktank close to the White House, has predicted a genocide ruling will lead to the US imposing sanctions on the ICJ.

The US and Israel fear the final judgment may clarify how to establish a government’s intent to commit a genocide, and so set a precedent that makes it more likely the court will later rule that Israel committed genocide in Gaza.

Myanmar, which has denied carrying out a genocide, claims its actions targeted a military uprising by insurgents.

The ICJ, sometimes described as the UN’s top court, is responsible for settling interstate disputes, unlike the international criminal court (ICC), which prosecutes individuals accused of war crimes and other international crimes.

The US has already imposed crippling sanctions on judges at the ICC after the court issued arrest warrants against the Israeli prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu. The US has also announced sanctions against the entire ICC as an institution, which it described as a “rogue court”. Washington fears the ICC could go after US troops.

The Gambia, as a litigant, was entitled to appoint an ad hoc judge in the case against Myanmar and chose the South African lawyer Pillay, who in October was awarded the Nobel peace prize. She is required to act as a jurist and not an advocate for the Gambia.

Mike Waltz, the US ambassador to the UN, has sent a warning shot to Pillay and the 15 ICJ judges, saying Washington “will not allow globalist, faux judges to weaponise the law against our troops”.

Waltz asserted last month: “The ICJ ad hoc judge Navi Pillay is tipping the scales on the ICJ’s current proceedings on Myanmar, intending to lower the legal threshold for proving genocide so that weakened and false standard can later be weaponised against American and allied soldiers whenever they raise a finger against terrorists that hide behind civilians.”

In remarks addressed to Pillay, he said: “We see your agenda and we’ll be watching the ICJ’s handling of these proceedings very closely.”

In a speech last Friday, the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, said: “For this global elite, international law … is an instrument to advance their interests under the pretence of universal principles.”

Our US morning briefing breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

He warned the ICC against quoting laws to “men with swords”.

Israel has previously criticised adverse advisory opinions issued by the ICJ and refused to implement 2024 orders requiring Israel to admit more aid into Gaza, and to cooperate with Unrwa, the UN relief and works agency.

In the Myanmar case, a 24-page submission jointly filed by the UK and five other mainly European states, argued that the ICJ, in judging intent to commit genocide, should examine a range of “facts and circumstances” assessed “comprehensively and holistically”.

It also stressed that genocide was not solely defined by killings, and that the treatment of children, gender-based violence and forced displacement were also relevant.

The US until recently had taken a relatively more benign view of The Hague-based ICJ, and under Antony Blinken as US secretary of state determined that Myanmar had committed genocide.

The 15 ICJ judges are elected for nine-year terms by the UN security council and the UN general assembly.

Rubio has given the ICC until April to respond to his criticisms before he imposes sanctions.

On Friday, the ICC formally decided to drop plans to prosecute US soldiers and intelligence agents for potentially unlawful acts in Afghanistan. The shelving of the near-dormant inquiry came after it was agreed evidence would be hard to obtain, but one side-effect may be a greater willingness on the part of the US to abandon its plan to impose sanctions on the ICC.

#17. 'Danger seems greater all the time': Ukrainians search for missing after Russian strikes

Several bodies have been recovered from the site of a residential building that was hit by a Russian strike in the Kyiv region, Ukraine's State Emergency Services said.

The attack early on Sunday morning in the village of Dymer killed six people, Kyiv's regional head Tymur Tkachenko said. Six people were injured, while others are still missing.

Continued warnings of further drone threats near the site have hampered efforts, the service added, but some people were successfully evacuated earlier in the day.

"We are living a nightmare... only God knows when it will end," one woman told the BBC's Sarah Rainsford, who's reporting from Ukraine's Kyiv region.

#14. One in four women globally unable to refuse sex within marriage, says UN

Almost half of women in west and central Africa cannot withhold consent, with girls worst affected, UNFPA finds

One in four women around the world have no power of consent if their husband or partner demands sex, according to a UN report.

In west and central Africa, almost half of women (45%) are not able to say no in their own marriage, while a third of women (33%) in east and southern Africa and in Arab states face the same predicament. In Latin America and the Caribbean, one in 10 women are not able to refuse sex, said the report by the UN Population Fund (UNFPA) published on Thursday.

Younger women and girls aged 15 to 19 had even less power to say no, compared with older women aged 45 to 49, according to analysis in the report of eight countries with comparable data (Angola, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Kazakhstan, Malawi, Mali, Nigeria, Tajikistan and Zambia). The findings align with research showing adolescent girls endure very high rates of intimate partner violence.

“These are really sad figures,” said Jean-Paul Murunga, a lawyer with the global human rights organisation Equality Now. “Especially given that the continent of Africa as a whole has adopted the Maputo protocol, which sets [binding legal standards for] equality between women and men. We still have a lot of work to do to ensure women and girls benefit from these commitments.”

The Maputo protocol, a landmark treaty adopted by the African Union in 2003, guarantees women’s rights and obliges states to protect women from violence. It affirms that marriage must be based on full consent and guarantees women’s right to health.

“The underlying principle is unambiguous: sexual intercourse without free and voluntary consent constitutes sexual violence, including within marriage,” said Murunga. “Marriage does not create perpetual or implied consent. A woman retains the right to decide whether, when and how she engages in sexual activity”.

He said that as a first step rape laws across the continent needed to change. According to Murunga’s 2024 report, rape within marriage is not criminalised in some African countries. Seven countries expressly exempt husbands from prosecution for marital rape, including Côte d’Ivoire, Ethiopia and South Sudan. Some countries, such as Tanzania and Eritrea, only criminalise marital rape when the couple do not live together.

In countries where child marriage is permitted and marital rape is not criminalised, children are left unprotected. A legal loophole is created for what, outside marriage, would be classed as rape, and young wives have few options when it comes to protecting themselves from sexual violence and exploitation. In Gabon, for example, when a man has married a child he abducted, he can only be prosecuted after the marriage is annulled.

Underpinning these laws is the mistaken belief that marital rape does not exist as a concept because, by marrying, wives are assumed to have permanently consented to sex with their husbands. In many areas, religion plays a key role in affirming these beliefs.

Alimatu Dimonekene, founder of A Girl At A Time, an organisation supporting adolescent girls in Sierra Leone, said a conversation around consent in marriage had yet to begin in Africa. “We’ve not even started the conversation [around marital rape],” she said. “Women will never call it rape; they make excuses for it or even blame themselves.”

She added: “In west Africa, sex is seen as an obligation in marriage. You must do whatever your husband demands.

“Once he has paid your bride price [a traditional marriage custom in which the bride’s family are paid money, livestock or goods by the groom], he owns you and you cannot reject his demands. Whatever he wants, he gets, including in terms of intimacy, finances and raising the children.”

→ Baca artikel asli

#13. Explosion on Russian ship at Libya port caused by suspected drone attack, officials say

Russian ship engulfed in flames after explosion at Libyan port

An explosion on a Russian ship docked at Benghazi naval base was caused by a suspected drone attack, Libya's eastern government has said.

The explosion happened at dawn on Sunday, while the ship had stopped for fuel and supplies on its way back to Russia, Libya state media reported, citing the eastern government.

The statement said there were no casualties. Moscow has yet to comment.

Later on Sunday, the health ministry said it had received injured people from another vessel, a commercial ship, that was also targeted off the coast of Benghazi, state media reported. Their injuries were minor and their condition stable, the ministry said.

Videos posted on social media and geolocated by BBC Verify show the ship that was struck in the morning engulfed in flames and smoke. Satellite imagery from Planet Labs PBC also shows a boat on fire at 05:47 GMT at the Benghazi port.

Satellite imagery showed a ship on fire at the port

US-based intelligence firm Vantor collected high-resolution images that it said showed Project 20380 Steregushchiy-class corvette (Merkury) - a Russian navy ship - moored in the port on Saturday, and on Sunday showed most of the same ship capsized and sunk with only the forward bow still visible.

The eastern-based Government of National Stability (GNS) said the Russian ship - which it did not name or identify - was using the Benghazi port within the framework of an existing maritime agreement.

Sunday's incident is under investigation, and authorities are coordinating with their Russian counterparts to determine the source of the suspected attack and who is responsible for it, GNS added.

GNS condemned the attack on a ship inside a Libyan port, and called it a violation of maritime safety and an attack on their sovereignty. It said Libya was not party to the Russia-Ukraine war, or any other conflict beyond its borders.

Separately, Libya's internationally recognised government in Tripoli - the Government of National Unity (GNU) - warned against hosting foreign forces and allowing them to use facilities in a statement published by state media.

GNU said such activity raises "serious fears" about "risks that may result from involving ports and civilian facilities in activities related to external conflicts, and the resulting threat to the lives of citizens and the safety of national facilities and assets".

Libya fractured into violence and instability after its leader Muammar Gaddafi was overthrown in 2011, leading to the opposing governments.

Benghazi is more than 2,000 km (1,243 miles) away from the Russian capital Moscow.

Although it has not been officially confirmed who was behind the attack, there is little doubt, according to Jalel Harchaoui, Libya specialist at the Royal United Services Institute (RUSI).

"Ukraine is the only actor brazen enough to pull something like that off," he told the BBC. "If I were to bet my own money, I would bet that it was the Ukrainians."

If confirmed, it would not be the first time Ukrainian forces had carried out an operation of this sort. In March, Russia accused Ukraine of targeting the Russian tanker Arctic Metagaz in the Mediterranean between Libya and Malta with "uncrewed sea drones" launched from the Libyan coast.

As well as having an economic impact on Russia, hampering the ability of their so-called shadow fleet of tankers to export oil and gas, attacks like this are seen as humiliating to the Kremlin, according to Harchaoui.

But it is not only Moscow that will want to react to this latest incident. The ship was in the region as part of a deal with the government of eastern Libya - allied with Khalifa Haftar, commander of the Libyan National Army.

Harchaoui said Haftar will take the attack as a personal slight. "You're going to have a response by the Haftar family. They will ask questions about who is hosting the Ukrainians."

Additional reporting by Benedict Garman and Shayan Sardarizadeh

#9. Race to replant five South African baobabs after export blocked

The trees, intended for an unnamed buyer in Georgia, were reportedly dug up without the necessary permits

Environmentalists in South Africa are racing to replant five centuries-old baobabs after the 20-tonne trees were dug up by an exporter, who tried to ship them thousands of miles to Georgia.

The baobabs arrived at a reserve in Timbavati, in north-eastern South Africa, on Wednesday, where they will be replanted and supported by cables to help them recover. The trees had been stranded at a warehouse in Durban since July, when they were intercepted by authorities who said they had been moved without the necessary permissions.

The department of forestry, fisheries and the environment confirmed the trees had been destined for Georgia but did not name the buyer.

The case echoes an episode in Kenya four years ago, when eight baobabs were uprooted to be exported to Shekvetili Dendrological Park, owned by Bidzina Ivanishvili, an influential Georgian billionaire and the country’s former prime minister. Ivanishvili has filled the park with ancient, giant trees from Georgia, transporting them by boat along the Black Sea.

The export of the Kenyan baobabs was initially blocked after an outcry by environmentalists, who condemned their export as biopiracy, but later allowed to go ahead.

Otherwise, such exports are very rare, say experts. David Newton, programme office director for east and southern Africa for Traffic, an NGO that monitors the trade in wild animals and plants, said: “It is very unusual to see baobabs being used as ornamental plants … mainly because they are exceptionally difficult to dig out of the ground and transport, and then to successfully re-establish.”

Sarah Venter, founder of the Baobab Foundation, who has led efforts to replant the South African trees, said she believes they have a good chance of surviving if they are replanted quickly. The winter months of May to October, when the trees lose their leaves and are dormant, is a good time to move them.

“As soon as spring starts [in November], the trees use their water to flush their new leaves, and that’s when we need to get them into the ground as soon as possible,” she said.

The rescue mission has faced delays because of the challenges associated with moving such large trees, which are transported on special trucks capable of carrying abnormal loads. The five baobabs weigh between 19 and 29 tonnes, have a diameter of up to 4.5 metres (15ft) and a height of up to 12.5 metres.

Baobabs, known as the tree of life, are protected under South African law, which means a permit is required to cut or disturb them.

“A baobab isn’t just part of an ecosystem, it is an ecosystem in itself,” said Wambui Ippolito, a landscape designer, lecturer and horticulturist from Kenya. “These trees are biodiversity anchors.”

Animals ranging from bats and bush babies, to grey-headed parrots and the southern ground hornbill shelter in its cavities, while elephants, monkeys and warthogs feast on its fruit.

The trees have huge cultural importance, added Ippolito. “It has always been a sacred tree in Africa. It doesn’t matter where you go: Senegal, Mali, Ghana – wherever you go, if there is a community around a baobab tree, that tree is sacred,” she said. “Somebody’s great-great-great-great-great-grandparents sat under that tree, and it is part of human history in that area.”

Baobabs have shallow roots, which means they do not need to be planted very deeply, and these regrow fairly quickly, said Venter. Once replanted, the trees will be stabilised with cables and protected by electric fences to prevent damage from elephants.

The trees are thought to be between 200 and 300 years old. “Baobabs easily reach 1,000 years old, so they’ve got a few hundred years ahead of them if they survive,” said Venter.

A criminal case has been opened against the owner of a landscaping company allegedly responsible for the illegal harvesting and transportation of the trees.

Find more age of extinction coverage here, and follow the biodiversity reporters Rebecca Ratcliffe, Phoebe Weston and Patrick Greenfield in the Guardian app for more nature coverage.

#8. What's next after no deal at COP31 climate talks?

The COP31 climate summit in Turkey has officially come to an end after countries failed to come to a consensus on a new deal.

The European Union and some other countries rejected a fourth draft agreement over its failure to mention a need to phase out fossil fuels, the largest driver of global climate change.

An EU representative accuses some oil states of hijacking talks, as Russia defends opposing the inclusion of such a commitment and levels direct criticism at the EU in its speech.

The talks have been the longest-running in the summit's history, with negotiations breaking down two days after they had been due to end.

BBC climate and science reporter Esme Stallard reports from Antalya, in Turkey.

#6. California’s coastal communities on alert as biggest waves in years hit state

Hazard warnings issued throughout week after powerful swells and waves of more than 9 metres recorded

Multiple coastal hazard warnings were issued this week across California as successive areas of low pressure generated powerful swells and large breaking waves for coastal communities. Beaches and piers were closed last weekend as large waves crashed into California’s coastline. Offshore National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration buoys recorded wave heights of greater than 30ft (9 metres) last Friday. The Monterey, California, buoy recorded a wave height of 34.1ft, which would make it the highest reading in 30 years.

Coastal warnings extended from last weekend through this week, with alerts extending until Friday night. Waves of 10-18ft are expected, with local sets of 18-22ft for San Luis Obispo county beaches and Santa Barbara county central coast beaches. These Pacific storm systems are also bringing unsettled conditions across the western US, with heavy rain, mountain snow, and strong winds through the coming days. Already, record-breaking rainfall affected San Fransisco on Wednesday, with heavy rain also causing 32,000 customers to lose power in Sacramento. Into this weekend, a further area of low pressure will bring considerable amounts of moisture for southern Oregon and northern California. By Sunday night, more than 100mm (4in) of rain is expected in places, with further snowfall for areas such as Donner Pass across the northern Sierra Nevada mountains.

Nearly 72,000 people have been evacuated from areas of Odisha in eastern India as Tropical Cyclone Akvan approaches land. Akvan is expected to make landfall on Friday between Narsapur and Puri, prompting cyclone warnings from the India Meteorological Department for north Andhra Pradesh, Yanam and south Odisha coasts. Peak wind gusts are expected to reach 70mph, with rainfall totals through Friday and Saturday potentially exceeding 200mm for northern coastal Andhra Pradesh and Odisha.

After a yellow level 2 severe thunderstorm warning by the South African Weather Service on Tuesday, torrential rainfall and large hail were reported across parts of Johannesburg. The storms triggered flash flooding and caused infrastructure damage in the township of Soweto. One person died, while another remains missing after the severe storms.

#5. COP31 fails to reach major climate deal as talks expose deep divisions

Countries have failed to reach a major new deal to tackle climate change after deep divisions over fossil fuels and money emerged at the UN COP31 talks in Turkey.

Negotiations were due to finish on Friday but ran late into Sunday in search of common ground, becoming the longest UN climate conference in history.

But the final deal proved unacceptable to the EU, Pacific island nations and others because it failed to directly mention fossil fuels, the main cause of global warming.

Meanwhile, many poorer countries objected to the absence of any clear financial commitments from richer nations to help them adapt to the impacts of rising temperatures.

The last time a COP's main political agreement collapsed like this was in 2009 in Copenhagen.

UN secretary general Antonio Guterres, in his last COP before he steps down, said he was "deeply disappointed".

He took aim at what he called "powerful fossil fuel interests" who he said were taking "a page from Big Tobacco's playbook: distorting the science, delaying policy, blocking action, and piling up huge profits while people everywhere pay the price".

After two weeks of negotiations, exhausted delegates were presented with a new, draft deal in the early hours of Sunday morning to try to break the deadlock.

But the words "fossil fuels" did not appear anywhere in the text. The only indirect reference to coal, oil and gas was a nod to a deal agreed at COP28 in 2023, when countries agreed to "transition away" from fossil fuels in energy systems.

The EU's representative, Irish Minister Darragh O'Brien, made it clear that the EU "strongly" objected, saying that the "decisions go nowhere near the level of ambition needed to tackle climate change".

"It is unconscionable that we would have a climate pact that makes no mention of fossil fuels," he said.

Other countries, such as Panama and the Marshall Islands, also criticised the deal for doing nowhere near enough to tackle the pace of climate change.

A first draft of the deal on Monday mentioned "fossil fuel" or "fossil fuels" six times, but that was unacceptable to oil-producing nations.

Saudi Arabia's delegate Mohammad Ayoub said that Arab countries "continued to engage in good faith" but argued that they only accounted for a small proportion of historical greenhouse gas emissions.

Russia warned not to "turn to slogans" over coal, oil and gas, claiming that if countries moved away from fossil fuels too quickly it would lead to "fuel poverty".

Whatâs next after no deal at COP31 climate talks?

Another major sticking point has been money for poorer countries to adapt to the impacts of climate change, such as more intense heatwaves and more severe droughts.

Last year at COP30 in Brazil, nations agreed to triple the finance for that purpose.

But this year's deal contained no reference to concrete figures, nor the baseline against which that pledge would be measured. This raised fears it would give richer nations too much wriggle room.

"Rich nations and polluting governments cannot keep arriving empty-handed," said Mohamed Adow of Power Shift Africa, an African climate and energy think tank.

The UK's Energy Secretary, Miatta Fahnbulleh, described the failure to reach a political agreement as "immensely disappointing".

"This only strengthens our resolve to work with partners around the world on the genuine progress made in Antalya on tackling the climate crisis," she said, adding that the UK's commitment was "unwavering".

While talks were hosted in Antalya in Turkey, Australia led the official negotiations - creating what some observers described as a chaotic dynamic.

Chris Bowen, Australian government minister and COP31 president of negotiations, said that there were "unbridgeable red lines" in the discussions.

He pointed to "the most complicated and fraught geopolitical environment in living memory". At the same time, "the impacts of climate change are getting worse", he said, raising the stakes.

The year 2026 could end as the hottest on record and has brought extreme weather and natural hazards to much of the world, from summer heat and drought in Europe to flash flooding in Nepal.

And COP31 was the first annual UN conference to formally discuss how one of the key international targets for limiting climate change would be missed.

In the Paris talks of 2015, nearly 200 countries agreed to try to limit global warming to 1.5C above pre-industrial levels of the late 1800s, aimed at avoiding some of the worst effects of climate change.

The UN says that the Paris deal has driven "near-universal" climate action. In the early 2010s, global warming of 4C or more was thought to be a serious possibility by the end of the century.

Now, the world is likely to limit warming to about 2.8C by 2100 based on current government policies, the UN says.

But action has not been fast enough to meet that Paris goal, with the world likely to "overshoot" 1.5C of long-term warming in the next few years.

Many countries say they are committed to eventually bringing warming back down to 1.5C, but that will require an ambitious ramping up of technologies - which do not yet exist at scale - to remove carbon dioxide from the atmosphere.

Although the main deal fell through, some agreements were reached, including progress on a "just transition mechanism" - a framework to support workers and communities as economies shift towards clean energy.

The COP31 president also said that more than 80 countries backed Turkey's target to increase the share of global energy use met by electricity to 35% by 2035

But following today's events, some have questioned whether the UN climate process - which relies on consensus - can succeed in a geopolitically divided world.

The UN's climate chief, Simon Stiell, said he would seek advice on potential reform to the process, with the next conference scheduled for November 2027, COP32 in Ethiopia.

"[The consensus process] has worked to get us where we are," he told the BBC. "It got us through Paris... so it can work," but acknowledged it "fell short" today.

The UN climate process has "suffered setback before and come back stronger," Stiell told delegates. "It must do so again."

COP31 climate talks end with no deal after fossil fuels row derails hopes of agreement

#3. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.

The Manhattan district attorney’s office has been investigating Trump’s alleged role in a hush money payment scheme and cover-up involving adult film star Stormy Daniels that dates to the 2016 presidential campaign.

Trump’s attorney said he plans to file “substantial” legal challenges. The former president has repeatedly denied wrongdoing in the matter and accused Democrats of targeting him politically.

See more photos of the days leading up to Trump’s indictment:

A small group of supporters chanted as former President Donald Trump arrived at his namesake tower on Monday afternoon, with a handful of people waving banners with messages like “Trump won” and “Arrest Biden.”

While Trump couldn’t see his admirers as he entered a side door on 56th Street, his arrival came with more fanfare and security than at any other time in his post presidency.

Now that he is inside the building — where he launched his presidential bid nearly eight years ago — Trump is expected to meet with lawyers and political advisers on the eve of his arraignment, an aide told CNN.

Trump’s mood was described by an adviser as “defiant and focused,” as he watched expansive television coverage while flying back to his native New York City from Florida.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Witnesses are being questioned about what they saw in Trump’s private residential and work areas within the club, some of the sources said.

Former President Donald Trump has arrived at his midtown Manhattan property Trump Tower.

He will stay at Trump Tower tonight and is expected to depart New York immediately after Tuesday’s arraignment to head back to Florida — where he plans to deliver remarks at his Mar-a-lago home.

President Joe Biden told CNN’s Betsy Klein he isn’t worried about unrest due to former President Donald Trump’s criminal indictment in New York.

“No, I have faith in the New York Police Department,” Biden said on Monday during a tour of a power generation facility in Fridley, Minnesota.

Asked if he had faith in the legal system, Biden replied: “Yes.”

As former President Donald Trump walked down from his plane at La Guardia Airport on the eve of his arraignment, CNN’s Gloria Borger noted the unique situation he’s in.

Trump is “one of the most famous people in the world, and he is a former president, presidential candidate, and now he’s a defendant. We haven’t seen that. It’s hard to kind of process that,” Borger said to CNN’s Dana Bash.

Borger also highlighted how Trump’s day in court is something he’s avoided for decades and that it will be a grievance he uses to rile up his supporters.

“Donald Trump is somebody who’s probably tried to avoid his day in court for a very long time — decades. And now, you know, you have this man who is saying to people who support him, ‘You know I am your retribution.’ And he is going to make his grievance their grievance. And I think we’re going to see that play out over and over again, and we’ll see it play out in court with his attorney,” she said.

CNN’s Audie Cornish noted how Trump ending up in court seemed unavoidable given the “sheer number of investigations against him.”

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan said he’ll likely issue a decision tonight on whether media outlets can broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

CNN is part of the media coalition that asked the judge for permission to broadcast the hearing.

More on this: Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

The Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request and said that it was deferring to the judge to make a decision on how best to manage his courtroom.

Donald Trump — the first former president in history to face criminal charges — has arrived in New York for an expected arraignment on Tuesday after being indicted last week by a Manhattan grand jury.

The former president will stay at Trump Tower tonight, according to a source familiar with his plans. But even before Trump’s appearance, his presence will be felt in the Manhattan courthouse Tuesday, as all trials and most other court activity is being halted before he is slated to arrive.

Here is what we know about tomorrow’s events:

CNN’s John Miller, Jeremy Herb, Katelyn Polantz, Tierney Sneed, Sydney Kashiwagi, Kristen Holmes, Holmes Lybrand, Hannah Rabinowitz, Paula Reid, Alayna Treene, Gregory Clary and Devan Cole contributed reporting to this post.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

The former president touched down shortly after 3:25 p.m. ET.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, CNN has reported, but the indictment remains under seal. He is the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history.

He will spend Monday night at the Trump Tower, according to a source familiar with his plans.

The Secret Service is scheduled to accompany Trump in the early afternoon Tuesday to the district attorney’s office, which is in the same building as the courthouse, where he is expected to be arraigned.

The former president is expected to return to Florida shortly after his court appearance and has scheduled an event that evening to speak publicly.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

Former President Donald Trump has hired a new attorney to serve as lead counsel in his defense against charges brought by the Manhattan district attorney’s office, multiple sources tell CNN.

Trump has hired Todd Blanche, who was most recently a partner at law firm Cadwalader, Wickersham & Taft. Blanche has previously represented Trump campaign manager Paul Manafort and Igor Fruman, a former associate of Rudy Giuliani who was also a key figure in Trump’s first impeachment trial. Politico was first to report the news of Blanche’s hiring.

Trump attorney’s Joe Tacopina and Susan Necheles will remain on Trump’s legal team.

Blanche’s hiring has been seen by some inside Trump’s orbit as a sidelining of Tacopina, who up until this point has been one of the most forward-facing attorney’s dealing with Trump’s defense. However, a senior adviser to Trump pushed back on that characterization and said the addition of Blanche was intended to add more legal firepower to Trump’s defense.

When pressed by CNN’s Kaitlan Collins on Friday if Tacopina was the right lawyer in New York to take Trump to trial, another lawyer representing the former president, Tim Parlatore, pointed to a potential conflict of interest.

“I know that Joe has certain potential conflict issues, given his prior contacts, with Stormy Daniels. So, who’s the right attorney, to take it to trial, is something that the client will have to decide. Ultimately, the decision of who to stand next to, before a jury, is a decision that only the client can make,” Parlatore said.

Asked by CNN’s Paula Reid if he is still on the case and whether he will be in court tomorrow, Tacopina said: “Yes of course ! And I will be the lead trial lawyer when the bell rings!”

CNN’s Paula Reid contributed reporting to this post.

Senate Homeland Security Chairman Gary Peters said he has not seen any indication that protests in Washington, DC, over former President Donald Trump’s indictment will become dangerous.

“I think you’re always going to be vigilant, so folks will be vigilant. Security and local law enforcement are certainly monitoring it,” he told reporters Monday.

Peters, who is also a member of Senate Democrat leadership, said it is unclear what effect the indictment will have on the Senate. “We don’t know what’s gonna transpire tomorrow, let alone what’s going to happen in the few weeks and months. I think we’d have to take a look at what the indictment is all about.”

Former President Donald Trump has departed Florida and is en route to New York to face criminal charges.

His personalized Boeing 757 took off from the Palm Beach International Airport this afternoon at about 12:59 p.m. ET ahead of his Tuesday appearance in a Manhattan courtroom to be arraigned.

Trump, the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history, is expected to land at New York’s LaGuardia airport around 3 p.m. ET, according to a source familiar with his plans. He will stay at Trump Tower tonight, the source told CNN.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, according to two sources familiar with the case. A New York grand jury handed down the sealed indictment late last week. The charges have yet to be made public.

The former president is not expected to stay in New York City for very long. Immediately after his Tuesday court appearance, Trump plans to return to Florida, the source said, where he has scheduled an event to speak publicly.

Track Trump’s plane from Florida to New York here.

CNN’s Alta D. Spells and Jack Forrest contributed reporting to this post.

New York City Mayor Eric Adams warned GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene to “be on your best behavior” after the Republican firebrand said she will travel to the city to protest former President Donald Trump’s indictment.

Adams made the statement at a press conference as New York security officials detailed plans ahead of Trump’s arrival in Manhattan for his arraignment scheduled on Tuesday.

“While there may be some rabble rousers thinking about coming to our city tomorrow, our message is clear and simple: Control yourselves. New York City is our home, not a playground for your misplaced anger. We have the safest large city in America because we respect the rule of law in New York City. And although we have no specific threats, people like Marjorie Taylor Greene, who is known to spread misinformation and hate speech — she stated she is coming to town. While you’re in town, be on your best behavior,” Adams said.

“As always, we will not allow violence or vandalism of any kind. And if one is caught participating in any act of violence, they will be arrested and held accountable. No matter who you are,” he added.

#1. Russian warship sinks in Benghazi after suspected Ukrainian drone attack

No one has claimed responsibility for the strike on the vessel, which had docked on its way back to Russia

A Russian warship docked in the Libyan port city of Benghazi was struck in the early hours of Sunday in a suspected Ukrainian sea drone attack, causing a dramatic night-time explosion and the sinking of the vessel.

The strike severely damaged the Merkury, a Russian navy corvette that had stopped to refuel and take on supplies, according to Libyan officials from the country’s eastern government.

Initial reports said several Ukrainian sea drones were launched at the ship. At least four drones were intercepted and destroyed, while two hit the warship, triggering the explosion, the officials told the Associated Press.

Satellite imagery showed the Merkury had sunk after the attack, which took place in the Mediterranean, more than 1,000 miles from south Ukraine, marking a notable geographic expansion of the now near five-year war.

Though Ukraine began the war with no navy, it has developed a sea drone capability that threatened Russian warships in the Black Sea. The Russian fleet has been forced to retreat to the eastern port of Novorossiysk because Sevastopol in occupied Crimea had become unsafe.

Hours later, a Russia-linked cargo ship said to be carrying military supplies, the Ascalon, was hit by a sea drone off the coast of Benghazi. Video showed the drone heading directly towards one end of the vessel, again causing a huge explosion.

Libyan naval forces ‌rescued the vessel’s crew, the ‌sources added. The health ministry said some crew members were in stable condition with minor injuries. The number of injured and their nationalities are not known.

Benghazi is controlled by the self-styled Libyan National Army, led by Gen Khalifa Haftar, which controls eastern and southern Libya and has been aligned with Russia.

There was no immediate reaction from Russian authorities, and no immediate claim of responsibility for the strike, but it comes as Ukrainian forces have stepped up their long-range attacks against Russia far beyond the frontlines.

The ‌eastern-based Libyan authority said port and naval base facilities, including warehouses, were safe and that ​normal operations would resume once the fire was brought under control and the site secured.

It condemned the suspected attack as a violation of Libya’s sovereignty and ⁠a threat to maritime safety, saying it would take ​legal and ​diplomatic measures against ​anyone found responsible.

“The Libyan government will not ​allow Libyan ‌ports, territory or territorial ​waters ​to be turned into an arena for settling scores or exporting international conflicts,” it said.

The specialist website Naval News earlier identified the ship attacked as the Merkury, a Russian corvette – the smallest class of warship, smaller than a frigate – and said it had almost certainly been destroyed.

HI Sutton, a naval analyst, said the attack was probably conducted by sea drones, after a photograph of one emerged apparently taken off the Libyan coast. The drone, he said, “is also likely the type used in the subsequent attack”. Similar-looking drones were involved in the subsequent attack on the Ascalon cargo ship.

If it is confirmed that Ukraine has been able to blow up or seriously damage a Russian warship in Benghazi, it would represent one of its most successful naval strikes during the almost five-year war.

But it also represents a risky extension of the fighting far beyond eastern Europe, in an incident that could affect civilians on another continent and the working of the port.

A small unit of Ukrainian special forces operatives has been based at Misrata, western Libya, and is believed to have coordinated drone attacks on Russian tankers and transport ships in the past year.

Russian ships regularly make use of the port, which has a growing military importance to the Kremlin, to supply Russian mercenary forces in Africa and as a base in the Mediterranean since the country lost its bases in Syria.

Separately, a fire onboard a Russian cargo ship that was ⁠struck by drones in the ⁠Baltic Sea continued to smoulder ​on Sunday, Finland’s border guard said, with emergency services from Finland, Russia and Estonia working together to put out the blaze.

The Finnish military said the strike on the Sparta II ⁠on Saturday was probably carried out by Ukraine and that the vessel later drifted into Finnish waters, where it remained at anchor. The 16 crew members were evacuated on to a nearby Russian ship. One crew member was slightly ⁠injured.

On Sunday, visible flames had been put out, with operations focusing on cooling down the containers and stabilising the vessel, Finnish authorities said.

The ship, bound for the Russian exclave of Kaliningrad, had been damaged in international waters west of Russia’s Gogland Island, Finland’s defence forces said, corroborating an account by Russian officials. Kyiv has sent waves of military drones towards the Leningrad region on Russia’s north-west coast this year, including some that strayed into the airspace of nearby Baltic countries, leading to fears that the war was spilling on to Nato territory.

The risk of ⁠further attacks in the Gulf of Finland, a narrow arm of the Baltic Sea, could not be ruled ​out, Ivo Värk, the Estonian navy commander, told the public ​broadcaster ERR on Sunday.

Finland’s military said last ​week it had detected a strike on a Russian warship in the same area during Ukrainian drone attacks.
